Asymptotic theory of Schatten classes
Michael L. Juhos, Zakhar Kabluchko, Joscha Prochno

Abstract
The study of Schatten classes has a long tradition in geometric functional analysis and related fields. In this paper we study a variety of geometric and probabilistic aspects of finite-dimensional Schatten classes of not necessarily square matrices. Among the main results are the exact and asymptotic volume of the Schatten - ∞ \infty unit ball, the boundedness of its isotropy constant, a Poincaré – Maxwell – Borel lemma for the uniform distribution on the Schatten - ∞ \infty ball, and Sanov - type large deviations principles for the singular values of matrices sampled uniformly from the Schatten - p p unit ball for any 0 < p ≤ ∞ 0<p\leq\infty . Keywords. Isotropic constant, large deviations principle, Poincaré – Maxwell – Borel lemma, Schatten class, volume MSC. Primary: 46B06, 52A23, 60F05, 60F10 Secondary: 52A22, 60D05



1
Introduction

1.1
General overview

The Schatten classes (or Schatten
–
von Neumann classes) of compact operators are a classical object of study in functional analysis, and their finite
-
dimensional counterparts, that is, spaces of matrices endowed with the
\ell_{p}
-
norm of their singular values, have attracted much attention within the last few decades and many results have been produced in a number of disciplines, among them local theory of Banach spaces, random matrix theory, and (asymptotic) convex geometry.

The Schatten
-
p
class
S_{p}
, where
p\in(0,\infty]
, consists of all compact linear operators between two given Hilbert spaces whose sequence of singular values lies in the sequence space
\ell_{p}
, and thus it subsumes the special cases of nuclear or trace
-
class operators (
p=1
) and of Hilbert
–
Schmidt operators (
p=2
). It was introduced by Schatten in
[
48
, Chapter 6, pp. 71]
(though he said “completely continuous” for “compact”), having its roots in earlier works by himself
[
47
]
and with von Neumann
[
49
,
50
]
concerning nuclear operators on Hilbert spaces; those works were generalized by Ruston
[
44
]
to Banach spaces and by Grothendieck
[
16
]
to locally convex spaces.

From the point of view of the geometry of Banach spaces, Gordon and Lewis
[
15
]
obtained that the space
S_{p}
for
p\neq 2
does not have local unconditional structure and therefore does not admit an unconditional basis,
Tomczak
-
Jaegerman
[
55
]
showed that this space has Rademacher cotype
2
, and
Szarek and Tomczak
-
Jaegerman
[
54
]
provided bounds for the volume ratio of
S_{1}^{n}
.
Saint Raymond
[
46
]
computed the asymptotic volumes of real and complex Schatten balls up to a yet undetermined factor,
and König, Meyer, and Pajor
[
33
]
established boundedness of the isotropic constant for all
p\in[1,\infty)
.
Guédon and Paouris
[
17
]
proved concentration of mass properties,
Barthe and Cordero
-
Erausquin
[
5
]
studied variance estimates,
Chávez
-
Domínguez and Kuzarova
[
8
]
determined the Gelfand widths of certain identity mappings between finite
-
dimensional Schatten classes,
Radtke and Vritsiou
[
42
]
proved the thin
-
shell conjecture and
Vritsiou
[
57
]
the variance conjecture for the spectral norm, i.e.
p=\infty
;
Hinrichs, Prochno, and Vybíral
[
20
]
computed the entropy numbers for natural embeddings
S_{p}^{n}\hookrightarrow S_{q}^{n}
, and in
[
21
]
the same authors determined the corresponding Gelfand numbers;
Prochno and Strzelecki
[
40
]
improved the last stated results and complemented them by the approximation and Kolmogorov numbers.
In a series of papers, Kabluchko, Prochno, and Thäle
[
27
,
26
,
28
]
refined some of the previous works by giving the precise asymptotic volume of Schatten balls, they computed the volume ratio, considered the volume of intersections in the spirit of Schechtman and Schmuckenschläger
[
51
]
, and derived Sanov
-
type large deviations principles for the empirical measures of singular values of random matrices sampled from Schatten balls.
This last results stands in line with the study of large deviations for spectral measures of random matrices initiated by Ben Arous and Guionnet
[
7
]
, and Kaufmann and Thäle
[
29
]
have generalized the underlying distribution to a certain mixture of uniform distribution and cone measure on the sphere in the spirit of Barthe, Guédon, Mendelson, and Naor
[
6
]
.
The question of volume of intersections has also been taken up by Sonnleitner and Thäle
[
53
]
.
Very recently Dadoun, Fradelizi, Guédon, and Zitt
[
11
]
determined the asymptotics of the moments of inertia of Schatten balls, thereby also providing the limit of the isotropic constant, and confirmed the variance conjecture for
p>3
.

One of the main deficiencies of most results mentioned above is that they have only been obtained for Schatten classes of
square
matrices, some even more specially for Hermitian matrices, which is in a sense quite restrictive. The aim of the present article is to extend the scope of some of the results to Schatten classes of
m
-
by
-
n
matrices for arbitrary
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, in as far as that is possible or sensible, thereby complementing and generalizing the existing literature. As we shall see, this is far from being trivial in several situations.

Let us be more specific about the results obtained in this paper. First we provide the
exact
and asymptotic volume of the Schatten
-
\infty
unit ball of
m
-
by
-
n
matrices (Theorem
A
); the former is done via a Weyl
-
type integral transformation formula (similar to the one already used by Saint Raymond
[
46
]
) and evaluating the Selberg integral that appears.
Next we provide bounds for its isotropic constant (Theorem
B
), thereby adding to the work of König, Meyer, and Pajor
[
33
]
; notably, we are also able to give the precise limit as
n\to\infty
in the case when
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{m}{n}
exists. As the last major purely geometrical result, we clarify the relation between the Hausdorff and cone measures on the Schatten
-
p
unit sphere for arbitrary
p
(Theorem
C
); in the case of the classical
\ell_{p}
-
spheres this was done by Naor and Romik
[
38
, Lemma 2]
, and, interestingly, the formulas for the Radon
–
Nikodým derivatives turn out to be formally equal.

We then continue to explore asymptotic properties of the Schatten
-
\infty
balls via a probabilistic representation of the uniform distribution on the ball (Proposition
4.1
) in the spirit of Schechtman and Schmuckenschläger
[
51
]
and of Rachev and Rüschendorf
[
41
]
, who introduced those for the classical
\ell_{p}
-
balls, leading us to a Poincaré
–
Maxwell
–
Borel result (Theorem
D
) stating that, fixing the number
m
of rows and letting the number
n
of columns tend to infinity, the projection onto the first
k
columns converges to an
m
-
by
-
k
matrix with i.i.d. Gaussian components. Under the same premises we also prove a central limit theorem for the inner product of two points sampled independently from the unit ball or sphere (Theorem
E
).

Then we introduce an apparently new notion of symmetry of (probability) measures w.r.t. a given convex body, and apply this notion, together with two
-
sided unitary invariance, to Schatten classes. As the main result (Theorem
F
), we produce a probabilistic representation of those random matrices whose distribution follows this new symmetry w.r.t. Schatten
-
p
balls, thereby subsuming the representations for the singular values applied by Kabluchko, Prochno, and Thäle
[
27
,
26
,
28
]
in the non
-
Hermitian case.

Finally, we present Sanov
-
type large deviations principles for random matrices sampled from Schatten
-
p
balls for any
p\in(0,\infty]
(Theorems
G
and
H
). This extends the result for non
-
Hermitian random matrices given in
[
28
]
. It is important to note here that for non
-
square matrices the joint density of singular values contains an additional dimension
-
dependent nonconstant factor, which renders the adaption of the proof in places highly nontrivial.

1.2
Organization of the paper

Since the present paper contains a wide range of results it is structured according to the general themes of those results, and the (mostly short) proofs directly follow their propositions; the exception is Section
6
where the central results are collected at the beginning and the corresponding (extensive) proofs are deferred to subsections.

Section
2
lays out the mathematical setup including the norms and spaces, as well as frequently used auxiliary results.

Section
3
treats the elementary geometric properties of Schatten balls, including Theorems
A
,
B
, and
C
.

Section
4
contains the results pertaining to the weak limit theorems for the Schatten
-
\infty
ball, that is Proposition
4.1
and Theorems
D
and
E
.

Section
5
deals with
K
-
symmetric measures and concludes with Theorem
F
.

Finally Section
6
is dedicated to the Sanov
-
type large deviations principles for the empirical spectral measures stated in Theorems
G
and
H
.

2
Mathematical setup

2.1
Norms and spaces

Let
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
, and by
\beta\in\{1,2\}
denote the
\mathbb{R}
-
dimension of
\mathbb{K}
. (Most of the present results remain valid even for
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{H}
, the skew field of quaternions; then
\beta=4
.) The nonnegative and positive real numbers are written
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, resp.;
\mathsf{i}
is the imaginary unit, and
\Re(x)
,
\Im(x)
,
\overline{x}
denote the real part, imaginary part, and complex conjugate of
x
(applied entrywise for vectors and matrices). For the
d
-
dimensional Lebesgue measure we write
\vol_{d}
. For
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\leq n
consider
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
, the
\beta mn
-
dimensional real vector space of
(m\times n)
-
matrices. This is endowed with a Euclidean structure via the inner product (the
Hilbert
–
Schmidt
or
Frobenius inner product
)

\langle x,y\rangle:=\Re\Spur(xy^{*})=\Re\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{i,j}\overline{y_{i,j}}\biggr)\!,

where
\Spur
means the trace of a square matrix.
I_{m}
denotes the
(m\times m)
-
identity matrix. The collection of self
-
adjoint
(m\times m)
-
matrices is denoted by
\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
, and that of self
-
adjoint and positive
-
definite
(m\times m)
-
matrices by
\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
. On
\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
define the usual partial order (also called
Loewner
-
order
): for
a,b\in\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
,
a\leq b
iff
b-a
is positive
-
semidefinite, or equivalently
\xi^{*}a\xi\leq\xi^{*}b\xi
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{K}^{m}
. (Elements of
\mathbb{K}^{m}
are always considered column
-
vectors here.)

Recall that for any
x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
the
(m\times m)
-
matrix
xx^{*}
is symmetric and positive
-
semidefinite, and hence all the latter’s eigenvalues are nonnegative real numbers, whose nonnegative square
-
roots are called the
singular values
of
x
. Let
s(x):=(s_{i}(x))_{i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the vector of the singular values of
x
, arranged non
-
increasingly.

For
p\in(0,\infty]
call
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{p}
the
\ell_{p}
-
norm on
\mathbb{K}^{m}
(which is a proper quasinorm iff
p<1
), i.e., for
\xi=(\xi_{i})_{i\leq m}
,

\lVert\xi\rVert_{p}:=\begin{cases}\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lvert\xi_{i}\rvert^{p}\bigr)^{1/p}&\text{if }p<\infty,\\
\max\{\lvert\xi_{i}\rvert\,:\,i\leq m\}&\text{if }p=\infty;\end{cases}

the corresponding space is written
\ell_{p,\beta}^{m}:=(\mathbb{K}^{m},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{p})
, the unit ball is denoted by
\mathbb{B}_{p,\beta}^{m}
, the unit sphere by
\mathbb{S}_{p,\beta}^{m}
,
1
1

1

Our notation differs from the convention of writing
\mathbb{S}_{p}^{m-1}
, where in the real case
m-1
is the dimension indeed. But already in the comlex case this is contradictory because
\partial\mathbb{B}_{p,2}^{m}
has real dimension
2m-1
. Therefore we have opted to only notate the number of components.
and
\omega_{p,\beta}^{m}:=\vol_{\beta m}(\mathbb{B}_{p,\beta}^{m})
. On
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
define the
Schatten
-
p
-
norm

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{S_{p}}
by

\lVert x\rVert_{S_{p}}:=\lVert s(x)\rVert_{p}=\Spur((xx^{*})^{p/2})^{1/p}\quad\text{for $x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}$.}

In particular,
\lVert x\rVert_{S_{\infty}}=s_{1}(x)
is the
spectral norm
of
x
and
\lVert x\rVert_{S_{2}}=\lVert s(x)\rVert_{2}=\langle x,x\rangle^{1/2}
is the
Hilbert
–
Schmidt
or
Frobenius norm.
The space
S_{p}:=S_{p,\beta}^{m\times n}:=(\mathbb{K}^{m\times n},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{S_{p}})
is called a
(finite
-
dimensional) Schatten
-
p
class;
we denote its unit ball by
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
and its unit sphere by
\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}
; additionally define
\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}:=\vol_{\beta mn}(\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n})
.

Remark 2.1
.

The assumption
m\leq n
is no severe restriction: given arbitrary
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
, the Hermitian positive
-
semidefinite matrices
x^{*}x
and
xx^{*}
have the same positive eigenvalues with matching multiplicities and the rest are zero, hence singular values are defined also for “tall” matrices, and we have
(s_{i}(x^{*}))_{i\leq r}=(s_{i}(x))_{i\leq r}
and consequently
\lVert x^{*}\rVert_{S_{p}}=\lVert x\rVert_{S_{p}}
, where
r=\min\{m,n\}
. There are results in the literature that also take the zero singular values into account, e.g.
[
19
, Theorem 5.5.10]
.

2.2
Auxiliary notions and results

Recall that a matrix
u\in\mathbb{K}^{n\times m}
is called
semiunitary
(
semiorthogonal
if
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
) iff
u^{*}u=I_{m}
. The collection of semiunitary (semiorthogonal)
(n\times m)
-
matrices is called the
Stiefel
-
manifold
and notated by
\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}
(it is indeed a submanifold of
\mathbb{K}^{n\times m}
); in particular
\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}:=\mathrm{U}_{m,m;\beta}
denotes the set of unitary (orthogonal) matrices, they satisfy
u^{*}u=uu^{*}=I_{m}
.

For any
x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
there exist
u\in\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}
and
v\in\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
such that
x=v\diag(s(x))u^{*}
, the so
-
called
singular
-
value
-
decomposition
of
x
; by slight abuse of notation, here we mean
\diag(\xi):=(\xi_{i}\delta_{i,j})_{i\leq m,j\leq n}\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
for a vector
\mathbb{K}^{m}
, i.e., the matrix whose entries on the main diagonal are the components of
\xi
and are zero else.

The conventions for integration (which can be justified by exploiting that all domains of integration concerned are Riemannian submanifolds of some full matrix space) are as follows, where for
\beta=1
ignore all imaginary parts: on
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
the volume form is (up to sign, as are the subsequent)
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}(\mathrm{d}\Re(x_{i,j})\wedge\mathrm{d}\Im(x_{i,j}))
(which corresponds to
\beta mn
-
dimensional Lebesgue measure); on
\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
and
\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
it is
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\bigl(\bigwedge_{j=1}^{i}\mathrm{d}\Re(x_{i,j})\wedge\bigwedge_{j=1}^{i-1}\mathrm{d}\Im(x_{i,j})\bigr)
; and on
\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}
,
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}\bigl(\bigwedge_{i=j+1}^{n}\Re\bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\overline{x_{k,i}}\,\mathrm{d}x_{k,j}\bigr)\wedge\bigwedge_{i=j}^{n}\Im\bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\overline{x_{k,i}}\,\mathrm{d}x_{k,j}\bigr)\bigr)
(where
x_{i,j}
for
j\geq m+1
are such that for the augmented matrix,
(x_{i,j})_{i,j\leq n}\in\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}
); in all cases we write shortly
\mathrm{d}x
. Note that the induced measure on
\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}
is invariant under left and right multiplication with unitary matrices; in particular, on
\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
this gives a Haar measure.

For
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Re(z)>\frac{\beta(m-1)}{2}
define the multivariate gamma function
\Gamma_{m,\beta}
by

\Gamma_{m,\beta}(z)=\int_{\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}}\det(x)^{z-1-\beta(m-1)/2}\,\mathsf{e}^{-\Spur(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x=\pi^{\beta m(m-1)/4}\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}\Gamma\Bigl(z-\frac{\beta k}{2}\Bigr).

The Riemannian volume of
\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}
is known at least since Hurwitz
[
25
]
(note that actually Hurwitz considers
\{u\in\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}\,:\,\det(u)=1\}
, the special unitary group) and is a special case of the Riemannian volume of
\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}
which equals

\begin{split}\Omega_{n,m;\beta}&:=2^{\beta m(m-1)/4}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}}\mathrm{d}u\\
&=\frac{2^{m+\beta m(m-1)/4}\,\pi^{\beta(2mn-m(m-1))/4}}{\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\beta(n-k)}{2}\bigr)}=\frac{2^{m+\beta m(m-1)/4}\,\pi^{\beta mn/2}}{\Gamma_{m,\beta}\bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\bigr)}.\end{split}

(1)

In particular we set
\Omega_{n;\beta}:=\Omega_{n,n;\beta}
, the volume of
\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}
; then
\Omega_{n,m;\beta}=2^{-\beta m(n-m)/2}\frac{\Omega_{n;\beta}}{\Omega_{n-m;\beta}}
.

The
Selberg integral
(see
[
52
]
) will be of some importance to our results: let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Re(\alpha),\Re(\beta)>0
and
\Re(\gamma)>-\min\bigl\{\frac{1}{m},\frac{\Re(\alpha)}{m-1},\frac{\Re(\beta)}{m-1}\bigr\}
, then

\int_{(0,1)^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\bigl(x_{i}^{\alpha-1}\,(1-x_{i})^{\beta-1}\bigr)\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert x_{i}-x_{j}\rvert^{2\gamma}\,\mathrm{d}x=\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+k\gamma)\Gamma(\beta+k\gamma)\Gamma(\gamma+1+k\gamma)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta+(m-1)\gamma+k\gamma)\Gamma(\gamma+1)}.

Often we are going to need the asymptotics of the product of gamma
-
functions as they appear in
\Omega_{n,m;\beta}
and the Selberg integral. Here we also remind the reader of the
Landau symbols:
for sequences
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{K}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathbb{N}}
, we write
a_{n}=\BigO(b_{n})
to mean,
\lvert a_{n}\rvert\leq Cb_{n}
for eventually all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with some
C\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
; and
a_{n}=\smallO(b_{n})
to mean
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}=0
.

Lemma 2.2
.

For any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
we have

\begin{split}\log\biggl(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\Gamma\Bigl(\frac{\beta k}{2}\Bigr)\biggr)&=\frac{\beta n^{2}}{4}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)-\frac{3\beta n^{2}}{8}+\frac{(\beta-2)n}{4}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)\\
&\quad+\frac{n}{4}\log(4\pi^{2}\,\mathsf{e}^{2-\beta})+\frac{\beta^{2}-6\beta+4}{24\beta}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)+A_{\beta}+\BigO\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}\Bigr),\end{split}

where
A_{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}
is some constant.

Proof.

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. We notice
\log\bigl(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\Gamma(\frac{\beta n}{2})\bigr)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log(\Gamma(\frac{\beta k}{2}))
, hence we apply the Euler
–
Maclaurin summation formula to the function
f\colon\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}
,
x\mapsto\log(\Gamma(\frac{\beta x}{2}))
, that is,

\sum_{k=1}^{n}f(k)=\int_{1}^{n}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{f(n)+f(1)}{2}+\frac{f^{\prime}(n)-f^{\prime}(1)}{12}+\int_{1}^{n}H_{3}(x)f^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
H_{3}(x):=\frac{1}{6}B_{3}(x-\lfloor x\rfloor)
with the Bernoulli
-
polynomial
B_{3}(x)=x^{3}-\frac{3}{2}x^{2}+\frac{1}{2}x
.

We already know the asymptotics of the gamma
-
function through Stirling’s formula,

\log(\Gamma(x))=x\log(x)-x-\frac{1}{2}\log(x)+\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)+\frac{1}{12x}+R(x),

where
R
satisfies
R(x)=\BigO(x^{-3})
as
x\to\infty
. Therewith we get

\begin{split}\int_{1}^{n}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x&=\frac{\beta n^{2}}{4}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)-\frac{\beta n^{2}}{8}-\frac{\beta n^{2}}{4}-\frac{n}{2}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)+\frac{n}{2}\\
&\quad+\frac{n}{2}\log(2\pi)+\frac{1}{6\beta}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)+c+\int_{1}^{n}R(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\end{split}

where we already have gathered all constant terms. Now since
\lvert R(x)\rvert\leq Cx^{-3}
,
R
is integrable over
[1,\infty)
, hence the limit
c_{1}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl(c+\int_{1}^{n}R(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigr)
exists in
\mathbb{R}
, and

\biggl\lvert c_{1}-c-\int_{1}^{n}R(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\biggr\rvert=\biggl\lvert\int_{n}^{\infty}R(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\biggr\rvert\leq\frac{C}{2n^{2}};

this results in

\begin{split}\int_{1}^{n}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x&=\frac{\beta n^{2}}{2}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)-\frac{3\beta n^{2}}{8}-\frac{n}{2}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)\\
&\quad+\frac{n}{2}\log(2\pi\mathsf{e})+\frac{1}{6\beta}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)+c_{1}+\BigO\Bigl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\Bigr).\end{split}

Obviously

\frac{f(n)}{2}=\frac{\beta n}{4}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)-\frac{\beta n}{4}-\frac{1}{4}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)+c_{2}+\BigO\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}\Bigr).

We also have, via the polygamma
-
functions,

\frac{f^{\prime}(n)}{12}=\frac{\beta}{24}\,\psi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)=\frac{\beta}{24}\log\Bigl(\frac{\beta n}{2}\Bigr)+\BigO\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}\Bigr),

because of
\psi_{0}(x)=\log(x)-\frac{1}{2x}+\BigO(x^{-2})
. Lastly we have
f^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)=(\frac{\beta}{2})^{3}\psi_{2}(\frac{\beta x}{2})
; as
\psi_{2}(x)=\BigO(x^{-2})
and
H_{3}
is bounded, the function
H_{3}f^{\prime\prime\prime}
is integrable over
[1,\infty)
, thus the limit
c_{3}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{1}^{n}H_{3}(x)f^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
exists in
\mathbb{R}
, and the error satisfies

\biggl\lvert c_{3}-\int_{1}^{n}H_{3}(x)f^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\biggr\rvert=\biggl\lvert\int_{n}^{\infty}H_{3}(x)f^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\biggr\rvert\leq\frac{C}{n}

with some constant
C
(different from the previous occurrence). The statement of the lemma now follows by gathering terms and defining
A_{\beta}:=c_{1}+c_{3}+c_{3}
.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

For
\beta\in\{1,2\}
the statement of Lemma
2.2
could also be obtained by expressing the product in terms of the
Barnes
-
G
-
function
whose asymptotics are well
-
known. But this approach fails for general
\beta
, and even for
\beta=1
the calculations are tedious.

For the present investigations the following transformation formulae for integrals with respect to two important matrix factorizations turn out to be useful. (Others exist that we do not need.)

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\to\mathbb{K}
be measurable and either nonnegative or integrable.

1.

(Polar decomposition
[
9
, Lemma 2.1]
, also
[
58
, Proposition 2.27]
)
2
2

2

Note that
[
9
]
works with “tall” matrices, i.e.
m\geq n
, so the representation needs to be transposed; also the measure on
\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}
is normalized differently.

\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=2^{-m}\int_{\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}}f(r^{1/2}\,u^{*})\,\mathrm{d}u\,\det(r)^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}r.

2.

(Singular value decomposition
[
10
, Lemma 1.5.3, (i)]
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{\beta n(n-1)/4+\beta m(m-1)/4+\beta m/2}}{2^{\beta mn/2}\,m!\Omega_{1;\beta}^{m}\Omega_{n-m;\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(s)u^{*})\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle\mspace{230mu}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert s_{i}^{2}-s_{j}^{2}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}s.

In particular, if
f=F\circ s
, where
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{K}
(“
f
depends only on the singular values of
x
”), then
[
3
, Proposition 4.1.3]

\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}F(s(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{2^{\beta m/2}\,\Omega_{m;\beta}\Omega_{n;\beta}}{2^{\beta mn/2}\,m!\Omega_{1;\beta}^{m}\Omega_{n-m;\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}F(s)\prod_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert s_{i}^{2}-s_{j}^{2}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Remark 2.5
.

Some comments on Proposition
2.4
, part 2, seem in place. The form given in the present work does not completely match the one given in
[
10
]
. Apart from the same notational differences as mentioned in Footnote
2
, the major discrepancy is the domain of integration for the singular values: Chikuse assumes them to be ordered decreasingly, whereas we impose no such restriction. But this poses no serious obstacle, for the following argument: define
W^{m}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}\,:\,x_{1}>\dotsb>x_{m}\}
, then almost every
s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}
can be uniquely written
s=\tau s^{\prime}
, where
\tau\in\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
is a permutation matrix and
s^{\prime}\in W^{m}
; therewith
v\diag(s)u^{*}=(v\tau)\diag(s^{\prime})(u\diag(\tau,I_{n-m}))^{*}
; also write
h(s):=\prod_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{i<j}\lvert s_{i}^{2}-s_{j}^{2}\rvert^{\beta}
, then clearly
h(\tau s)=h(s)
. Now decompose
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}=\bigcup_{\tau\text{ perm.\ m.}}\tau W^{m}
, neglecting the set of vectors for which at least two coordinates are equal. Then for any permutation matrix
\tau
,

\int_{\tau W^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(s)u^{*})h(s)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s\\
\begin{aligned} &=\int_{W^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(\tau^{\mathsf{T}}s)u^{*})h(\tau^{\mathsf{T}}s)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\int_{W^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\tau^{\mathsf{T}}\diag(s)(u\diag(\tau^{\mathsf{T}},I_{n-m}))^{*})h(s)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\int_{W^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(s)u^{*})h(s)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s,\end{aligned}

where we also have used that on
\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}
and
\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
we are integrating with respect to the (scaled) Haar measures. This leads to

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(s)u^{*})h(s)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s\\
\begin{aligned} &=\sum_{\tau\text{ perm.\ m.}}\int_{\tau W^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(s)u^{*})h(s)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=m!\int_{W^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(s)u^{*})h(s)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{aligned}

Summing up, an additional factor
m!
has to be taken into account.

Lastly, for any set
A
we denote the (set
-
theoretic) indicator function by
\Ind_{A}
, that is,
\Ind_{A}(x)=1
if
x\in A
, and
\Ind_{A}(x)=0
else. Further notions and notations shall be introduced on the fly as needed.

3
Geometry of Schatten
-
p
balls

The first result is an explicit expression for the exact and asymptotic
\beta mn
-
dimensional Lebesgue volume of
\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
. In the case
m=n
this has already been accomplished in principle by Saint Raymond
[
46
, Théorème 1]
, though he does not evaluate the Selberg integral encountered. A statement concerning
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
for
p<\infty
is found in Proposition
6.10
.

Theorem A
.

The exact volume of
\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
is given by

\begin{split}\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}&=\frac{\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\beta k}{2})\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\beta k}{2})}{\prod_{k=0}^{m+n-1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\beta k}{2})}\,\pi^{\beta mn/2}\\
&=\frac{\Gamma_{m,\beta}(1+\frac{\beta(m-1)}{2})\,\Gamma_{n,\beta}(1+\frac{\beta(n-1)}{2})}{\Gamma_{m+n,\beta}(1+\frac{\beta(m+n-1)}{2})}\,\pi^{\beta mn}.\end{split}

Hence follow the asymptotics of the volume radius,

(\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})^{1/(\beta mn)}=\Bigl(\frac{2\pi\mathsf{e}^{3/2}}{\beta(m+n)}\Bigr)^{1/2}\Bigl(\frac{m}{m+n}\Bigr)^{(m+2/\beta-1)/(4n)}\Bigl(\frac{n}{m+n}\Bigr)^{(n+2/\beta-1)/(4m)}\bigl(1+r_{\beta}(m,n)\bigr),

where
r_{\beta}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies
C_{1}\leq mn\,r_{\beta}(m,n)+\frac{\beta^{2}-6\beta+4}{24\beta^{2}}\log\bigl(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}\bigr)\leq C_{2}
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta\in\{1,2\}
, with global constants
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

In particular, consider
m
as depending on
n
, then if
c:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{m}{n}\in[0,1]
exists, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}(\beta n)^{1/2}(\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})^{1/(\beta mn)}=\Bigl(\frac{2\pi\mathsf{e}^{3/2}}{1+c}\Bigr)^{1/2}(1+c)^{-1/(4c)}\Bigl(1+\frac{1}{c}\Bigr)^{-c/4},

where in the case
c=0
we interpret
(1+c)^{1/c}=\mathsf{e}
and
(1+\frac{1}{c})^{c}=1
.

Proof.

We apply Proposition
2.4
, part 2, to
f:=\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}=\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{\infty,1}^{m}}{}\circ s
and get

\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}=\frac{2^{\beta m/2}\,\Omega_{m;\beta}\Omega_{n;\beta}}{2^{\beta mn/2}\,m!\Omega_{1;\beta}^{m}\Omega_{n-m;\beta}}\int_{[0,1]^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert s_{i}^{2}-s_{j}^{2}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Via the transformation
s_{i}\mapsto s_{i}^{1/2}
the last integral is identified as Selberg’s integral whose exact value equals

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert s_{i}^{2}-s_{j}^{2}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=2^{-m}\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\beta(n-m+1)}{2}+\frac{\beta k}{2})\Gamma(1+\frac{\beta k}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2}+1+\frac{\beta k}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\beta n}{2}+1+\frac{\beta k}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2}+1)}.

Plugging in and rearranging terms yields the claimed result.

The asymptotic formulas are an application of Lemma
2.2
; in order to apply it, it helps to rearrange terms a bit via
\Gamma(x+1)=x\Gamma(x)
, like this,

\frac{\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\beta k}{2})\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\beta k}{2})}{\prod_{k=0}^{m+n-1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\beta k}{2})}=\frac{2}{\beta}\,\frac{\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n)\Gamma(\frac{\beta(m+n)}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\beta m}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta n}{2})\Gamma(m+n)}\,\frac{\prod_{k=1}^{m}\Gamma(\frac{\beta k}{2})\prod_{k=1}^{n}\Gamma(\frac{\beta k}{2})}{\prod_{k=1}^{m+n}\Gamma(\frac{\beta k}{2})}.

Now thence follows, after gathering terms,

\begin{split}(\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})^{1/(\beta mn)}&=\Bigl(\frac{2\pi\mathsf{e}^{3/2}}{\beta(m+n)}\Bigr)^{1/2}\Bigl(\frac{m}{m+n}\Bigr)^{(m+2/\beta-1)/(4n)}\Bigl(\frac{n}{m+n}\Bigr)^{(n+2/\beta-1)/(4m)}\\
&\quad\cdot\Bigl(\frac{mn}{m+n}\Bigr)^{(\beta^{2}-6\beta+4)/(24\beta^{2}mn)}\mathsf{e}^{(c_{\beta}+\BigO(1/m+1/n))/(mn)},\end{split}

where
c_{\beta}:=\frac{A_{\beta}}{\beta}+\frac{\beta^{2}-18\beta+4}{24\beta^{2}}\log\bigl(\frac{\beta}{2}\bigr)
. Note
\frac{m+n}{mn}=\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}\leq 2
because of
m,n\geq 1
; furthermore by the inequality of arithmetic and geometric means
\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}\geq 2(mn)^{-1/2}
and hence
(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{m})^{1/(mn)}\to 1
as
n\to\infty
. Therefore

\Bigl(\frac{mn}{m+n}\Bigr)^{(\beta^{2}-6\beta+4)/(24\beta^{2}mn)}\mathsf{e}^{(c_{\beta}+\BigO(1/m+1/n))/(mn)}\\
=1+\frac{1}{mn}\Bigl\{-\frac{\beta^{2}-6\beta+4}{24\beta^{2}}\log\Bigl(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}\Bigr)+c_{\beta}+\BigO\Bigl(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}\Bigr)\Bigr\}+R_{\beta}(m,n),

where
R_{\beta}(m,n)
is of the order of the square of the first
-
order term; from this it also follows that
mn\,R_{\beta}(m,n)
is bounded. Naming

r_{\beta}(m,n):=\frac{1}{mn}\Bigl\{-\frac{\beta^{2}-6\beta+4}{24\beta^{2}}\log\Bigl(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}\Bigr)+c_{\beta}+\BigO\Bigl(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}\Bigr)\Bigr\}+R_{\beta}(m,n),

we arrive at the claimed result.

Assume now that
c=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{m}{n}\in[0,1]
exists, then we define
c_{n}:=\frac{m}{n}\in(0,1)
and we can write
m=c_{n}n
. Using this yields

\Bigl(\frac{m}{m+n}\Bigr)^{(m+2/\beta-1)/n}\Bigl(\frac{n}{m+n}\Bigr)^{(n+2/\beta-1)/m}=\Bigl(1+\frac{1}{c_{n}}\Bigr)^{-c_{n}+(1-2/\beta)/n}(1+c_{n})^{-1/c_{n}+(1-2/\beta)/m}.

Now
\lim_{n\to\infty}(1+c_{n})^{-1/c_{n}}=(1+c)^{-1/c}
(
=\mathsf{e}^{-1}
if
c=0
); furthermore
\lim_{n\to\infty}(1+c_{n})^{(1-2/\beta)/m}=1
, which is clear if
c=0
, irrespective of whether
m
is bounded or not, and in the case
c>0
we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}m=\infty
and the result follows again; the limit
\lim_{n\to\infty}(1+\frac{1}{c_{n}})^{-c_{n}}=(1+\frac{1}{c})^{-c}
(
=1
if
c=0
) is unremarkable; and concerning the remaining term observe
c_{n}n=m\geq 1
, thus
1\leq 1+\frac{1}{c_{n}}\leq 1+n
; hence irrespective of the value of
\beta
,
(1+\frac{1}{c_{n}})^{(1-2/\beta)/n}
is sandwiched between
1
and
(1+n)^{(1-2/\beta)/n}
, either of which converges to
1
as
n\to\infty
, and therefore also

\Bigl(1+\frac{1}{c_{n}}\Bigr)^{(1-2/\beta)/n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}1.\qed

Remark 3.1
.

In the quadratic case (i.e.
m=n
) Saint Raymond has derived the following asymptotics
[
46
, Corollaire 4]
:

(\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{n\times n})^{1/(\beta n^{2})}\sim\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\mathsf{e}^{3/2}}{\beta n}},

where
a_{n}\sim b_{n}
means
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}=1
. It is easily seen that Theorem
A
reproduces his result when taking
c=1
.

Recall that a
convex body
is a subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
d\in\mathbb{N}
) that is compact, convex, and has nonempty interior. A convex body
K
is said to be
isotropic
iff
\vol_{d}(K)=1
, it is centred, i.e.,

\int_{K}x\,\mathrm{d}x=0,

and

\exists L\in[0,\infty)\forall\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\int_{K}\langle x,\theta\rangle^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=L^{2}\lVert\theta\rVert_{2}^{2}.

The last condition can be expressed equivalently as

\exists L\in[0,\infty)\forall i\in[1,d]\colon\int_{K}x_{i}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=L^{2}\quad\wedge\quad\forall i,j\in[1,d]\colon\biggl(i\neq j\Longrightarrow\int_{K}x_{i}x_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x=0\biggr).

For convex bodies in
\mathbb{C}^{d}
one would have to check the above conditions for the
2d
real components
\Re(x_{1}),\Im(x_{1}),\dotsc,\Re(x_{d}),\Im(x_{d})
. We provide below an equivalent characterization using directly the complex components.

Lemma 3.2
.

A centred convex body
K\subset\mathbb{C}^{d}
with unit volume is isotropic iff both of the following conditions are satisfied:

\exists L\in\mathbb{R}\forall i\in[1,d]\colon\int_{K}\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=L^{2}

(2)

and

\forall i,j\in[1,d],i\neq j\colon\int_{K}x_{i}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{K}x_{i}x_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{K}x_{i}\overline{x_{j}}\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

(3)

Proof.

Let
i,j\in[1,d]
with
i\neq j
, then using
\Re(x)=\frac{x+\overline{x}}{2}
and
\Im(x)=\frac{x-\overline{x}}{2\mathsf{i}}
we can rewrite,

\displaystyle\int_{K}\Re(x_{i})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{K}\frac{1}{4}(x_{i}^{2}+2x_{i}\overline{x_{i}}+\overline{x_{i}^{2}})\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}^{2})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{K}\frac{\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{2}}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

(4)

and similarly,

\displaystyle\int_{K}\Re(x_{i})\Re(x_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}x_{j})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}\overline{x_{j}})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

(5)

\displaystyle\int_{K}\Im(x_{i})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{K}\frac{1}{-4}(x_{i}^{2}-2x_{i}\overline{x_{i}}+\overline{x_{i}^{2}})\,\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}^{2})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{K}\frac{\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{2}}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

(6)

\displaystyle\int_{K}\Im(x_{i})\Im(x_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}x_{j})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}\overline{x_{j}})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

(7)

and without the restriction
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle\int_{K}\Re(x_{i})\Im(x_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{K}\frac{\Im(x_{i}x_{j})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{K}\frac{\Im(x_{i}\overline{x_{j}})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x.

(8)

\Rightarrow
: Let
K
be isotropic, then we know from (
4
) and (
6
), for some
L\in\mathbb{R}
,

\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}^{2})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{K}\frac{\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{2}}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=L^{2}=-\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}^{2})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{K}\frac{\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{2}}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x;

substract the two equations to get

\int_{K}\Re(x_{i}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x=0,

and add them to get

\int_{K}\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=2L^{2}.

In like manner we obtain from (
5
) and (
7
) that

\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}x_{j})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}\overline{x_{j}})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=0=-\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}x_{j})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{K}\frac{\Re(x_{i}\overline{x_{j}})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

hence

\int_{K}\Re(x_{i}x_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x=0=\int_{K}\Re(x_{i}\overline{x_{j}})\,\mathrm{d}x.

Swap
i
and
j
in Equation (
8
), then
\Im(x_{j}\overline{x_{i}})=\Im(\overline{x_{i}\overline{x_{j}}})=-\Im(x_{i}\overline{x_{j}})
, and therefore

\int_{K}\frac{\Im(x_{i}x_{j})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{K}\frac{\Im(x_{i}\overline{x_{j}})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=0=\int_{K}\frac{\Im(x_{i}x_{j})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{K}\frac{\Im(x_{i}\overline{x_{j}})}{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

and again from both subtracting and adding there follow

\int_{K}\Im(x_{i}x_{j})\,\mathrm{d}x=0=\int_{K}\Im(x_{i}\overline{x_{j}})\,\mathrm{d}x.

Putting all things together yields the claim.

\Leftarrow
: Separate the real and imaginary parts in Condition (
3
) (all are zero), and together with (
2
) substitute them in Equations (
4
)
–
(
8
) to get the desired isotropy.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

In relatively recent years interest in the study of complex convex bodies has grown, in the sense that the ambient space
\mathbb{C}^{d}
is considered over
\mathbb{C}
, not over
\mathbb{R}
; examples are the works
[
32
,
30
,
1
,
24
,
31
,
23
,
14
]
. This also leads to a different notion of isotropy; more specifically, a compact convex set
K\subset\mathbb{C}^{d}
is called
(complex) isotropic
iff it has unit volume, it is centred, and it possesses a constant
L\in[0,\infty)
such that,

\forall\theta\in\mathbb{C}^{d}\colon\int_{K}\lvert x^{*}\theta\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=L^{2}\lVert\theta\rVert_{2}^{2};

contrast this to
real isotropy
where the last condition reads,

\forall\theta\in\mathbb{C}^{d}\colon\int_{K}\Re(x^{*}\theta)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=L^{2}\lVert\theta\rVert_{2}^{2}.

These two notions are not equivalent, the class of complex isotropic bodies is strictly larger; see
[
34
, Theorem 2.1]
.

The authors stress that the present article considers
real
isotropy only.

Proposition 3.4
.

The volume
-
normalized ball,
(\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n})^{-1/(\beta mn)}\,\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
, is isotropic.

Proof.

Since
(\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n})^{-1/(\beta mn)}\,\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
differs from
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
only by a dilation, it suffices to consider the latter. Being a norm unit ball,
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
is centred; thence it remains to check the second moments, and we will exploit the unitary invariance of
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
throughout (inherited from the singular values). Let
(i,j)\in m\times n
, then let
v\in\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
swap the first and
i
th
row and
u\in\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}
swap the first and
j
th
column, effecting

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}x_{i,j}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{v\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}u}x_{1,1}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}x_{1,1}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

so the diagonal moments are equal to a common value.

Now let
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in m\times n
with
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
. If
i\neq i^{\prime}
, define
v\in\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
to multiply the
i^{\prime}
th
row by
-1
; therewith

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}x_{i,j}x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{v\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}x_{i,j}(-x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})\,\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}x_{i,j}x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}x,

which results in the integral being zero. If
i=i^{\prime}
, then
j\neq j^{\prime}
by assumption; and then multiply the
j^{\prime}
th
column by
-1
. Eventually all off
-
diagonal moments are zero, and in the case
\beta=1
this finishes the proof.

In the case
\beta=2
we apply Lemma
3.2
; performing the same steps as before with the integral
\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},2}^{m\times n}}x_{i,j}\overline{x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}x
we see, for
(i,j)=(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
,

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},2}^{m\times n}}\lvert x_{i,j}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},2}^{m\times n}}\lvert x_{1,1}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

and for
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
,

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},2}^{m\times n}}x_{i,j}\overline{x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

It remains to show
\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},2}^{m\times n}}x_{1,1}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=0
, but in order to do so let
v\in\mathrm{U}_{m;2}
multiply the first row by
\mathsf{i}
, therewith

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},2}^{m\times n}}x_{1,1}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{v^{*}\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},2}^{m\times n}}(\mathsf{i}x_{1,1})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},2}^{m\times n}}x_{1,1}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

which leads to the desired statement.
∎

For a convex body
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
which is isotropic up to dilation, the isotropy constant
L_{K}\in(0,\infty)
is defined via

\displaystyle L_{K}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d\vol_{d}(K)^{1+2/d}}\int_{K}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d\vol_{d}(K)^{2/d}}\,\frac{\int_{K}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{K}\mathrm{d}x}.

Theorem B
.

The isotropy constant
L_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}
remains bounded in
m
and
n
; to be precise,

L_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathsf{e}^{3/2}}\,c_{\beta}(m,n),

where we know

\mathsf{e}^{1/2}\Bigl(1+\frac{C_{1}}{mn}\Bigr)\leq c_{\beta}(m,n)\leq\mathsf{e}^{2}\Bigl(1+C_{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}\Bigr)\Bigr)

for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, with global constants
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

In particular, consider
m
as depending on
n
, then if
c:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{m}{n}\in[0,1]
exists, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}L_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathsf{e}^{3/2}}(1+c)^{1/(2c)}\Bigl(1+\frac{1}{c}\Bigr)^{c/2},

with the same interpretations for the case
c=0
as in Theorem
A
.

Proof.

Note that in our case
\langle x,x\rangle=\lVert s(x)\rVert_{2}^{2}
and
\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}=\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{\infty,1}^{m}}\circ s
, therefore

\frac{\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}\langle x,x\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{\infty,1}^{m}}(s)\lVert s\rVert_{2}^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{i<j}\lvert s_{i}^{2}-s_{j}^{2}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}s}{\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{\infty,1}^{m}}(s)\prod_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{i<j}\lvert s_{i}^{2}-s_{j}^{2}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}s};

but via the transformation
s_{i}\mapsto s_{i}^{1/2}
this quotient equals

\frac{\int_{[0,1]^{m}}\lVert s\rVert_{1}\prod_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{i<j}\lvert s_{i}-s_{j}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}s}{\int_{[0,1]^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{i<j}\lvert s_{i}-s_{j}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}s},

and after observing that the integrand of the enumerator is symmetric, we can apply a theorem of Aomoto’s
[
4
]
and obtain the exact value

\frac{\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}\langle x,x\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{mn}{m+n+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}.

Using this together with the asymptotic volume from Theorem
A
, we have

L_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}^{2}=\frac{m+n}{2\mathsf{e}^{3/2}\,\pi(m+n+\frac{2}{\beta}-1)}\Bigl(\frac{m+n}{m}\Bigr)^{(m+2/\beta-1)/(2n)}\Bigl(\frac{m+n}{n}\Bigr)^{(n+2/\beta-1)/(2m)}\bigl(1+r_{\beta}(m,n)\bigr)^{-2},

with
r_{\beta}(m,n)
as in Theorem
A
. In order to find suitable bounds, we consider

\begin{split}\log(c_{\beta}(m,n))&:=\log\Bigl(\frac{m+n}{m+n+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}\Bigr)+\frac{m+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}{2n}\log\Bigl(\frac{m+n}{m}\Bigr)\\
&\quad+\frac{n+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}{2m}\log\Bigl(\frac{m+n}{n}\Bigr)-2\log\bigl(1+r_{\beta}(m,n)\bigr).\end{split}

Recall the general estimates for the logarithm: for any
x>0
we have

1-\frac{1}{x}\leq\log(x)\leq x-1.

On the one hand, together with
\frac{c_{1}}{mn}\log(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n})\leq\log(1+r_{\beta}(m,n))\leq\frac{c_{2}}{mn}
with suitable global constants
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, this yields the lower bound

\displaystyle\log(c_{\beta}(m,n))

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{m+n+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}{m+n}+\frac{m+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}{2n}\Bigl(1-\frac{m}{m+n}\Bigr)+\frac{n+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}{2m}\Bigl(1-\frac{n}{m+n}\Bigr)-\frac{2c_{2}}{mn}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2c_{2}}{mn}.

On the other hand for an upper bound we get

\displaystyle\log(c_{\beta}(m,n))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m+n}{m+n+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}-1+\frac{m+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}{2n}\Bigl(\frac{m+n}{m}-1\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}{2m}\Bigl(\frac{m+n}{n}-1\Bigr)-\frac{2c_{1}}{mn}\log\Bigl(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{m+n}{m+n+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}+\frac{2-\beta}{2\beta m}+\frac{2-\beta}{2\beta n}-\frac{2c_{1}}{mn}\log\Bigl(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}\Bigr);

obviously
\frac{m+n}{m+n+\frac{2}{\beta}-1}\leq\frac{m+n}{m+n-1}\leq 2
, and it remains to show that
\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}
dominates
\frac{1}{mn}\log(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n})
when at least
n
grows indefinitely. But we have

\frac{\frac{1}{mn}\log(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n})}{\frac{1}{m}+\frac{1}{n}}=\frac{\log(m+n)}{m+n}-\frac{\log(mn)}{m+n};

clearly the first summand is bounded, and via the inequality of arithmetic and geometric means we know
0\leq\log(mn)\leq 2\log(m+n)-2\log(2)
and therefore also the second summand is bounded.

The value of
\lim_{n\to\infty}L_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}^{2}
in the case
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{m}{n}=c
is a direct consequence of the corresponding values of
\lim_{n\to\infty}(\beta n)^{1/2}(\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})^{1/(\beta mn)}
given in Theorem
A
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Although König, Meyer, Pajor
[
33
]
claim that their theorem includes the case
p=\infty
(that is, the spectral norm), their method of proof actually does not cover that, because the integral in question contains the exponential
\mathsf{e}^{-\lVert x\rVert_{p}^{p}}
which in the limit case equals just
\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{\infty,1}^{d}}(x)
, being an exponential no longer and rendering their integral transfrom moot.

Define a
star body
to be a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with zero in its interior which is star
-
shaped w.r.t. zero, i.e.,
tK\subset K
for all
t\in[0,1]
, and whose Minkowski
-
functional
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}
is continuous. Any convex body with zero in its interior is a star body. For such a star body
K
define the associated
(normalized) cone measure

\kappa=\kappa_{K}
on its boundary
\partial K
by

\kappa(A):=\frac{\vol_{d}\bigl(\{tx\,:\,t\in[0,1],x\in A\}\bigr)}{\vol_{d}(K)}

for any measurable
A\subset\partial K
; it is the unique measure on
\partial K
which satisfies the following polar integration formula (see
[
38
, Proposition 1]
): for any
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
measurable and nonnegative,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=d\vol_{d}(K)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\partial K}f(r\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa(\theta)\,r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}r.

(9)

A useful identity for evaluating integrals w.r.t. the cone measure can be obtained by integrating
x\mapsto f(\frac{x}{\lvert x\rvert_{K}})
over
K
, to wit,

\int_{\partial K}f(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa(\theta)=\frac{1}{\vol_{d}(K)}\int_{K}f\Bigl(\frac{x}{\lvert x\rvert_{K}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x.

(10)

The surface measure on
\partial K
is defined to be the
(d-1)
-
dimensional Hausdorff measure restricted to
\partial K
. The two measures are related according to the following statement.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\nu(x)
be the outer unit normal vector in
x\in\partial K
, then for
\Hausd^{d-1}
-
almost every
x\in\partial K
,

\mathrm{d}\!\Hausd^{d-1}(x)=\frac{d\vol_{d}(K)}{\langle\nu(x),x\rangle}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa(x).

Proof.

This is a rewriting of Naor and Romik
[
38
, Lemma 1]
. Adapting their notation, note that
\Hausd^{d-1}=\operatorname{area}(\partial K)\sigma_{K}
; it remains to treat
\lVert\nabla\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}(x)\rVert_{2}
. But in their proof they derive
\langle\nabla\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}(x),x\rangle=1
, and therewith

\lVert\nabla\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}(x)\rVert_{2}=\frac{\lVert\nabla\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}(x)\rVert_{2}}{\langle\nabla\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}(x),x\rangle}=\frac{1}{\bigl\langle\frac{\nabla\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}(x)}{\lVert\nabla\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}(x)\rVert_{2}},x\bigr\rangle},

from which the claim follows because of
\nu(x)=\frac{\nabla\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}(x)}{\lVert\nabla\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}(x)\rVert_{2}}
.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

Rewritten as
\vol_{d}(K)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa(x)=\frac{1}{d}\langle\nu(x),x\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\!\Hausd^{d-1}(x)
, the formula has a nice elementary interpretation: fix a point
x\in\partial K
, then this determines an “infinitesimal cone” with apex
o
and an “infinitesimal base” tangent to
\partial K
at
x
; the height of this cone equals
\lVert\langle\nu(x),x\rangle\nu(x)\rVert_{2}=\langle\nu(x),x\rangle
(the length of the orthogonal projection of
x
onto
\mathbb{R}\nu(x)
), the base measures
\mathrm{d}\!\Hausd^{d-1}(x)
, and therefore the cone has volume
\frac{1}{d}\langle\nu(x),x\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\!\Hausd^{d-1}(x)
. But by the very definition of the cone measure this also equals
\vol_{d}(K)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa(x)
.

As a consequence of Lemma
3.6
, if
x\mapsto\langle\nu(x),x\rangle
is constant on
\partial K
, then the normalized measures coincide. This is known to be the case for
\mathbb{S}_{p,\beta}^{m}
iff
p\in\{1,2,\infty\}
, going back to
[
41
, p. 1314]
. Let us fix the notation
\kappa_{p,\beta}^{m}:=\kappa_{\mathbb{B}_{p,\beta}^{m}}
and
\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}:=\kappa_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{R}
, then the trace power map

\tau_{p}\colon\left\{\begin{aligned} \text{Pos}_{m,\beta}&\to\mathbb{R}\\
x&\mapsto\Spur(x^{p})\end{aligned}\right.

is continuously differentiable and for any
x\in\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
,

\nabla\tau_{p}(x)=px^{p-1}.

Proof.

Case
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
:
Continuous differentiability is obvious, as is the gradient, for
p\in\{0,1\}
. So let
p\geq 2
, then the map
f\colon\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}\to\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
,
x\mapsto x^{p}
, is continuously differentiable with derivative
\mathrm{d}f(x)(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}x^{k-1}hx^{p-k}
for any
x\in\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
and
h\in\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
. This implies, because of
\tau_{p}=\Spur\circ f
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\tau_{p}(x)(h)

\displaystyle=\Spur(\mathrm{d}f(x)(h))=\Spur\biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{p}x^{k-1}hx^{p-k}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\Spur(x^{k-1}hx^{p-k})=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\Spur(x^{p-1}h)

\displaystyle=p\Spur(x^{p-1}h)=\langle px^{p-1},h\rangle.

(Note
\Spur(ab)\in\mathbb{R}
for any
a,b\in\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
.)

Case
p
arbitrary:
Write
x^{p}=\mathsf{e}^{p\log(x)}
, then this already suggests continuous differentiability, and expanding the exponential we have

\tau_{p}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{p^{k}}{k!}\Spur(\log(x)^{k}).

Call
f_{k}(x):=\Spur(\log(x)^{k})
, then we have to determine
\nabla f_{k}(x)
and show that
\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{p^{k}}{k!}\nabla f_{k}
converges locally uniformly on
\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
; this will yield
\nabla\tau_{p}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{p^{k}}{k!}\nabla f_{k}
.

From the case of integer powers and the chain rule we know
\mathrm{d}f_{k}(x)(h)=\langle k\log(x)^{k-1},\mathrm{d}\log(x)(h)\rangle
for any
x\in\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
and
h\in\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
. In order to determine
\mathrm{d}\log(x)(h)
consider the exponential map again, that is,
\mathsf{e}^{x}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}x^{k}
. Call
g_{k}(x):=x^{k}
, then each
g_{k}
is differentiable with derivative
\mathrm{d}g_{k}(x)(h)=\sum_{l=1}^{k}x^{l-1}hx^{k-l}
; therefore
\lVert\mathrm{d}g_{k}(x)(h)\rVert_{S_{2}}\leq k\lVert x\rVert_{S_{2}}^{k-1}\lVert h\rVert_{S_{2}}
(any submultiplicative norm would do) and for the operator norm we get
\lVert\mathrm{d}g_{k}(x)\rVert\leq k\lVert x\rVert_{S_{2}}^{k-1}
. Now given any
R\in(0,\infty)
we find

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\lVert\mathrm{d}g_{k}(x)\rVert\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\,k\lVert x\rVert_{S_{2}}^{k-1}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}R^{k}=\mathsf{e}^{R}<\infty

for all
x\in\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
with
\lVert x\rVert_{S_{2}}\leq R
; thus
\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}\mathrm{d}g_{k}
is locally uniformly convergent on
\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
and hence
\mathrm{d}\exp=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\mathrm{d}g_{k}
. From that and from the identity
x=\mathsf{e}^{\log(x)}
follows, for any
x\in\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
and
h\in\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
,

h=\mathrm{d}\exp(\log(x))(\mathrm{d}\log(x)(h))=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\log(x)^{l-1}\mathrm{d}\log(x)(h)\log(x)^{k-l}.

Fix a spectral decomposition
x=u\diag(\lambda)u^{*}
with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}
and
u\in\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
and define
\xi:=u^{*}hu
and
\eta:=u^{*}\mathrm{d}\log(x)(h)u
, then the above equation is equivalent to

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\log(\diag(\lambda))^{l-1}\eta\log(\diag(\lambda))^{k-l}=\xi.

Let
i\in[1,n]
, then the
(i,i)
-
component of the equation reads

\xi_{i,i}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\log(\lambda_{i})^{l-1}\eta_{i,i}\log(\lambda_{i})^{k-l}=\eta_{i,i}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\,k\log(\lambda_{i})^{k-1}=\eta_{i,i}\mathsf{e}^{\log(\lambda_{i})}=\lambda_{i}\eta_{i,i}

which yields the solution

\eta_{i,i}=\frac{\xi_{i,i}}{\lambda_{i}}.

Therewith we can continue,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}f_{k}(x)(h)

\displaystyle=\langle k\log(x)^{k-1},\mathrm{d}\log(x)(h)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle k\log(\diag(\lambda))^{k-1},\eta\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}k\log(\lambda_{i})^{k-1}\,\eta_{i,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}k\log(\lambda_{i})^{k-1}\lambda_{i}^{-1}\,\xi_{i,i}=\langle k\log(\diag(\lambda))^{k-1}\diag(\lambda)^{-1},\xi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle k\log(x)^{k-1}x^{-1},h\rangle,

and so
\nabla f_{k}(x)=k\log(x)^{k-1}x^{-1}
. Finally let
R\in(1,\infty)
, then we have, for all
x\in\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
such that
\frac{1}{R}\leq\lambda_{m}(x)\leq\lambda_{1}(x)\leq R
,

\lVert\nabla f_{k}(x)\rVert_{S_{2}}\leq k\lVert\log(x)\rVert_{S_{2}}^{k-1}\lVert x^{-1}\rVert_{S_{2}}\leq k(m^{1/2}\log(R))^{k-1}(m^{1/2}R),

and since

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{p^{k}}{k!}\,k(m^{1/2}\log(R))^{k-1}(m^{1/2}R)=pm^{1/2}R\mathsf{e}^{pm^{1/2}\log(R)}=pm^{1/2}\,R^{pm^{1/2}+1}<\infty,

the series
\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{p^{k}}{k!}\nabla f_{k}
converges locally uniformly on
\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
as desired, so we obtain, for any
x\in\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
,

\nabla\tau_{p}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{p^{k}}{k!}k\log(x)^{k-1}x^{-1}=p\mathsf{e}^{p\log(x)}x^{-1}=px^{p-1}.\qed

Theorem C
.

For
\Hausd^{\beta mn-1}
-
almost all
x\in\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}
,

\langle\nu(x),x\rangle=\begin{cases}\lVert x\rVert_{S_{2p-2}}^{1-p}&\textit{if }p<\infty,\\
1&\textit{if }p=\infty;\end{cases}

in particular the cone measure
\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
coincides with the normalized Hausdorff
-
measure on
\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}
iff
p\in\{1,2,\infty\}
. Thereby also,

\Hausd^{\beta mn-1}(\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n})=\begin{cases}\beta m^{3/2}n\omega_{S_{1},\beta}^{m\times n}&\text{if }p=1,\\
\beta mn\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}&\text{if }p\in\{2,\infty\}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Case
p<\infty
:
Define the map

g\colon\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathbb{K}^{m\times n}&\to\mathbb{R}\\
x&\mapsto\Spur((xx^{*})^{p/2})\end{aligned}\right.,

then
\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}=g^{-1}\{1\}
, and via Lemma
3.8

g
is differentiable at any
x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
such that
xx^{*}\in\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
, which is almost everywhere, and then, for any
h\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}g(x)(h)

\displaystyle=\Bigl\langle\frac{p}{2}(xx^{*})^{p/2-1},hx^{*}+xh^{*}\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle p(xx^{*})^{p/2-1}x,h\rangle,

which means

\nabla g(x)=p(xx^{*})^{p/2-1}x.

From this follow, for almost any
x\in\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}
, both

\displaystyle\langle\nabla g(x),x\rangle

\displaystyle=p\langle(xx^{*})^{p/2-1}x,x\rangle=p\langle(xx^{*})^{p/2-1}xx^{*},I_{m}\rangle

\displaystyle=p\langle(xx^{*})^{p/2},I_{m}\rangle=p\Spur((xx^{*})^{p/2})=p,

and

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla g(x)\rVert_{S_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=p^{2}\langle(xx^{*})^{p/2-1}x,(xx^{*})^{p/2-1}x\rangle=p^{2}\langle(xx^{*})^{p-2}xx^{*},I_{m}\rangle

\displaystyle=p^{2}\Spur((xx^{*})^{p-1})=p^{2}\lVert x\rVert_{S_{2p-2}}^{2p-2},

which imply

\langle\nu(x),x\rangle=\frac{\langle\nabla g(x),x\rangle}{\lVert\nabla g(x)\rVert_{S_{2}}}=\frac{1}{\lVert x\rVert_{S_{2p-2}}^{p-1}},

as claimed.

Case
p=\infty
:
Define
D:=\{x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\,:\,s_{2}(x)<1\}
, then
D
is an open subset of
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
, and we can write
\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}=D\cap\{x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\,:\,\det(I_{m}-xx^{*})=0\}
up to the null set
\{x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\,:\,s_{1}(x)=s_{2}(x)=1\}
. Define the map

g\colon\left\{\begin{aligned} D&\to\mathbb{R}\\
x&\mapsto\det(I_{m}-xx^{*})\end{aligned}\right.,

then
g
is continuously differentiable,
\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}=g^{-1}\{0\}
, and for any
x,h\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
the derivative is

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}g(x)(h)

\displaystyle=\Spur\bigl(\Adj(I_{d}-xx^{*})(-hx^{*}-xh^{*})\bigr)

\displaystyle=-2\langle\Adj(I_{m}-xx^{*})x,h\rangle,

where
\Adj
denotes the adjugate of a matrix, and hence,

\nabla g(x)=-2\Adj(I_{m}-xx^{*})x.

For any
x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
with singular value decomposition
x=v\diag(s(x))u^{*}
we have

\Adj(I_{m}-xx^{*})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\biggl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{m}\bigl(1-s_{j}(x)^{2}\bigr)\biggr)v_{i}v_{i}^{*},

where
v_{1},\dotsc,v_{m}\in\mathbb{K}^{m}
are the columns of
v
; in particular for all
x\in\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}
we get

\Adj(I_{m}-xx^{*})x=\biggl(\prod_{j=2}^{m}\bigl(1-s_{j}(x)^{2}\bigr)\biggr)v_{1}(x^{*}v_{1})^{*}=\alpha v_{1}u_{1}^{*},

with
\alpha:=\prod_{j=2}^{m}(1-s_{j}(x)^{2})
and
u_{1}\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
the first column of
u
. Thus
\nabla g(x)\neq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}
which have simple maximal singular value
1
, and this means that
\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}
has a continuous outer unit normal vector field almost everywhere and up to sign it is given by
\frac{\nabla g(x)}{\lVert\nabla g(x)\rVert_{S_{2}}}
at
x\in\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}
with simple singular value
1
, then

\displaystyle\langle\nabla g(x),x\rangle

\displaystyle=-2\alpha\langle v_{1}u_{1}^{*},x\rangle

\displaystyle=-2\alpha\langle v_{1},xu_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=-2\alpha\langle v_{1},v_{1}\rangle=-2\alpha,

and similarly,

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla g(x)\rVert_{S_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=4\alpha^{2}\langle v_{1}u_{1}^{*},v_{1}u_{1}^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=4\alpha^{2}\langle v_{1}^{*}v_{1},u_{1}^{*}u_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=4\alpha^{2}\langle 1,1\rangle=4\alpha^{2},

and this yields

\frac{\lvert\langle\nabla g(x),x\rangle\rvert}{\lVert\nabla g(x)\rVert_{S_{2}}}=1.

This also implies that the cone and normalized Hausdorff
-
measures conincide.

In the case
p=1
we have

\Spur((xx^{*})^{p-1})=\Spur(I_{m})=m

which leads to
\langle\nu(x),x\rangle=m^{-1/2}
, which is constant; and in the case
p=2
we see

\lVert x\rVert_{S_{2p-2}}^{p-1}=\lVert x\rVert_{S_{2}}=1

for all
x\in\mathbb{S}_{S_{2},\beta}^{m,n}
, which is constant too. (Also notice that
S_{2,\beta}^{m\times n}
and
\ell_{2,1}^{\beta mn}
are isomorphic Euclidean spaces.) For
p\notin\{1,2,\infty\}
it suffices to show that
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{S_{2p-2}}^{2p-2}
attains at least two different values on
\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}
at points with full rank, since then by continuity it cannot be constant outside of a null set. But this is achieved with, say,
m^{-1/p}\diag(1,\dotsc,1)
(
m
ones) and
(m-\frac{1}{2})^{-1/p}\diag(1,\dotsc,1,2^{-1/p})
(
m-1
ones).

The volume of
\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}
follows from Lemma
3.6
and the concrete values of
\langle\nu(x),x\rangle
.
∎

The ball
\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
can be described without direct reference to singular values, as the following proposition states.

Proposition 3.9
.

The following identity holds true,

\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}=\{x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\,:\,xx^{*}\leq I_{m}\}.

Proof.

Call
B:=\{x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\,:\,xx^{*}\leq I_{m}\}
. Let
x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
, then
x\in B
iff for all
\xi\in\mathbb{K}^{m}
,
\xi^{*}xx^{*}\xi\leq\xi^{*}\xi
, equivalently
\lVert x^{*}\xi\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\lVert\xi\rVert_{2}^{2}
, this itself means
s_{1}(x^{*})=s_{1}(x)\leq 1
, which is equivalent to
x\in\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
.
∎

The “boundary”
S_{\beta}^{m,n}:=\{x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\,:\,xx^{*}=I_{m}\}
is easy to identify: a close glance at the definition of the Stiefel manifold (see beginning of Section
2.2
) reveals
S_{\beta}^{m,n}=\{x^{*}\,:\,x\in\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}\}=\{x^{\mathsf{T}}\,:\,x\in\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}\}
. Also note the chain of inclusions
S_{\beta}^{m,n}\subset\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}\subset\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
(concerning the first inclusion observe
s(x)=(1)_{i\leq m}
for any
x\in S_{\beta}^{m,n}
).

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times m}
be regular, then the following hold true.

1.

\{x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\,:\,xx^{*}\leq rr^{*}\}=r\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
, and
\vol_{\beta mn}(r\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})=\lvert\det(r)\rvert^{\beta n}\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
.

2.

\partial(r\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})=r\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
, and

\Hausd^{\beta mn-1}(r\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n})=\frac{\lvert\det(r)\rvert^{\beta(n-1)}\Hausd^{\beta m-1}(r\mathbb{S}_{2,\beta}^{m})}{\Hausd^{\beta m-1}(\mathbb{S}_{2,\beta}^{m})}\Hausd^{\beta mn-1}(\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}).

3.

\{x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\,:\,xx^{*}=rr^{*}\}=rS_{\beta}^{m,n}
, and

\Hausd^{d}(rS_{\beta}^{m,n})=2^{-\beta m(m-1)/4}\lvert\det(r)\rvert^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{1\leq j<i\leq m}(s_{j}(r)^{2}+s_{i}(r)^{2})^{\beta/2}\cdot\Omega_{n,m;\beta},

where
d=\frac{\beta m(2n-m+1)}{2}-m
.

Proof.

Before proving the volumes, we argue the claimed set identities, and we fix the map
f\colon\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\to\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
,
x\mapsto rx
.
f
is linear and bijective, hence a homeomorphism, and this immediately gives
\partial(r\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})=r\partial\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}=r\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
. Concerning
r\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
note that
xx^{*}\leq rr^{*}
iff
\xi^{*}xx^{*}\xi\leq\xi^{*}rr^{*}\xi
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{K}^{m}
; now substitute
\eta:=r^{*}\xi
, which is bijective, and then
\eta^{*}r^{-1}xx^{*}(r^{*})^{-1}\eta\leq\eta^{*}\eta
for all
\eta\in\mathbb{K}^{m}
, which means
(r^{-1}x)(r^{-1}x)^{*}\leq I_{m}
and so, by Proposition
3.9
,
r^{-1}x\in\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
, hence the claim. Concerning
rS_{\beta}^{m,n}
, note
xx^{*}=rr^{*}
iff
r^{-1}xx^{*}(r^{*})^{-1}=I_{m}
, and the claim follows.

We now turn to the volumes. The general tool is to calculate the determinant of
f
restricted to the tangent spaces of the respective manifolds; to that end we are going to choose orthonormal bases as fits our need.

1.

Fix a singular value decomposition
r=v\diag(s(r))u^{*}
. Here
f
is acting on the whole space
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
which is its own tangent space, and we choose as basis elements the matrices
ue_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
, additionally
\mathsf{i}ue_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
in the complex case, then

f(ue_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}})=rue_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}=s_{i}(r)ve_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}},\quad f(\mathsf{i}ue_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}})=\mathsf{i}rue_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}=s_{i}(r)\mathsf{i}ve_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}

for all
i\in[1,m],j\in[1,n]
; and because also the
ve_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}

—
and
\mathsf{i}ve_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}

—
together constitute an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
, we get

\det(f)=\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{n}s_{i}(r)\biggr)^{\beta}=\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}(r)\biggr)^{\beta n}=\lvert\det(r)\rvert^{\beta n},

concluding this part.

2.

From the proof of Theorem
C
we know
\nu(x)=v_{1}u_{1}^{*}=ve_{1}e_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}u^{*}
for
x\in\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}
with singular value decomposition
x=v\diag(s(x))u^{*}
such that
s_{1}(x)=1
is isolated. Therefore an orthonormal base of the tangent space at
x
is given by
v_{i}u_{j}^{*}
for all
(i,j)\in[1,m]\times[1,n]
except
(i,j)=(1,1)

—
in the complex case additionally
\mathsf{i}v_{i}u_{j}^{*}
for all
(i,j)\in[1,m]\times[1,n]
. Because
u
,
v
depend on
x
it makes no sense to fix a decomposition of
r
beforehand. Instead, given
x
, define
t:=v^{*}rv\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times m}
; then
rv_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}t_{k,i}v_{k}
for any
i\in[1,m]
. Let
j\in[1,n]
and
i\in[1,m]
, then

f(v_{i}u_{j}^{*})=rv_{i}u_{j}^{*}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}t_{k,i}v_{k}u_{j}^{*}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Re(t_{k,i})v_{k}u_{j}^{*}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Im(t_{k,i})\mathsf{i}v_{k}u_{j}^{*},

and specially for the complex case,

f(\mathsf{i}v_{i}u_{j}^{*})=rv_{i}u_{j}^{*}=-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Im(t_{k,i})v_{k}u_{j}^{*}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Re(t_{k,i})\mathsf{i}v_{k}u_{j}^{*}.

This means that for any
j\in[1,n]
, the subspace spanned by
v_{i}u_{j}^{*}
with
i\in[1,m]

—
additionally
\mathsf{i}v_{i}u_{j}^{*}
in the complex case
—
is
f
-
invariant and with respect to that orthonormal basis the matrix of
f
is given by
t

—
by
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\Re(t)&-\Im(t)\\
\Im(t)&\Re(t)\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
in the complex case. Also note that for distinct
j
those subspaces are mutually orthogonal. Thus for any
j\in[2,n]
the associated subspace makes the contribution
\lvert\det(t)\rvert^{\beta}=\lvert\det(r)\rvert^{\beta}
to the functional determinant, yielding a total of
\lvert\det(r)\rvert^{\beta(n-1)}
.

For
j=1
though we lack
f(v_{1}u_{1}^{*})
, therefore the matrix of
f
is the (
\beta m\times(\beta m-1)
)
-
matrix
\mathbf{t}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
I_{\beta m-1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, that is
\mathbf{t}
sans the first column; here we mean
\mathbf{t}:=t
in the real case and
\mathbf{t}:=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\Re(t)&-\Im(t)\\
\Im(t)&\Re(t)\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
in the complex case. The contribution to the functional determinant in this case is the Gramian

G(x):=\det\left((0,I_{\beta m-1})\mathbf{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{t}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
I_{\beta m-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)^{1/2}=\det\left((0,I_{\beta m-1})\mathbf{v}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{r}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{r}\mathbf{v}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
I_{\beta m-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)^{1/2},

where we have used that
ab
corresponds to
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}
, that
a^{*}
corresponds to
\mathbf{a}^{\mathsf{T}}
, and that
v\in\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
corresponds to
\mathbf{v}\in\mathrm{U}_{\beta m;1}
. Summing up, the functional determinant of
f
at
x
is given by

D(x):=\lvert\det(r)\rvert^{\beta(n-1)}G(x);

note that it depends on
x
only through
v
, not
u
nor
s(x)
. This allows us to compute the volume of
r\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}
as follows, where we use Theorem
C
to transform from Hausdorff- to cone measure,

\displaystyle\Hausd^{\beta mn-1}(r\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}}D(x)\,\mathrm{d}\!\Hausd^{\beta mn-1}(x)

\displaystyle=\Hausd^{\beta mn-1}(\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n})\int_{\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}}D(x)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Hausd^{\beta mn-1}(\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n})}{\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}D\Bigl(\frac{x}{\lVert x\rVert_{S_{\infty}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\Hausd^{\beta mn-1}(\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n})}{\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}\,Cm!\int_{W^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}(v\diag(\sigma)u^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot D\Bigl(\frac{v\diag(\sigma)u^{*}}{\lVert v\diag(\sigma)u^{*}\rVert_{S_{\infty}}}\Bigr)h(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\Hausd^{\beta mn-1}(\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n})\,\frac{2^{\beta m(m-1)/4}}{\Omega_{m;\beta}}\,\lvert\det(r)\rvert^{\beta(n-1)}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}G(v\diag(e_{1}))\,\mathrm{d}v;

we note a few points: in the fourth line we have abbreviated
C:=\frac{2^{\beta m/2+\beta m(m-1)/4+\beta n(n-1)/4}}{2^{\beta mn/2}m!\Omega_{1;\beta}^{m}\Omega_{n-m;\beta}}
and
h(\sigma):=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\sigma_{j}^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{1\leq j<i\leq m}\lvert\sigma_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2}\rvert^{\beta}
, and as we need the singular values to be in decreasing order for
G
we have integrated over
W^{m}
, cf. Remark
2.5
; for the last line we have invested the structure of
D
and the knowledge that
G
only depends on
v
, therefore we have inserted some constant values for
\sigma
and
u
, namely
e_{1}
and
I_{n}
, resp. In order to identify the last integral we rely on the following transformation formula, which can be derived from the coarea formula in considering the map
\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}\to\mathbb{S}_{2,\beta}^{m}
given by
v\mapsto ve_{1}
:

\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}f(v)\,\mathrm{d}v=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{2,\beta}^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m-1;\beta}}f\left(\hat{\theta}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&w\end{pmatrix}\right)\,\mathrm{d}w\,\mathrm{d}\!\Hausd^{\beta m-1}(\theta),

(11)

where
\hat{\theta}\in\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
is such that
\hat{\theta}e_{1}=\theta
. In our situation we need to consider, in the real case,

\hat{\theta}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&w\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
I_{m-1}\end{pmatrix}=\hat{\theta}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
I_{m-1}\end{pmatrix}w,

and similarly in the complex case,

\hat{\bm{\theta}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&0\\
0&\Re(w)&0&-\Im(w)\\
0&0&1&0\\
0&\Im(w)&0&\Re(w)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
I_{2m-1}\end{pmatrix}=\hat{\bm{\theta}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
I_{2m-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Re(w)&0&-\Im(w)\\
0&1&0\\
\Im(w)&0&\Re(w)\end{pmatrix}\!;

now we recall that a Gramian is orthogonally invariant, hence

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}G(v\diag(e_{1}))\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{2,\beta}^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m-1;\beta}}\det\left((0,I_{\beta m-1})\hat{\bm{\theta}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{r}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{r}\hat{\bm{\theta}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
I_{\beta m-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}w\,\mathrm{d}\!\Hausd^{\beta m-1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{m-1;\beta}}{2^{\beta(m-1)(m-2)/4}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{2,\beta}^{m}}\det\left((0,I_{\beta m-1})\hat{\bm{\theta}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{r}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{r}\hat{\bm{\theta}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
I_{\beta m-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\!\Hausd^{\beta m-1}(\theta).

The conclusion of this part is reached through the identities

\int_{\mathbb{S}_{2,\beta}^{m}}\det\left(0,I_{\beta m-1})\hat{\bm{\theta}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{r}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{r}\hat{\bm{\theta}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
I_{\beta m-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\!\Hausd^{\beta m-1}(\theta)=\Hausd^{\beta m-1}(r\mathbb{S}_{2,\beta}^{m})

and

\Omega_{m;\beta}=2^{\beta(m-1)/2}\,\Omega_{m-1;\beta}\Hausd^{\beta m-1}(\mathbb{S}_{2,\beta}^{m}),

the first of which can be proved by considering the map
\mathbb{S}_{2,\beta}^{m}\to\mathbb{K}^{m}
given by
\theta\mapsto r\theta
, leading to calculations which essentially are the same as those above for
\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}
without writing the
u_{j}
and only considering
j=1
; the latter can be proved either from the volume formula (
1
) or from the transformation formula (
11
).

3.

Fix a singular value decomposition
r=v\diag(s(r))u^{*}
. We already know
S_{\beta}^{m,n}=\{w^{*}\,:\,w\in\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}\}
and hence
rS_{\beta}^{m,n}=\{(wr^{*})^{*}\,:\,w\in\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}\}
, and for any
w\in\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}
the tangent space is spanned by the matrices
E_{i,j}

—
additionally
F_{i,j}
in the complex case
—
given by

\displaystyle F_{j,j}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{i}wue_{j}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}u^{*}

\displaystyle\text{ for }j\in[1,m],

\displaystyle E_{i,j}

\displaystyle:=\frac{w}{\sqrt{2}}\,u(e_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}-e_{j}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})u^{*}

\displaystyle\text{ for }1\leq j<i\leq m,

\displaystyle F_{i,j}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathsf{i}w}{\sqrt{2}}\,u(e_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}+e_{j}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})u^{*}

\displaystyle\text{ for }1\leq j<i\leq m,

\displaystyle E_{i,j}

\displaystyle:=\hat{w}e_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}u^{*}

\displaystyle\text{ for }i\in[m+1,n],j\in[1,m],

\displaystyle F_{i,j}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{i}\hat{w}e_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}u^{*}

\displaystyle\text{ for }i\in[m+1,n],j\in[1,m],

where
\hat{w}\in\mathrm{U}_{n,\beta}
is an extension of
w
with suitable
n-m
columns. Their respective images are, in the same order,

\displaystyle f(F_{j,j})

\displaystyle=s_{j}(r)\mathsf{i}wue_{j}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}v^{*},

\displaystyle f(E_{i,j})

\displaystyle=\frac{w}{\sqrt{2}}\,u(s_{j}(r)e_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}-s_{i}(r)e_{j}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})v^{*},

\displaystyle f(F_{i,j})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathsf{i}w}{\sqrt{2}}\,u(s_{j}(r)e_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}+s_{i}(r)e_{j}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})v^{*},

\displaystyle f(E_{i,j})

\displaystyle=s_{j}(r)\hat{w}e_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}v^{*},

\displaystyle f(F_{i,j})

\displaystyle=s_{j}(r)\mathsf{i}\hat{w}e_{i}e_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}v^{*}.

Clearly the elements of each line are orthogonal to the other lines, and the elements of the first, fourth and fifth lines also are orthogonal among themselves and their norms are the indicated singular values. Orthogonality is even true for the second and third lines, although less obvious: let
1\leq j<i\leq m
and
1\leq j^{\prime}<i^{\prime}\leq m
, then

\langle f(E_{i,j}),f(E_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})\rangle=\frac{s_{i}(r)s_{i^{\prime}}(r)+s_{j}(r)s_{j^{\prime}}(r)}{2}\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}-\frac{s_{i}(r)s_{j^{\prime}}(r)+s_{i^{\prime}}(r)s_{j}(r)}{2}\delta_{i,j^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime},j};

but for our choice of indices always
\delta_{i,j^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime},j}=0
, and so orthgonality follows. The analogous calculation goes through for
f(F_{i,j})
. Therefore the functional determinant equals

\biggl(\prod_{j=1}^{m}s_{j}(r)\biggr)^{\beta-1}\biggl(\prod_{1\leq j<i\leq m}\frac{(s_{j}(r)^{2}+s_{i}(r)^{2})^{1/2}}{\sqrt{2}}\biggr)^{\beta}\biggl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq m<i\leq n}s_{j}(r)\biggr)^{\beta}\\
=2^{-\beta m(m-1)/4}\lvert\det(r)\rvert^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{1\leq j<i\leq m}(s_{j}(r)^{2}+s_{i}(r))^{\beta/2}.

Since this is indpendent of
w
, this implies directly

\Hausd^{d}(\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}r^{*})=2^{-\beta m(m-1)/4}\lvert\det(r)\rvert^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{1\leq j<i\leq m}(s_{j}(r)^{2}+s_{i}(r))^{\beta/2}\cdot\Omega_{n,m;\beta},

and because adjoining is an isometry the result for
S_{\beta}^{m,n}
follows. ∎

Remark 3.11
.

The factor
\Hausd^{\beta m-1}(r\mathbb{S}_{2,\beta}^{m})
in Proposition
3.10
, part 2, is the surface area of an ellipsoid, as such it cannot be expressed in terms of elementary functions of
s(r)
(the semiaxes of the ellipsoid).

4
Probabilistic results and weak limit theorems for
p=\infty

For all subsequent probabilistic results we suppose a sufficiently rich probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\Wsk)
on which all random variables that appear in the present paper are defined. The expected value of a random variable
X
w.r.t.
\Wsk
is denoted
\Erw[X]
and is to be understood component
-
wise for vector
-
valued
X
.
X\sim\mu
means the law, or distribution, of
X
is
\mu
, and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}
denotes equality in law. For
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\vol_{d}(A)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
the uniform distribution on
A
(w.r.t. Lebesgue measure) is
\Gleichv(A)
, and
\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta})
means the normalized invariant measure on
\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}
(see also Section
2.2
). Convergence in law, in probability, and almost surely are denoted by
\xrightarrow{\text{d}}
,
\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}
, and
\xrightarrow{\text{a.s.}}
respectively.

The following can be considered a variant of the Schechtman
–
Zinn representation of the uniform distribution on the
\ell_{p}
-
balls. It uses the
matrix
-
variate type
-
I beta
-
distribution,
written
B_{m,\beta}^{\text{I}}(a,b)
and defined as follows (see
[
18
, Definition 5.2.1]
): suppose
a,b>\frac{\beta(m-1)}{2}
, then
B_{m,\beta}^{\text{I}}(a,b)
is the probability distribution on
\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
with density

x\mapsto\frac{\Gamma_{m,\beta}(a+b)}{\Gamma_{m,\beta}(a)\Gamma_{m,\beta}(b)}\det(x)^{a-1-\beta(m-1)/2}\det(I_{m}-x)^{b-1-\beta(m-1)/2}\Ind_{(0,I_{m})}(x),

where of course
(0,I_{m}):=\{x\in\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}\,:\,0<x<I_{m}\}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

A random variable
X\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
is distributed uniformly on
\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
iff there exist independent random variables
R\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times m}
and
U\in\mathbb{K}^{n\times m}
such that
R\sim B_{m,\beta}^{\text{I}}(\frac{\beta n}{2},\frac{\beta(m-1)}{2}+1)
,
U\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta})
, and

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R^{1/2}\,U^{*}.

Remark 4.2
.

Note that from
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R^{1/2}\,U^{*}
follow
R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}XX^{*}
and
U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}X^{*}(XX^{*})^{-1/2}
.

Proof.

The statement is a simple consequence of Proposition
2.4
, part 1. We have
X\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})
iff, for any
f\colon\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
nonnegative and measurable,

\displaystyle\Erw[f(X)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}(x)f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-m}}{\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}\int_{\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}(r^{1/2}\,u^{*})f(r^{1/2}\,u^{*})\,\mathrm{d}u\det(r)^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-m}}{\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}\int_{\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}}f(r^{1/2}\,u^{*})\,\mathrm{d}u\Ind_{(0,I_{m}]}(r)\det(r)^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\Erw[f(R^{1/2}\,U^{*})],

where
R
and
U
have the claimed joint distribution. (This is obvious for
U
; concerning
R
one may compare our result with the known density.) In the last integral,
r^{1/2}\,u^{*}\in\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
iff
r\leq I_{m}
, and note that
(0,I_{m}]\setminus(0,I_{m})
is negligible.
∎

Another representation, related to the first one, can be obtained in the special case
n=dm
for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Here we may identify
\mathbb{K}^{m\times dm}\cong(\mathbb{K}^{m\times m})^{d}
, constisting of
d
-
dimensional row vectors whose entries are from
\mathbb{K}^{m\times m}
. Then, for
x=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{d}),y=(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{d})\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times dm}
,
xy^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}y_{i}^{*}
, and hence also
\langle x,y\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\langle x_{i},y_{i}\rangle
. It uses the
matrix
-
variate type
-
I Dirichlet
-
distribution

D_{m,\beta}^{\text{I}}(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{d};a_{d+1})
, with
a_{1},\dotsc,a_{d+1}>\frac{\beta(m-1)}{2}
, defined to be the probability distribution on
\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}^{d}
with density (see
[
18
, Definition 6.2.1]
)

\begin{split}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{d})&\mapsto\frac{\Gamma_{m,\beta}\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}a_{i}\bigr)}{\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}\Gamma_{m,\beta}(a_{i})}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\det(x_{i})^{a_{i}-1-\beta(m-1)/2}\\
&\qquad\cdot\det\biggl(I_{m}-\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}\biggr)^{a_{d+1}-1-\beta(m-1)/2}\Ind_{\Delta_{m,\beta}^{d}}(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{d}),\end{split}

where
\Delta_{m,\beta}^{d}:=\bigl\{(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{d})\in\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}^{d}\,:\,x_{1},\dotsc,x_{d}>0\wedge\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}<I_{m}\bigr\}
.

Remark 4.3
.

With this componentwise interpretation the spectral norm unit ball can be written
\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times dm}=\bigl\{(x_{i})_{i\leq d}\in(\mathbb{K}^{m\times m})^{d}\,:\,\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}x_{i}^{*}\leq I_{m}\bigr\}
, which looks like a natural generalization of the usual
\ell_{2}
-
ball
\mathbb{B}_{2,\beta}^{d}=\bigl\{(x_{i})_{i\leq d}\in\mathbb{K}^{d}\,:\,\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{2}\leq 1\bigr\}
, replacing the scalar components with matrix
-
valued ones. In truth this was the starting point of the present investigations.

Proposition 4.4
.

A random vector
X\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times dm}
is distributed uniformly on
\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times dm}
iff there exist independent random variables
R=(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{d})\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times dm}
and
U_{1},\dotsc,U_{d}\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times m}
such that
R\sim D_{m,\beta}^{\text{I}}(\frac{\beta m}{2}^{[d]};\frac{\beta(m-1)}{2}+1)
(i.e., the parameter
\frac{m}{2}
