occurs
d
times),
U_{i}\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta})
for each
i\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
, and

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}(R_{1}^{1/2}\,U_{1},\dotsc,R_{d}^{1/2}\,U_{d}).

Proof.

Again we apply Proposition
2.4
, part 1, this time separately to each of the
d
components of
\mathbb{K}^{m\times dm}=\mathbb{K}^{m\times m}\times\dotsm\times\mathbb{K}^{m\times m}
. We have
X\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times dm})
iff, for any
f\colon\mathbb{K}^{m\times dm}\to\mathbb{R}
measurable and nonnegative,

\displaystyle\Erw[f(X)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times dm}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m\times dm}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times dm}}(x)f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{(2^{-m})^{d}}{\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times dm}}\int_{\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\dotsi\int_{\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times dm}}(r_{1}^{1/2}\,u_{1}^{*},\dotsc,r_{d}^{1/2}\,u_{d}^{*})

\displaystyle\mspace{100mu}\cdot f(r_{1}^{1/2}\,u_{1}^{*},\dotsc,r_{d}^{1/2}\,u_{d}^{*})\prod_{i=1}^{d}\det(r_{i})^{\beta/2-1}\prod_{i=1}^{d}(\mathrm{d}u_{i}\,\mathrm{d}r_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-dm}}{\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times dm}}\int_{\Delta_{m,\beta}^{d}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}^{d}}f(r_{1}^{1/2}\,u_{1},\dotsc,r_{d}^{1/2}\,u_{d})\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathrm{d}u_{i}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\det(r_{i})^{\beta/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\Erw[f(R_{1}^{1/2}\,U_{1},\dotsc,R_{d}^{1/2}\,U_{d})],

where the desired conclusion is reached by noticing a few points: firstly,
(r_{1}^{1/2}\,u_{1},\dotsc,r_{d}^{1/2}\,u_{d})\in\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times dm}
iff
\sum_{i=1}^{d}r_{i}\leq I_{m}
; secondly, we have transformed
u_{i}\mapsto u_{i}^{*}
, which has no import since we are integrating with respect to the (scaled) Haar measure; thirdly and lastly, the distribution of the
U_{i}
is obvious again, and that of
(R_{1},\dotsc,R_{d})
can be read off by comparing with the known density.
∎

Next we present a Poincaré
–
Maxwell
–
Borel
-
type result. It is useful to keep the following relationships in mind. The standard Gaussian distribution on
\mathbb{R}
is denoted by
\mathcal{N}_{1}
, defined by its Lebesgue
-
density
x\mapsto(2\pi)^{-1/2}\mathsf{e}^{-x^{2}/2}
. The standard Gaussian distribution on
\mathbb{C}
is denoted by
\mathcal{N}_{2}
and defined to be the law of
2^{-1/2}(x+\mathsf{i}y)
, where
(x,y)\sim\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{1}
. Note
\Erw[\lvert X\rvert^{2}]=1
for
X\sim\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
. The content of the following proposition is standard from random matrix theory, especially as used in statistics; textbook references are
[
18
,
35
]
.

Proposition 4.5
.

1.

Let
X\sim\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\otimes(m\times n)}
, then
XX^{*}\sim\Gamma_{m,\beta}(\frac{\beta n}{2},\frac{2}{\beta}I_{m})
, where the matrix
-
variate gamma
-
distribution
\Gamma_{m,\beta}(\alpha,b)
with
\alpha>\frac{\beta(m-1)}{2}
and
b\in\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
is defined on
\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
by its density

x\mapsto\frac{\det(x)^{\alpha-1-\beta(m-1)/2}}{\det(b)^{\alpha}\Gamma_{m,\beta}(\alpha)}\,\mathsf{e}^{-\Spur(b^{-1}x)}.

2.

B_{m,\beta}^{\text{I}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=D_{m,\beta}^{\text{I}}(\alpha_{1};\alpha_{2})
.

3.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{d+1}>\frac{\beta(m-1)}{2}
,
b\in\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}
, and
(X_{i})_{i\leq d+1}\sim\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d+1}\Gamma_{m,\beta}(\alpha_{i},b)
. Define
Y_{i}:=\bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{d+1}X_{j}\bigr)^{-1/2}X_{i}\bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{d+1}X_{j}\bigr)^{-1/2}
for
i\in[1,d]
and
Y_{d+1}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}X_{i}
, then
(Y_{i})_{i\leq d}
and
Y_{d+1}
are independent, and
(Y_{i})_{i\leq d}\sim D_{m,\beta}^{\text{I}}(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{d};\alpha_{d+1})
and
Y_{d+1}\sim\Gamma_{m,\beta}\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i},b\bigr)
.

4.

Consequently, given
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d+1}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq m}
, then
(X_{i})_{i\leq d}\sim D_{m,\beta}^{\text{I}}(\frac{\beta n_{1}}{2},\dotsc,\frac{\beta n_{d}}{2};\frac{\beta n_{d+1}}{2})
iff there exists
(G_{i})_{i\leq d+1}\sim\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d+1}\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\otimes(m\times n_{i})}
such that
X_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{d+1}G_{j}G_{j}^{*}\bigr)^{-1/2}G_{i}G_{i}^{*}\bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{d+1}G_{j}G_{j}^{*}\bigr)^{-1/2}
for each
i\in[1,d]
.

Theorem D
.

Consider
m
fixed, let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
(X_{n})_{n\geq m}
be a sequence of random variables such that either

•

X_{n}\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})
for each
n\geq m
, or

•

X_{n}\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n})
for each
n\geq m
, or

•

X_{n}\sim\Gleichv(S_{\beta}^{m,n})
for each
n\geq m
.

Denote the projection of
X_{n}
onto the first
k
columns by
X_{n}^{(k)}
. Then in any case

(n^{1/2}X_{n}^{(k)})_{n\geq\max\{m,k\}}\xrightarrow{\text{d}}\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\otimes(m\times k)}.

Proof.

We begin with the case
X_{n}\sim\Gleichv(S_{\beta}^{m,n})
, then
X_{n}^{*}\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta})
. Let
(g_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an i.i.d.
-
sequence of
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\otimes m}
-
distributed vectors and set
G_{n}:=(g_{1},\dotsc,g_{n})
for
n\geq 1
, then
G_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\otimes(m\times n)}
, and by Proposition
5.5
we have
X_{n}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}G_{n}^{*}(G_{n}G_{n}^{*})^{-1/2}
. It follows that

X_{n}^{(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}(G_{n}G_{n}^{*})^{-1/2}G_{k}.

By the strong law of large numbers,

\frac{1}{n}\,G_{n}G_{n}^{*}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}g_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\text{a.s.}}\Erw[g_{1}g_{1}^{*}]=I_{m},

(12)

and therefore,

n^{1/2}X_{n}^{(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}\,G_{n}G_{n}^{*}\Bigr)^{-1/2}G_{k}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\text{a.s.}}G_{k},

which proves the claim.

We proceed with the case
X_{n}\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})
, then by Proposition
4.1

X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R_{n}^{1/2}\,U_{n}^{*}

with independent random variables
R_{n}\sim B_{m,\beta}^{\text{I}}(\frac{\beta n}{2},\frac{\beta(m-1)}{2}+1)
and
U_{n}\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta})
. The first case implies

(n^{1/2}{U_{n}^{*}}^{(k)})_{n\geq\max\{m,k\}}\xrightarrow{\text{d}}G_{k}.

Using the probabilistic representation of
B_{m,\beta}^{\text{I}}(\frac{\beta n}{2},\frac{\beta(m-1)}{2}+1)
from Proposition
4.5
we can write

R_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}(G_{n}G_{n}^{*}+H)^{-1/2}G_{n}G_{n}^{*}(G_{n}G_{n}^{*}+H)^{-1/2},

where
H\sim\Gamma_{m,\beta}(\frac{\beta(m-1)}{2}+1,\frac{2}{\beta}I_{m})
is independent from
(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. Since
(n^{-1}H)_{n\geq 1}\xrightarrow{\text{a.s.}}0
, together with Equation (
12
) we infer

(R_{n})_{n\geq m}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}I_{m},

and therewith we conclude

n^{1/2}X_{n}^{(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R_{n}^{1/2}\,n^{1/2}{U_{n}^{*}}^{(k)}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\text{d}}G_{k},

as desired.

Lastly we consider the case
X_{n}\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n})=\kappa_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
. Let
Y_{n}\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})
, then by Proposition
5.4
,

X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\frac{Y_{n}}{\lVert Y_{n}\rVert_{S_{\infty}}}

and by the same proposition and by Remark
5.2
, part 1, the distribution of
\lVert Y_{n}\rVert_{S_{\infty}}
has density
r\mapsto\beta mnr^{\beta mn-1}\Ind_{[0,1]}(r)
. The latter fact implies
\lVert Y_{n}\rVert_{S_{\infty}}^{\beta mn}\sim\Gleichv([0,1])
and therefore

(\lVert Y_{n}\rVert_{S_{\infty}})_{n\geq m}\xrightarrow{\text{a.s.}}1.

(First use
\lVert Y_{n}\rVert_{S_{\infty}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}U^{1/(\beta mn)}
with
U\sim\Gleichv([0,1])
to get convergence in distribution; because the limit is constant, promote to convergence in probability; use Borel
–
Cantelli to obtain almost sure convergence.) This finally leads to

n^{1/2}X_{n}^{(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\frac{n^{1/2}Y_{n}^{(k)}}{\lVert Y_{n}\rVert_{S_{\infty}}}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\text{d}}G_{k},

and the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

Essentially the same result, but using a very different method, for the case of
\Gleichv(S_{\beta}^{m,n})
has been obtained recently by Petrov and Vershik
[
56
, Theorem 3.1]
.

The next proposition has a nice geometric interpretation: pick two points independently from either
\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
,
\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}
, or
S_{\beta}^{m,n}
, then they will be nearly orthogonal, when the dimension is high. This will be formulated as a distributional limit theorem.

Theorem E
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq m}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\geq m}
be sequences of random variables such that individually their distributions satisfy the same conditions as in Theorem
D
, and additionally let
\{X_{n},Y_{n}\}
be independent for each
n\geq m
. Then

(n^{1/2}X_{n}Y_{n}^{*})_{n\geq m}\xrightarrow{\text{d}}\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\otimes(m\times m)},

and

((\beta m^{-1}\,n)^{1/2}\langle X_{n},Y_{n}\rangle)_{n\geq m}\xrightarrow{\text{d}}\mathcal{N}_{1}.

Proof.

We use the same notation as in the proof of Theorem
D
. Note that the second convergence is an almost immediate consequence of the first one: apply the real part of the trace to get

\frac{(\beta n)^{1/2}}{m^{1/2}}\langle X_{n},Y_{n}\rangle=\frac{\beta^{1/2}}{m^{1/2}}\Re\Spur(n^{1/2}X_{n}Y_{n}^{*})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\text{d}}\frac{\beta^{1/2}}{m^{1/2}}\Re\Spur(G_{m})=\frac{\beta^{1/2}}{m^{1/2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Re(g_{i,i})\sim\mathcal{N}_{1},

since the
\Re(g_{i,i})
are i.i.d. Gaussian with zero mean and variance
\frac{1}{\beta}
.

Concerning the first statement, it suffices to consider the case
X_{n},Y_{n}\sim\Gleichv(S_{\beta}^{m,n})
; then use the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
D
to get rid of any ‘radial’ components of the random variables. Write

X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}(G_{n}G_{n}^{*})^{-1/2}G_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}(G_{n}^{\prime}(G_{n}^{\prime})^{*})^{-1/2}G_{n}^{\prime},

where
G_{n}^{\prime}=(g_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,g_{n}^{\prime})
is an independent copy of
G_{n}
, to obtain

\displaystyle n^{1/2}X_{n}Y_{n}^{*}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}n^{1/2}(G_{n}G_{n}^{*})^{-1/2}G_{n}(G_{n}^{\prime})^{*}(G_{n}^{\prime}(G_{n}^{\prime})^{*})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}G_{n}G_{n}^{*}\Bigr)^{-1/2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}G_{n}(G_{n}^{\prime})^{*}\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}G_{n}^{\prime}(G_{n}^{\prime})^{*}\Bigr)^{-1/2}.

Because of Equation (
12
) the square root terms are accounted for and they do not contribute to the limit. The middle term can be written out,

G_{n}(G_{n}^{\prime})^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}g_{j}(g_{j}^{\prime})^{*},

and this is a sum of i.i.d. random variables; the summands are centred because of

\Erw[g_{1}g_{1}^{\prime*}]=\Erw[g_{1}]\Erw[g_{j}^{\prime}]^{*}=oo^{\mathsf{T}}=0,

their covariance matrix equals

\displaystyle\Cov[\Vek(g_{1}(g_{1}^{\prime})^{*})]=\Erw[\Vek(g_{1}(g_{1}^{\prime})^{*})\Vek(g_{1}(g_{1}^{\prime})^{*})^{*}]=\overline{\Cov[g_{1}^{\prime}]}\otimes\Cov[g_{1}]=I_{m}\otimes I_{m}=I_{m^{2}},

and in the complex case their pseudo
-
covariance matrix (also called relation matrix) equals

\Rel[\Vek(g_{1}(g_{1}^{\prime})^{*})]=\Erw[\Vek(g_{1}(g_{1}^{\prime})^{*})\Vek(g_{1}(g_{1}^{\prime})^{*})^{\mathsf{T}}]=\overline{\Rel[g_{1}^{\prime}]}\otimes\Rel[g_{1}]=0\otimes 0=0.

Therefore by the multivariate central limit theorem

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}G_{n}(G_{n}^{\prime})^{*}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\text{d}}G_{m}.

This finally yields

n^{1/2}X_{n}Y_{n}^{*}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\text{d}}G_{m}.\qed

5
Theory of
K
-
symmetric measures

Referring back to Remark
4.2
, we see that if
X\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})
, then
X^{*}(XX^{*})^{-1/2}\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta})
. But it is also well known that if
X\sim\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\otimes(m\times n)}
, then
X^{\mathsf{T}}(XX^{\mathsf{T}})^{-1/2}\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n,m;1})
too (see
[
9
, Theorem 2.2. and top of p. 270]
, e.g.). This can be generalized. But first we need to introduce a notion of symmetry of a measure.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a star body. A Borel
-
measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called
K
-
symmetric
iff there exists a Borel
-
measure
\rho
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that for any measurable, nonnegative map
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\partial K}f(r\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(r),

where recall that
\kappa_{K}
denotes the normalized cone measure on
\partial K
.

Remark 5.2
.

1.

By the polar integration formula (
9
) the uniform distribution on
K
is
K
-
symmetric with
\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho}{\mathrm{d}r}(r)=dr^{d-1}\Ind_{[0,1]}(r)
; and
\kappa_{K}
itself is
K
-
symmetric with
\rho=\delta_{1}
.

2.

Given a
K
-
symmetric measure
\mu
, the ‘radial measure’
\rho
as introduced above is unique. (This can be proved by inserting
f=\Ind_{A}
with
A=\{r\theta\,:\,r\in A_{1},\theta\in\partial K\}
for some Borel
-
set
A_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.)

That Definition
5.1
captures the symmetry of
K
in a natural way is argued by the following statement which tells us that the density of a
K
-
symmetric measure is constant on the dilates of
\partial K
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
d
,
K
be as in Definition
5.1
, and let
\mu
be absolutely continuous w.r.t.
\vol_{d}
. Then
\mu
is
K
-
symmetric iff its
\vol_{d}
-
density is of the form
f\circ\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}
with some measurable, nonnegative
f\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
; and then
\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho(r)}{\mathrm{d}r}=d\vol_{d}(K)f(r)r^{d-1}
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
: Call
F:=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\!\vol_{d}}
. Let
h\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and nonnegative. Then, with a suitable measure
\rho
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\partial K}h(r\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x)F(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

(13)

\displaystyle=d\vol_{d}(K)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\partial K}h(r\theta)F(r\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)\,r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}r.

Choose
h=h_{1}\circ\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}
with some measurable, nonnegative
h_{1}\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
, then
h(r\theta)=h_{1}(r)
for any
r>0
and
\theta\in\partial K
, and therewith

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}h_{1}(r)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(r)=d\vol_{d}(K)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}h_{1}(r)\int_{\partial K}F(r\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)\,r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}r.

(14)

Define
f(r):=\int_{\partial K}F(r\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)
for
r\geq 0
, then Equation
14
tells us
\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho}{\mathrm{d}r}(r)=d\vol_{d}(K)f(r)r^{d-1}
, and going back to Equation
13
, again with
h
arbitrary, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x)F(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\partial K}h(r\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)\,d\vol_{d}(K)f(r)r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=d\vol_{d}(K)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\partial K}h(r\theta)f(\lvert r\theta\rvert_{K})\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)\,r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x)f(\lvert x\rvert_{K})\,\mathrm{d}x,

and from this we obtain
F=f\circ\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}
, as claimed.

\Leftarrow
: Let
h\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and nonnegative. Then,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(x)f(\lvert x\rvert_{K})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=d\vol_{d}(K)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\partial K}h(r\theta)f(\lvert r\theta\rvert_{K})r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\partial K}h(r\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)\,d\vol_{d}(K)f(r)r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}r,

hence the claim follows with
\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho}{\mathrm{d}r}(r):=d\vol_{d}(K)f(r)r^{d-1}
.
∎

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
d
,
K
,
\mu
,
\rho
be as in Definition
5.1
, with
\mu
a probabiliy measure, and let
X
be a
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-
valued random variable. Then
X\sim\mu
iff there exist independent random variables
R
and
\Theta
such that
R\sim\rho
,
\Theta\sim\kappa_{K}
, and
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R\Theta
. In that case
\lvert X\rvert_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R
.

If in addition
\mu\{o\}=0
, then
\frac{X}{\lvert X\rvert_{K}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\Theta
, and
\lvert X\rvert_{K}
and
\frac{X}{\lvert X\rvert_{K}}
are stochastically independent.

Proof.

The representation
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R\Theta
together with the properties of
R
and
\Theta
are a mere rewriting of Definition
5.1
. Because of
\Theta\sim\kappa_{K}
we have
\Theta\in\partial K
almost surely, hence
\lvert\Theta\rvert_{K}=1
almost surely, also
R\geq 0
almost surely, and thus
\lvert X\rvert_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\lvert R\Theta\rvert_{K}=R\lvert\Theta\rvert_{K}=R
.

Note
\Wsk[X=o]=\mu\{o\}
and
X=o\Longleftrightarrow\lvert X\rvert_{K}=0
, hence if
\mu\{o\}=0
, then
\frac{X}{\lvert X\rvert_{K}}
is almost surely defined. In the latter case, let
f\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
and
g\colon\partial K\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and nonnegative, then,

\displaystyle\Erw\Bigl[f(\lvert X\rvert_{K})g\Bigl(\frac{X}{\lvert X\rvert_{K}}\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\partial K}f(\lvert r\theta\rvert_{K})g\Bigl(\frac{r\theta}{\lvert r\theta\rvert_{K}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\partial K}f(r)g(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}f(r)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(r)\int_{\partial K}g(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta).

From that follow the distribution of
\frac{X}{\lvert X\rvert_{K}}
and the independence of
\lvert X\rvert_{K}
and
\frac{X}{\lvert X\rvert_{K}}
.
∎

Thus equipped we turn to probability distributions on
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
be a star body and let
X
be an
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
-
valued random variable whose distribution is
K
-
symmetric with radial distribution
\rho
.

1.

XX^{*}
is almost surely invertible, iff
X\neq 0
almost surely, iff
\rho\{0\}=0
.

2.

If
\rho\{0\}=0
and additionally
K
is right
-
unitarily invariant (that is
Ku=K
for all
u\in\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}
), then
XX^{*}
and
X^{*}(XX^{*})^{-1/2}
are stochastically independent, and
X^{*}(XX^{*})^{-1/2}\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta})
.

3.

If
K
is right
-
unitarily invariant and the distribution of
X
has
\vol_{\beta mn}
-
density
h\circ\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}
, then the distribution of
XX^{*}
has density proportional to
r\mapsto h(\lvert r^{1/2}\rvert_{\lvert K\rvert})\det(r)^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}
, where
\lvert K\rvert:=\{(xx^{*})^{1/2}\,:\,x\in K\}\subset\text{Sym}_{m,\beta}
.

Proof.

1.

Write
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R\Theta
with
R\sim\rho
and
\Theta\sim\kappa_{K}
independent, according to Proposition
5.4
; then
X=0
iff
\lvert X\rvert_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R=0
, hence
\Wsk[X=0]=\Wsk[R=0]=\rho\{0\}
.

Next note that
XX^{*}
is invertible iff
X
has full rank, iff
s_{m}(X)>0
; using the representation of
X
his yields

\begin{split}\Wsk[s_{m}(X)>0]&=\Wsk[s_{m}(R\Theta)>0]=\Wsk[Rs_{m}(\Theta)>0]\\
&=\Wsk[R>0\wedge s_{m}(\Theta)>0]=\Wsk[R>0]\Wsk[s_{m}(\Theta)>0].\end{split}

Trivially
\Wsk[R>0]=1-\rho\{0\}
; take
U\sim\Gleichv([0,1])
independent of
\Theta
, then
U^{1/(\beta mn)}\Theta\sim\Gleichv(K)
(this follows from Remark
5.2
, part 1) and
\Wsk[U^{1/(\beta mn)}>0]=1
, and by doing the same calculation as before,

1=\frac{\vol_{\beta mn}(\{x\in K\,:\,s_{m}(x)>0\})}{v_{\beta mn}(K)}=\Wsk[s_{m}(U^{1/(\beta mn)}\Theta)>0]=\Wsk[s_{m}(\Theta)>0].

This establishes
\Wsk[s_{m}(X)>0]=1-\rho\{0\}
, thus
XX^{*}
is almost surely invertible iff
\rho\{0\}=0
.

2.

Let
f\colon\!\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}\to\mathbb{R}
and
g\colon\!\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and nonnegative, then

\displaystyle\Erw[f(X^{*}(XX^{*})^{-1/2})g(XX^{*})]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\partial K}f(r\theta^{*}(r^{2}\theta\theta^{*})^{-1/2})g(r^{2}\theta\theta^{*})\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial K}f(\theta^{*}(\theta\theta^{*})^{-1/2})\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}g(r^{2}\theta\theta^{*})\,\mathrm{d}\rho(r)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{K}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\vol_{\beta mn}(K)}\int_{K}f(x^{*}(xx^{*})^{-1/2})\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}g\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}xx^{*}}{\lvert x\rvert_{K}^{2}}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}\rho(r)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-m}}{\vol_{\beta mn}(K)}\int_{\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}}\Ind_{K}(t^{1/2}u^{*})f(ut^{1/2}(t^{1/2}u^{*}ut^{1/2})^{-1/2})

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}g\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}t^{1/2}u^{*}ut^{1/2}}{\lvert t^{1/2}u^{*}\rvert_{K}^{2}}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}\rho(r)\,\mathrm{d}u\det(t)^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}t.

We have
t^{1/2}u^{*}\in K
iff
t^{1/2}\in\lvert K\rvert
; indeed, concerning “
\Rightarrow
” let
t^{1/2}u^{*}\in K
, then
(t^{1/2}u^{*}ut^{1/2})^{1/2}=t^{1/2}\in\lvert K\rvert
. Concerning “
\Leftarrow
” let
t^{1/2}\in\lvert K\rvert
, then there is some
x\in K
such that
t^{1/2}=(xx^{*})^{1/2}
; let
x=\lvert x\rvert v^{*}
be its polar decomposition, then
t^{1/2}=\lvert x\rvert
; and as
K
is right
-
unitarily invariant we get
x\hat{v}\hat{u}^{*}\in K
, and
x\hat{v}\hat{u}^{*}=\lvert x\rvert v^{*}\hat{v}\hat{u}^{*}=\lvert x\rvert u^{*}=t^{1/2}u^{*}
. This also implies
\Ind_{K}(t^{1/2}u^{*})=\Ind_{\lvert K\rvert}(t^{1/2})
. Hence we may continue,

\displaystyle\Erw[f(X^{*}(XX^{*})^{-1/2})g(XX^{*})]

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-m}}{\vol_{\beta mn}(K)}\int_{\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}}\Ind_{\lvert K\rvert}(t^{1/2})f(u)

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}g\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}t}{\lvert t^{1/2}\rvert_{K}^{2}}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}\rho(r)\,\mathrm{d}u\det(t)^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-m}}{\vol_{\beta mn}(K)}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}}f(u)\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\int_{\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}}\Ind_{\lvert K\rvert}(t^{1/2})\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}g\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}t}{\lvert t^{1/2}\rvert_{K}^{2}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(r)\det(t)^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}t.

This reveals the claimed independence of
X^{*}(XX^{*})^{-1/2}
and
XX^{*}
, and the distribution of
X^{*}(XX^{*})^{-1/2}
.

3.

Let the distribution of
X
have
\vol_{\beta mn}
-
density
h\circ\lvert\cdot\rvert_{K}
according to Proposition
5.3
and let
g
be as before, then,

\displaystyle\Erw[g(XX^{*})]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}g(xx^{*})h(\lvert x\rvert_{K})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2^{-m}\int_{\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta}}g(r^{1/2}u^{*}ur^{1/2})h(\lvert r^{1/2}u^{*}\rvert_{K})\,\mathrm{d}u\det(r)^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=2^{-m}\,\frac{\Omega_{n,m;\beta}}{2^{\beta m(m-1)/4}}\int_{\text{Pos}_{m,\beta}}g(r)h(\lvert r^{1/2}\rvert_{\lvert K\rvert})\det(r)^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}r.

This last line yields the claimed density of the distribution of
XX^{*}
. ∎

Remark 5.6
.

1.

Because
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
is both left- and right
-
unitarily invariant, all of Proposition
5.5
is applicable to it.

2.

The classical case
X\sim\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\otimes(m\times n)}
is recovered by noticing its density
(\frac{\beta}{2\pi})^{\beta mn/2}\mathsf{e}^{-\beta\Spur(xx^{*})/2}=(\frac{\beta}{2\pi})^{\beta mn/2}\mathsf{e}^{-\beta\lVert x\rVert_{S_{2}}^{2}/2}
, that is,
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\otimes(m\times n)}
is
\mathbb{B}_{S_{2},\beta}^{m\times n}
-
symmetric. As an aside,
XX^{*}
is Wishart
-
distributed, which fact can also be inferred from part 3 given above.

3.

Part 3 of Proposition
5.5
is always satisfied for
X\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n})
(density
(\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n})^{-1}\Ind_{[0,1]}\circ\lVert\cdot\rVert_{S_{p}}
).

Proposition
5.5
partly overlaps with
[
18
, Theorem 8.2.5]
, as follows from the next statement. But in Proposition
5.5

X
need not have a
\vol_{\beta mn}
-
density in order to ensure almost sure invertibility of
XX^{*}
and to achieve
X^{*}(XX^{*})^{-1/2}\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n,m;\beta})
. (Of course, left or right unitary invariance is not sufficient for invertibility of
XX^{*}
: just take
X:=V\diag(1,\dotsc,1,0)U^{*}
with
U\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta})
and
V\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta})
, for instance.)

In the sequel
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
denotes the symmetric group of degree
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

A
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
-
valued random variable
X
has a two
-
sided unitarily invariant distribution, that is
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}vXu
for all
u\in\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}
and
v\in\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
, if and only if there exist independent random variables
S
,
U
, and
V
such that
S
takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
U\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta})
,
V\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta})
, and
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}V\diag(S)U^{*}
.

Moreover, if
X
has a two
-
sided unitarily invariant distribution, then
S
can be constructed such that
\Wsk[S\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}]=1
, it has exchangeable coordinates and then
(s_{\pi(i)}(X))_{i\leq m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}S
with
\pi\sim\Gleichv(\mathfrak{S}_{m})
independent of
X
, or such that
\Wsk[S\in W^{m}]=1
(recall
W^{m}=\{(x_{i})_{i\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\,:\,0\leq x_{m}\leq\dotsb\leq x_{1}\}
) and then
s(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}S
.

Proof.

\Leftarrow
: This is trivial because
\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{d;\beta})
is a Haar measure and thus
wW\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}W
for
W\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{d;\beta})
and any
w\in\mathrm{U}_{d;\beta}
.

\Rightarrow
: Call
\mu:=\Wsk\circ X^{-1}
; then we have, for any
f\colon\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
measurable and nonneagtive and any
u\in\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}
and
v\in\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
,

\Erw[f(X)]=\Erw[f(vXu)]=\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}f(vxu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Integrate on both sides over the unitary groups to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{m;\beta}\Omega_{n;\beta}}{2^{\beta m(m-1)/4+\beta n(n-1)/4}}\Erw[f(X)]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}f(vxu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(vxu)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

For any
x\in\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
choose a singular value decomposition
x=v_{x}\diag(s(x))u_{x}^{*}
and get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(vxu)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(vv_{x}\diag(s(x))u_{x}^{*}u)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(s(x))u^{*})\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v,

where we have used unitary invariance of the respective Haar measures and also its invariance under adjoining. This leads to (abbreviating the normalization constant by
c
)

\displaystyle\Erw[f(X)]

\displaystyle=c\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(s(x))u^{*})\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=c\int_{W^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(\sigma)u^{*})\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}(\mu\circ s^{-1})(\sigma),

and this yields
X=V\diag(S)U^{*}
with independent
S
,
U
, and
V
such that
U\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta})
,
V\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta})
, and
S\sim\mu\circ s^{-1}=\Wsk{}\circ s(X)^{-1}
, so
s(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}S
.

Now let
P\sim\Gleichv(\mathfrak{S}_{m})
be independent of all other random variables and set
T:=(S_{P(i)})_{i\leq m}
, then
T\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}
almost surely,
T
has exchangeable coordinates, and
T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}(s_{P(i)}(X))_{i\leq m}
, and we also have

\displaystyle\Erw[f(VTU^{*})]

\displaystyle=c\,\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\int_{W^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag((\sigma_{\pi(i)})_{i\leq m})u^{*})\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}(\mu\circ s^{-1})(\sigma)

\displaystyle=c\,\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\int_{W^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(\sigma)u^{*})\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}(\mu\circ s^{-1})(\sigma)

\displaystyle=c\int_{W^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(\sigma)u^{*})\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}(\mu\circ s^{-1})(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Erw[f(X)],

where from the first to second line we have used
\diag((\sigma_{\pi(i)})_{i\leq m})=\tau_{\pi}\diag(\sigma)\diag(\tau_{\pi},I_{n-m})^{\mathsf{T}}
with
\tau_{\pi}=(\delta_{\pi(i),j})_{(i,j)\in n\times n}
and unitary invariance, as in Remark
2.5
. This proves
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}VTU^{*}
.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

Proposition
5.7
tells us that if the distribution of
X
is two
-
sided unitarily invariant, then it is alredy completely determined by the distribution of the singular values of
X
, in the sense that
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}V\diag(s(X))U^{*}
with independent
U\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta})
and
V\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta})
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Any
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
-
symmetric measure on
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
is two
-
sided unitarily invariant.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be a
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
-
symmetric measure on
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
with radial measure
\rho
, and let
u\in\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}
and
v\in\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
. Keeping in mind that singular values, and hence also
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{S_{p}}
and
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
, are two
-
sided unitarily invariant, let
f\colon\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and nonnegative, then we have for any
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}}f(rv\theta u)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}(x)f\Bigl(\frac{rvxu}{\lVert x\rVert_{S_{p}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}(v^{*}xu^{*})f\Bigl(\frac{rx}{\lVert v^{*}xu^{*}\rVert_{S_{p}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}(x)f\Bigl(\frac{rx}{\lVert x\rVert_{S_{p}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}}f(r\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}(\theta).

Now intgerate w.r.t.
\rho
in order to get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}f(vxu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}}f(rv\theta u)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}}f(r\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(r)=\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

and the claim follows.
∎

The next theorem is a major generalization of the probabilistic representation of singular values of random matrices as used in
[
27
]
and
[
28
]
.

Theorem F
.

Let
X
be a
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
-
valued random variable.

1.

X
has a
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
-
symmetric distribution with radial distribution
\rho
if and only if there exist independent random variables
R
,
U
,
V
, and
Y
such that
R\sim\rho
,
U\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta})
,
V\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta})
, and
Y=(Y_{i})_{i\leq m}
is
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-
valued with Lebesgue
-
density

g(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m}):=\frac{1_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}(y)}{Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\lVert y\rVert_{p/2}^{p/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert y_{i}-y_{j}\rvert^{\beta}

(15)

(here
Z_{m,n,p,\beta}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is the appropriate normalization constant, and we interpret
\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\lVert y\rVert_{p/2}^{p/2}}:=\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{\infty,1}^{m}}(y)
for
p=\infty
), and

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R\,V\,\dfrac{\diag\bigl((Y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m}\bigr)}{\lVert Y\rVert_{p/2}^{1/2}}\,U^{*}.

2.

Special cases of 1.:

(a)

X\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n})
iff
R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}W^{1/(\beta mn)}
, where
W\sim\Gleichv([0,1])
is independent of all other random variables.

(b)

X\sim\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
iff
R=1
almost surely.

3.

For the particular value
p=\infty
we have the following additional representations:

(a)

X\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})
if and only if
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}V\diag((Y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m})U^{*}
, where
U
,
V
, and
Y
have the same meanings as in 1.

(b)

X\sim\kappa_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}
if and only if
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}V\diag\bigl((Y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m}\bigr)U^{*}
, where
U
und
V
have the same meanings as in 1., and
Y=(Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{m})^{\mathsf{T}}
is
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-
valued with
Y_{1}=1
almost surely and
(Y_{2},\dotsc,Y_{m})
has Lebesgue
-
density proportional to

(y_{2},\dotsc,y_{m})\mapsto g(1,y_{2},\dotsc,y_{m}).

Proof.

For ease of notation we abbreviate normalization constants (whose value also may change from one occurrence to the other) and set
h(x):=\Ind_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}(x)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\cdot\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert x_{i}^{2}-x_{j}^{2}\rvert^{\beta}
.

1.

\Rightarrow
: From Proposition
5.4
we know
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R\Theta
where
R
and
\Theta
are independent,
R\sim\rho
and
\Theta\sim\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
. Since
\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
is
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
-
symmetric (see Remark
5.2
), from Propositions
5.7
and
5.9
we know
\Theta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}V\diag(S)U^{*}
with independent variables
S
,
U
, and
V
such that
S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}(s_{P(i)}(\Theta))_{i\leq m}
,
U\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta})
, and
V\sim\Gleichv(\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta})
, where
P\sim\Gleichv(\mathfrak{S}_{m})
is independent of all others. Thence it suffices to establish
S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\lVert Y\rVert_{p/2}^{-1/2}(Y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m}
where
Y
has the claimed distribution. We further define
x_{\pi}:=(x_{\pi(i)})_{i\leq m}
for
x\in\mathbb{K}^{m}
and
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}
. So let
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and nonnegative, then

\Erw[f(S)]=\Erw[f(s(\Theta)_{P})]=\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}}f(s(\theta)_{\pi})\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}(\theta);

express the integral over
\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}
as one over
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
and use the positive homogeneity of singular values to get

\Erw[f(S)]=\frac{C}{m!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}f\Bigl(\frac{s(x)_{\pi}}{\lVert x\rVert_{S_{p}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x;

now use Proposition
2.4
, part 2, noting that the integrand already depends only on the singular values of
x
,

\Erw[f(S)]=\frac{C_{1}}{m!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{p,1}^{m}}f\Bigl(\frac{s(\diag(\sigma))_{\pi}}{\lVert\sigma\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)\,h(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma;

as in Remark
2.5
we split
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}=\bigcup_{\pi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\tau_{\pi^{\prime}}W^{m}
(up to null sets), transform
\tau_{\pi^{\prime}}W^{m}
to
W^{m}
while bearing the symmetry of
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{p}
and
h
in mind, and use
s(\diag(\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}))=\sigma
for
\sigma\in W^{m}
, so

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{p,1}^{m}}f\Bigl(\frac{s(\diag(\sigma))_{\pi}}{\lVert\sigma\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)\,h(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\int_{\tau_{\pi^{\prime}}W^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{p,1}^{m}}(\sigma)f\Bigl(\frac{s(\diag(\sigma))_{\pi}}{\lVert\sigma\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)\,h(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\int_{W^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{p,1}^{m}}(\sigma)f\Bigl(\frac{s(\diag(\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}))_{\pi}}{\lVert\sigma\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)\,h(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\int_{W^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{p,1}^{m}}(\sigma)f\Bigl(\frac{\sigma_{\pi}}{\lVert\sigma\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)\,h(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=m!\int_{\tau_{\pi^{-1}}W^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{p,1}^{m}}(\sigma)f\Bigl(\frac{\sigma}{\lVert\sigma\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)\,h(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma;

therefore

\displaystyle\Erw[f(S)]

\displaystyle=C_{1}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\int_{\tau_{\pi^{-1}}W^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{p,1}^{m}}(\sigma)f\Bigl(\frac{\sigma}{\lVert\sigma\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)\,h(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle=C_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{p,1}^{m}}(\sigma)f\Bigl(\frac{\sigma}{\lVert\sigma\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)\,h(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma.

Introduce polar coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and use the homogeneity of
h
to arrive at

\Erw[f(S)]=C_{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{p,1}^{m}}f(\theta)h(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{p,1}^{m}(\theta),

which shows that the distribution of
S
has
\kappa_{p,1}^{m}
-
density proportional to
h
. It remains to prove that
\lVert Y\rVert_{p/2}^{-1/2}(Y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m}
also has
\kappa_{p,1}^{m}
-
density proportional to
h
; so let
f
be as before, then via substitution
x:=(y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m}
and polar integration, and noting that
g
and
h
contain the indicator function
\Ind_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}
,

\displaystyle\Erw\biggl[f\biggl(\frac{(Y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m}}{\lVert Y\rVert_{p/2}^{1/2}}\biggr)\biggr]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}f\biggl(\frac{(y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m}}{\lVert y\rVert_{p/2}^{1/2}}\biggr)g(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=2^{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}f\Bigl(\frac{x}{\lVert x\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)g((x_{i}^{2})_{i\leq m})\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}f\Bigl(\frac{x}{\lVert x\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\lVert x\rVert_{p}^{p}}h(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=C_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{p,1}^{m}}f\Bigl(\frac{r\theta}{\lVert r\theta\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\lVert r\theta\rVert_{p}^{p}}h(r\theta)r^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{p,1}^{m}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=C_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}r^{\beta mn-1}\mathsf{e}^{-\beta nr^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}r\int_{\mathbb{S}_{p,1}^{m}}f(\theta)h(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{p,1}^{m}(\theta),

where for the last step we have invested the homogeneity of
h
; and we have finished.

\Leftarrow
: Because of Proposition
5.4
it suffices to prove
\Theta:=\lVert Y\rVert_{p/2}^{-1/2}\,V\diag((Y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m})U^{*}\sim\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
, which makes sense since
\lVert\Theta\rVert_{S_{p}}=\lVert Y\rVert_{p/2}^{-1/2}\lVert\diag((Y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m})\rVert_{S_{p}}=1
. From the proof of
\Rightarrow
we already know that
\lVert Y\rVert_{p/2}^{-1/2}(Y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m}
has
\kappa_{p,1}^{m}
-
density proportional to
h
. So let
f\colon\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and nonnegative, then, by immediately converting back to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
we get

\displaystyle\Erw[f(\Theta)]

\displaystyle=C\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{p,1}^{m}}f(v\diag(\theta)u^{*})h(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{p,1}^{m}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=C_{1}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{p,1}^{m}}f\Bigl(\frac{v\diag(x)u^{*}}{\lVert x\rVert_{p}}\Bigr)\frac{h(x)}{\lVert x\rVert_{p}^{\beta mn-m}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v.

Use
\lVert x\rVert_{p}=\lVert v\diag(x)u^{*}\rVert_{S_{p}}
and Proposition
2.4
, part 2, in order to revert to
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
, so

\Erw[f(\Theta)]=C_{2}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}f\Bigl(\frac{x}{\lVert x\rVert_{S_{p}}}\Bigr)\lVert x\rVert_{S_{p}}^{m-\beta mn}\,\mathrm{d}x;

finally introduce polar coordinates on
\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}
to achieve

\displaystyle\Erw[f(\Theta)]

\displaystyle=C_{3}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}(r\theta)f\Bigl(\frac{r\theta}{\lVert r\theta\rVert_{S_{p}}}\Bigr)\lVert r\theta\rVert_{S_{p}}^{m-\beta mn}\,r^{\beta mn-1}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{3}}{m}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m,n}}f(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}(\theta),

and this proves the claim.

2.

We refer to Remark
5.2
: a
K
-
symmetric measure equals
\Gleichv(K)
iff
\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho(r)}{\mathrm{d}r}=dr^{d-1}\Ind_{[0,1]}(r)
, and it equals
\kappa_{K}
iff
\rho=\delta_{1}
. Since we know
R\sim\rho
, the claim is immediate for
\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
, and in the case of
\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n})
observe
R^{\beta mn}\sim\Gleichv([0,1])
.

3.

(a)

We start anew from Proposition
2.4
, part 2; let
f\colon\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and nonnegative, then, via the usual substitution
s=(y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m}
,

\displaystyle\Erw[f(X)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}\int_{\mathbb{K}^{m\times n}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}(x)f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=C\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n}}(v\diag(s)u^{*})f(v\diag(s)u^{*})h(s)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=C2^{-m}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{\infty,1}^{m}}(y)f(v\diag((y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m})u^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot h((y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m})\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=C_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag((y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m})u^{*})g(y)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}y,

where recall our convention
\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\lVert y\rVert_{\infty}^{\infty}}=\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{\infty,1}^{m}}(y)
. The last line implies the claim.

(b)

We start from 2.; let
f\colon\mathbb{S}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m,n}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and nonnegative, then,

\displaystyle\Erw[f(X)]

\displaystyle=C\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f\biggl(\frac{v\diag((y_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m})u^{*}}{\lVert y\rVert_{\infty}^{1/2}}\biggr)g(y)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=C_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{\infty,1}^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f\biggl(\frac{v\diag(((r\theta_{i})^{1/2})_{i\leq m})u^{*}}{\lVert r\theta\rVert_{\infty}^{1/2}}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot g(r\theta)r^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{\infty,1}^{m}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=C_{1}\int_{(0,1]}r^{\beta mn/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}r\int_{\mathbb{S}_{\infty,1}^{m}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f\bigl(v\diag((\theta_{i}^{1/2})_{i\leq m})u^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot g(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{\infty,1}^{m}(\theta),

where we have introduced polar coordinates and used the homogeneity of
g
, to wit
g(r\theta)=\Ind_{[0,1]}(r)r^{\beta mn/2-m}\,g(\theta)
. Now essentially
\mathbb{S}_{\infty,1}^{m}\cap\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}
consists of those faces of
[0,1]^{m}
where at least one coordinate equals
1
, and they all are permutations of
\{1\}\times[0,1]^{m-1}
; the matrix
\diag(\theta)
is permuted accordingly, but as we are integrating w.r.t. the Haar
-
measures on
\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}
and
\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}
, the value of the integral over each of the faces is the same; cf. Remark
2.5
for more details. Thus we get

\displaystyle\Erw[f(X)]

\displaystyle=C_{2}\int_{[0,1]^{m-1}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{m;\beta}}\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n;\beta}}f(v\diag(1,\theta_{2}^{1/2},\dotsc,\theta_{m}^{1/2})u^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot g(1,\theta_{2},\dotsc,\theta_{m})\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\theta

and the result follows. ∎

6
Sanov
-
type LDPs for singular values

6.1
Prerequisites

At first glance it may seem an odd choice that Theorem
F
contains a representation for the squares of the singular values instead of the singular values themselves. The main reason is the application of results which lead to large deviations principles.

Having a broad readership in mind, we shall recall the basic definitions from the theory of large deviations principles; more information may be found in textbooks like
[
12
]
,
[
22
]
,
[
43
]
, or the survey article
[
39
]
, which also provides the reader with the current state of the art concerning large deviations theory in geometric functional analysis. In what follows let
E
be a Polish space (a complete separable metrizable topological space) and by
\mathcal{B}_{E}
denote its Borel
\sigma
-
algebra.

Definition 6.1

(rate function)
.

A
rate function
is a lower semicontinuous map
\mathcal{I}\colon E\to[0,\infty]
which is not identical
\infty
. A rate function
\mathcal{I}
is called a
good rate function
iff for any
y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the sublevel set
\mathcal{I}^{-1}([0,y])
is compact.

Definition 6.2

(large deviations principle)
.

A sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
E
-
valued random variables is said to satisfy a
large deviations principle
(LDP) with speed
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and (good) rate function
\mathcal{I}\colon E\to[0,\infty]
, iff
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}s_{n}=\infty
,
\mathcal{I}
is a (good) rate function in the sense of Definition
6.1
, and for any
A\in\mathcal{B}_{E}
the following chain of inequalities holds true,

-\inf\bigl(\mathcal{I}(A^{\circ})\bigr)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{s_{n}}\log\Wsk[\xi_{n}\in A]\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{s_{n}}\log\Wsk[\xi_{n}\in A]\leq-\inf\bigl(\mathcal{I}(\overline{A})\bigr),

where
A^{\circ}
and
\overline{A}
denote the interior and closure of
A
resp.

Definition 6.3

(exponentially tight)
.

A sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
E
-
valued random variables is
exponentially tight
with speed
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
iff
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is as in Definition
6.2
and there holds true,

\inf_{C}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{s_{n}}\log\Wsk[\xi_{n}\notin C]=-\infty,

where
C
ranges over all compact subsets of
E
.

There are several tools for proving the existence of an LDP; one which starts form first principles is the following.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a basis of the topology on
E
. Let
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of
E
-
valued random variables and let
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}s_{n}=\infty
. If there exists a function
\mathcal{I}\colon E\to[0,\infty]
such that, for all
a\in E
,

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\in\mathcal{T}\\
a\in U\end{subarray}}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{s_{n}}\log\Wsk[\xi_{n}\in U]=-\mathcal{I}(a)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\in\mathcal{T}\\
a\in U\end{subarray}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{s_{n}}\log\Wsk[\xi_{n}\in U],

and if in addition
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is exponentially tight with speed
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, then
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies a large deviations principle with speed
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and good rate function
\mathcal{I}
.

A very useful result to transform an already existing LDP on
E
to one on another Polish space
F
is the following.

Proposition 6.5

(contraction principle)
.

Let
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of
E
-
valued random variables that satisfies a large deviations principle with speed
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and good rate function
\mathcal{I}\colon E\to[0,\infty]
, and let
f\colon E\to F
be a continuous map. Then the sequence
(f(\xi_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies a large deviations principle in
F
with the same speed
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and good rate function
\mathcal{J}\colon F\to[0,\infty]
given by

\mathcal{J}(y):=\inf_{x\in f^{-1}\{y\}}\mathcal{I}(x),

where
\inf(\emptyset):=\infty
.

Given a Polish space
E
, define
\mathcal{M}_{1}(E)
to be the set of all probability measures on
E
. The standard topology on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(E)
is the
weak topology,
that is, the coarsest topology such that the functional

\mathcal{M}_{1}(E)\ni\mu\mapsto\int_{E}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu

is continuous for any given
f\in C_{\text{b}}(E)
, the set of all bounded continuous real
-
valued functions on
E
. It follows that for
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(E)
a basis of open neighborhoods consists of sets
\mathcal{O}_{f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d};\varepsilon}
, where

\mathcal{O}_{f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d};\varepsilon}:=\biggl\{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(E)\,:\,\forall i\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}\colon\biggl\lvert\int_{E}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu-\int_{E}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\biggr\rvert<\varepsilon\biggr\},

with
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, and
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d}\in C_{\text{b}}(E)
.

The weak topology on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(E)
may be metrized, for instance with the Lévy
–
Prokhorov metric or with the bounded Lipschitz metric
[
13
, Chapter 11.3]
, and endowed with such a metric
\mathcal{M}_{1}(E)
becomes a Polish space itself. For this reason it makes sense to consider LDPs for sequences of random probability measures, and in the sequel any set of probability measures is to be understood as endowed with the weak topology.

6.2
Results

For any array
((X_{n,i})_{i\leq m(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of (say) real
-
valued random variables the empirical measures
\mu_{n}:=\frac{1}{m(n)}\sum_{i=1}^{m(n)}\delta_{X_{n,i}}
(where
\delta_{a}
denotes the Dirac measure supported on
a
) form a sequence of random probability measures, and the following Theorems
G
and
H
treat the situation when the input array consists of the (scaled) singular values of a suitably distributed random matrix.

Theorem G

(
p<\infty
)
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
, let
m\in\mathbb{N}
depend on
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
m\leq n
and
c:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{m}{n}\in(0,1]
exists, and let
(X^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of random variables such that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
either
X^{(n)}\sim\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
or
X^{(n)}\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n})
. Then the sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of empirical measures
\mu_{n}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{1/p}s_{i}(X^{(n)})}
satisfies a large deviations principle on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
with speed
\beta mn
and good rate function
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}\colon\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\to[0,\infty]
given by

\mathcal{I}_{c,p}(\mu):=\begin{cases}-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x^{2}-y^{2}\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-(1-c)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\frac{\log(\mathsf{e}p)}{p}+B_{c,p},&\text{if }m_{p}(\mu)\leq 1,\\
\infty&\text{else,}\end{cases}

which possesses a unique global mimimizer
\mu_{c,p}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
with compact support and some Lebesgue density. Here

B_{c,p}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log(Z_{m,n,p,\beta})\in\mathbb{R},

with
Z_{m,n,p,\beta}
as in Theorem
F
, and
m_{p}(\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}x^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
.

Remark 6.6
.

Unfortunately neither the minimizer
\mu_{c,p}
nor the constant
B_{c,p}
are explicitly known for most values of
c
and
p
. The exceptions are
c=1
for arbitrary
p\in(0,\infty)
since this corresponds to the quadratic case already solved in
[
28
]
, and
p=2
for arbitrary
c\in(0,1]
since here the theory for Wishart matrices is applicable; further information for the latter case is given in Remark
6.25
below; and for selected values of
c
the density of
\mu_{c,2}
is plotted in Figure
1
.

Figure 1:
Plot of the density of
\mu_{c,2}
for
c=0.1
(dash
-
dotted),
c=0.5
(dashed), and
c=1
(solid).

Remark 6.7
.

Recently Kaufmann and Thäle
[
29
]
have proved large deviations principles for the empirical spectral measures of random square matrices whose distribution is a weighted mixture of
\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{n\times n}
and
\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{n\times n})
in the spirit of Barthe, Guédon, Mendelson, and Naor
[
6
]
. Since those measures are
\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{n\times n}
-
symmetric they allow a representation according to Theorem
F
with an appropriate radial component
R_{n}
. According to the outline given in Section
6.3
below,
R_{n}
only enters in the last step, and as is seen in the proof of Proposition
6.27
an LDP for
(R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
needs to be established, which is done in
[
29
, Lemma 6.6]
. We do not go any further into this here.

Theorem H

(
p=\infty
)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
depend on
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
m\leq n
and
c:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{m}{n}\in(0,1]
exists, and let
(X^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of random variables such that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
X^{(n)}\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})
. Then the sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of empirical measures
\mu_{n}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{s_{i}(X^{(n)})}
satisfies a large deviations principle on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
with speed
\beta mn
and good rate function
\mathcal{I}_{c,\infty}\colon\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\to[0,\infty]
given by

\mathcal{I}_{c,\infty}(\mu):=\begin{cases}-\frac{c}{2}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\log\lvert x^{2}-y^{2}\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-(1-c)\int_{[0,1]}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+B_{c,\infty},&\text{if }\supp(\mu)\subset[0,1],\\
\infty&\text{else,}\end{cases}

which possesses a unique global mimimizer
\mu_{c,\infty}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
given by its Lebesgue density,

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{c,\infty}(x)}{\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{1+c}{c\pi x}\,\frac{\bigl(x^{2}-(\frac{1-c}{1+c})^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}}{(1-x^{2})^{1/2}}\Ind_{\bigl[\frac{1-c}{1+c},1\bigr]}(x).

Here

B_{c,\infty}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log(Z_{m,n,\infty,\beta})=\frac{c}{2}\log(c)-\frac{(1-c)^{2}}{4c}\log(1-c)-\frac{(1+c)^{2}}{4c}\log(1+c)

with
Z_{m,n,\infty,\beta}
as in Theorem
F
.

Remark 6.8
.

In the extreme case
c=1
,
\mu_{1,\infty}
has the density

x\mapsto\frac{2}{\pi}\,(1-x^{2})^{-1/2}\Ind_{[0,1]}(x)

which corresponds to the absolute arcsine distribution, and this is consistent with
[
28
]
. For selected values of
c
the density of
\mu_{c,\infty}
is plotted in Figure
2
.

Figure 2:
Plot of the density of
\mu_{c,\infty}
for
c=0.2
(dash
-
dotted),
c=0.5
(dashed), and
c=1
(solid).

The following statement is a strong law of large numbers for the empirical measures of singular values and a corollary of the large deviations principles of Theorems
G
and
H
and parallels the results
[
28
, Corollaries 1.4, 1.6]
.

Proposition 6.9
.

Under the premises of Theorems
G
or
H
we have, w.r.t. the weak topology,

(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\xrightarrow{\text{a.s.}}\mu_{c,p}.

Proof.

Let
d_{\text{w}}
be some metric that induces the weak topology on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
. Via the Borel
–
Cantelli lemma it suffices to show, for any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Wsk[d_{\text{w}}(\mu_{n},\mu_{c,p})\geq\varepsilon]<\infty.

Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, then Theorem
G
or
H
, resp., yields

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk[d_{\text{w}}(\mu_{n},\mu_{c,p})\geq\varepsilon]\leq-\inf\{\mathcal{I}_{c,p}(\nu)\,:\,d_{\text{w}}(\nu,\mu_{c,p})\geq\varepsilon\}=:-M.

We will argue
M>0
below; once we have established that, there follows

\Wsk[d_{\text{w}}(\mu_{n},\mu_{c,p})\geq\varepsilon]\leq\mathsf{e}^{-M\beta mn/2}

for all but finitely many
n
, and clearly the majorant is summable.

In order to establish
M>0
we may assume
M<\infty
, else there is nothing to prove. By definition of the infimum there exists some
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
with
d_{\text{w}}(\nu,\mu_{c,p})\geq\varepsilon
such that
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}(\nu)\leq M+1
. Because
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}
is a good rate function the sublevel set
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}^{-1}\bigl([0,M+1]\bigr)
is compact and therefore
C:=\{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\,:\,d_{\text{w}}(\nu,\mu_{c,p})\geq\varepsilon\wedge\mathcal{I}_{c,p}(\nu)\leq M+1\}
is nonempty and compact, thus
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}
attains its miminmum on
C
in some
\nu_{\varepsilon}\in C
, and by construction we get
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}(\nu_{\varepsilon})=M
. But we also know
d_{\text{w}}(\nu_{\varepsilon},\mu_{c,p})\geq\varepsilon
, in particular
\nu_{\varepsilon}\neq\mu_{c,p}
, and as
\mu_{c,p}
is the unique point where
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}
attains its global minimum
0
, this implies
M=\mathcal{I}_{c,p}(\nu_{\varepsilon})>0
.
∎

Although we are not able to give a closed expression for
\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
for
p\notin\{2,\infty\}
, we are still able to obtain some asymptotics of the volume radius, as stated below.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
, consider
m
as depending on
n
such that
c:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{m}{n}\in(0,1]
exists, and let
B_{c,p}
be as in Theorem
G
. Then we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}(\beta n)^{1/2+1/p}(\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n})^{1/(\beta mn)}=(2\pi\mathsf{e}^{3/2})^{1/2}\Bigl(\frac{\mathsf{e}p}{c}\Bigr)^{1/p}\mathsf{e}^{B_{c,p}}\,(1-c)^{(1-c)^{2}/(4c)}\,c^{-c/4},

where in the case
c=1
we interpret
(1-c)^{(1-c)^{2}}:=1
.

Proof.

Refer to Proposition
2.4
, part 2, and denote
c_{n}:=\frac{2^{\beta m/2}\,\Omega_{m;\beta}\Omega_{n;\beta}}{2^{\beta mn/2}\,m!\Omega_{1;\beta}^{m}\Omega_{n-m;\beta}}
. Then because of
\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}}=\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{p,1}^{m}}{}\circ s
we have

\displaystyle\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}

\displaystyle=c_{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{p,1}^{m}\cap\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert x_{i}^{2}-x_{j}^{2}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2^{-m}\,c_{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{p/2,\beta}^{m}\cap\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert x_{i}-x_{j}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}x,

where we have transformed
y_{i}=x_{i}^{2}
and immediately renamed
y_{i}
as
x_{i}
. As the integrand is positive
-
homogeneous of degree
\frac{\beta mn}{2}-m
, via polar integration the last expression equals

\displaystyle\omega_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}

\displaystyle=2^{-m}\,c_{n}\,\frac{n^{\beta mn/p}}{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\beta mn}{p}+1\bigr)}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\lVert x\rVert_{p/2}^{p/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert x_{i}-x_{j}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2^{-m}\,c_{n}\,\frac{n^{\beta mn/p}}{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\beta mn}{p}+1\bigr)}\,Z_{m,n,p,\beta}.

Stirling’s formula implies
\Gamma(\frac{\beta mn}{p}+1)^{1/(\beta mn)}=(\frac{\beta mn}{\mathsf{e}p})^{1/p}(1+\smallO(1))
, and also
(2^{-m})^{1/(\beta mn)}=2^{-1/(\beta n)}=1+\smallO(1)
. Furthermore Proposition
6.13
tells us

\lim_{n\to\infty}Z_{m,n,p,\beta}^{1/(\beta mn)}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\exp\Bigl(\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log Z_{m,n,p,\beta}\Bigr)=\mathsf{e}^{B_{c,p}}.

It remains to determine the asymptotics of
c_{n}^{1/(\beta mn)}
. For that substitute the explicit expression for
\Omega_{n;\beta}
given in (
1
) and use the asymptotics stated in Lemma
2.2
to arrive at (skipping the details here)

c_{n}^{1/(\beta mn)}=(2\pi\mathsf{e}^{3/2})^{1/2}\,(1-c)^{(1-c)^{2}/(4c)}\,c^{-c/4}\,(\beta n)^{-1/2}\,(1+\smallO(1)).

Putting things together yields the result.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

In the new preprint
[
37
]
Naor shows the asymptotics

(\omega_{S_{p},1}^{m\times n})^{1/(mn)}\asymp\min\{m,n\}^{-1/p}\max\{m,n\}^{-1/2},

where
\asymp
denotes asymptotic equivalence, i.e., boundedness from above and from below up to dimension
-
independent constant factors. If those bounds were known, they could be used together with Proposition
6.10
to get bounds for
B_{c,p}
, and vice versa.

6.3
Proof of Theorem
G

The proof of Theorem
G
parallels that of
[
28
, Theorem 1.1]
and accordingly proceeds in several steps. As already mentioned in the overview (Section
1.1

ad fin.
), the adaption amounts to more than merely substituting
m
for
n
in the right places, because the now dimension
-
dependent term
\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}
in the density (
15
) prompts special treatment involving delicate estimations; all these turn up in Step 2 below. Apart from that, while going through the original paper
[
28
]
carefully, the authors discovered a few technical gaps and inaccuracies there; we will refer to them in the appropriate places in our proof and provide amendments (which of course have to be readapted in the Hermitian case).

For the convenience of the reader we repeat the outline of the proof in
[
28
]
here, together with an addition for the present Theorem
G
(Step 4):

1.

Establish an LDP for
(\nu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, where
\nu_{n}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{Y_{i}^{(n)}}
and
Y^{(n)}=(Y_{i}^{(n)})_{i\leq m}
has density (
15
).

2.

Establish an LDP for
\bigl((\nu_{n},m_{p/2}(\nu_{n}))\bigr)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
\nu_{n}
is as above.

3.

Use the contraction principle to prove an LDP for
(\mu_{n}^{2})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\mu_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{2/p}Y_{i}^{(n)}/\lVert Y^{(n)}\rVert_{p/2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{2/p}s_{i}(X^{(n)})^{2}}
for the case of
X^{(n)}\sim\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
.

4.

Contract again to obtain an LDP for
(\mu_{n}^{\kappa})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\mu_{n}^{\kappa}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{1/p}s_{i}(X^{(n)})}
, still for
X^{(n)}\sim\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
.

5.

Contract one last time to get an LDP for
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
with
\mu_{n}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{1/p}s_{i}(X^{(n)})}
where
X^{(n)}\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{p}}^{m\times n})
.

The main justification for this procedure are Proposition
5.7
and Theorem
F
which together yield

s(X^{(n)})_{\pi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}R\,\frac{((Y_{i}^{(n)})^{1/2})}{\lVert Y^{(n)}\rVert_{p/2}^{1/2}},

where
\pi\sim\Gleichv(\mathfrak{S}_{m})
is independent of
X^{(n)}
, and therewith

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{1/p}s_{i}(X^{(n)})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{1/p}R((Y_{i}^{(n)})^{1/2})_{i\leq m}/\lVert Y^{(n)}\rVert_{p/2}^{1/2}};

also note

m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}y^{p/2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{n}(y)=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(Y_{i}^{(n)})^{p/2}=\frac{\lVert Y^{(n)}\rVert_{p/2}^{p/2}}{m}.

That
\pi
indeed is immaterial is argued by the result below.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
E
be a topological space and
d\in\mathbb{N}
, let
S=(S_{i})_{i\leq d}
be an
E^{d}
-
valued random variable and let
\pi\sim\Gleichv(\mathfrak{S}_{d})
be independent of
S
. Then

\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{S_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{S_{\pi(i)}}.

Proof.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{M}_{1}(E)\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and nonnegative, then

\displaystyle\Erw\biggl[f\biggl(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{S_{\pi(i)}}\biggr)\biggr]

\displaystyle=\int_{E^{d}}\frac{1}{d!}\sum_{\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}}f\biggl(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{s_{\tau(i)}}\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}(\Wsk\circ S^{-1})(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{E^{d}}\frac{1}{d!}\sum_{\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}}f\biggl(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{s_{i}}\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}(\Wsk\circ S^{-1})(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{E^{d}}f\biggl(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{s_{i}}\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}(\Wsk\circ S^{-1})(s)=\Erw\biggl[f\biggl(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{S_{i}}\biggr)\biggr],

where we have invested the elementary fact that addition is commutative and thus
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{s_{\tau(i)}}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{s_{i}}
for any
s\in E^{d}
and
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
.
∎

In order to keep the wording simple, in the sequel we are going to work under the premises of Theorem
G
, that is,
p\in(0,\infty)
,
m
varies with
n
such that
m\leq n
and
c:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{m}{n}\in(0,1]
exists, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}

Y^{(n)}=(Y_{i}^{(n)})_{i\leq m}
is a random vector with density (
15
), and
\nu_{n}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{Y_{i}^{(n)}}
.

Step 1

This is dealt with readily using a result from
[
19
]
.

Proposition 6.13
.

The limit
B_{c,p}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log(Z_{m,n,p,\beta})
exists and is finite and does not depend on
\beta
, and the sequence
(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of empirical measures satisfies a large deviations principle on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
at speed
\beta mn
with good rate function
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}\colon\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\to[0,\infty]
given by

\mathcal{J}_{c,p}(\mu):=-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\Bigl(x^{p/2}-\frac{1-c}{2}\log(x)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+B_{c,p},

which has a unique global minimizer
\nu_{c,p}
with compact support and a Lebesgue density.

Proof.

This follows almost immediately from
[
19
, Theorem 5.5.1]
. Said Theorem implies the existence of
B_{\beta}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\log(Z_{m,n,p,\beta})
, which might depend on
\beta
somehow, and
(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies an LDP at speed
n^{2}
with good rate function
J_{c,p,\beta}=\beta cJ_{c,p}+B_{\beta}
where

J_{c,p}(\mu)=-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\Bigl(x^{p/2}-\frac{1-c}{2}\log(x)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

which no longer depends on
\beta
; hence a measure
\mu
minimizes
J_{c,p,\beta}
iff it minimizes
J_{c,p}
, and therefore the minimizer
\nu_{c,p}
does not depend on
\beta
. Being the rate function of an LDP,
J_{c,p,\beta}
satisfies
J_{c,p,\beta}(\nu_{c,p})=0
, hence
B_{\beta}=-\beta cJ_{c,p}(\nu_{c,p})
, and
B_{c,p}:=-J_{c,p}(\nu_{c,p})
does not depend on
\beta
and it satisfies

B_{c,p}=\frac{1}{\beta c}\,B_{\beta}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{n}{m}\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta n^{2}}\log(Z_{m,n,p,\beta})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log(Z_{m,n,p,\beta}).

Writing out the defining inequalities of the LDP, we have, for any measurable set
A
,

-\inf(J_{c,p,\beta}(A^{\circ}))\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\log\Wsk[\nu_{n}\in A]\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\log\Wsk[\nu_{n}\in A]\leq-\inf(J_{c,p,\beta}(\overline{A}));

and since
J_{c,p,\beta}=\beta cJ_{c,p}+\beta cB_{c,p}=\beta c\mathcal{J}_{c,p}
and
\frac{1}{\beta c}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{n}{\beta m}
, we can divide the LDP by
\beta c
to get

-\inf(\mathcal{J}_{c,p}(A^{\circ}))\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk[\nu_{n}\in A]\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk[\nu_{n}\in A]\leq-\inf(\mathcal{J}_{c,p}(\overline{A})),

so
(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the claimed LDP. The compactness of the support of the minimizer
\nu_{c,p}
is argued by
[
19
, Theorem 5.3.3]
, and the existence of a Lebesgue density by
[
45
, Theorem IV.2.5]
as the following argument makes clear: up to a constant factor the rate function
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}
can be considered an energy functional with external potential
Q(x):=\frac{1}{c}x^{p/2}-\frac{1-c}{2c}\log(x)
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
(
\infty
else), or weight function
w(x)=\mathsf{e}^{-Q(x)}=x^{(1-c)/(2c)}\,\mathsf{e}^{-x^{p/2}/c}
(
0
on
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
); then
w
is positive on the interval
I=\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and infinitely often differentiable there, and so all premises of
[
45
, Theorem IV.2.5]
are met.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

There seem to be no special results available for weight functions of the form
w(x)=x^{\theta}\mathsf{e}^{-\lambda x^{p/2}}\Ind_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}(x)
unless
p=2
(for which see Remark
6.25
below). The general theory set out in
[
45
]
tells us that
\supp(\nu_{c,p})=[a,b]
with
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, that
a=0
only for
c=1
, and else
a
and
b
satisfy the simultaneous equations

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\Bigl(\frac{px^{p/2-1}}{2c}-\frac{1-c}{2cx}\Bigr)(x-a)^{-1/2}(b-x)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\pi,

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\Bigl(\frac{px^{p/2-1}}{2c}-\frac{1-c}{2cx}\Bigr)(x-a)^{1/2}(b-x)^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\pi.

The integrals can be expressed in terms of the Gaussian hypergeometric functions, but that does not help any further. We also cannot determine the density of
\nu_{c,p}
as the only pertinent result
[
45
, Theorem IV.3.2]
cannot be applied: upon transferring
[a,b]
to
[0,1]
via an affine map the conditions on the external field are violated.

Step 2

We repeat the observation made in
[
28
]
that the moment map
m_{p/2}
is not continuous w.r.t. the weak topology, thus contraction is not applicable, and therefore an LDP for
\bigl((\nu_{n},m_{p/2}(\nu_{n}))\bigr)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
must be proved directly.

Proposition 6.15
.

The sequence of pairs
\bigl((\nu_{n},m_{p/2}(\nu_{n}))\bigr)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies an LDP on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with speed
\beta mn
and good rate function
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}\colon\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to[0,\infty]
given by

\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu,M)=\begin{cases}-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-\frac{1-c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+M+B_{c,p}&\text{if }m_{p/2}(\mu)\leq M,\\
\infty&\text{if }m_{p/2}(\mu)>M,\end{cases}

which attains its global minimum precisely at
(\nu_{c,p},m_{p/2}(\nu_{c,p}))
, where
\nu_{c,p}
is the same as in Proposition
6.13
.

Seeing that the proof of the corresponding
[
28
, Theorem 4.1]
is rather lengthy, here we do not spell out all details anew but restrict ourselves mostly to the adaptions necessitated by the new term
\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}
in density (
15
) for
m\neq n
.

[
28
, Lemma 4.2]
is carried over with the obvious modifications (i.e., replace
\mathbb{R}
by
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
p
by
\frac{p}{2}
) since it does not use the density of
Y^{(n)}
.

[
28
, Lemma 4.4]
too can be adopted with the obvious modifications for the same reason as before; in particular the definition of
D_{n}
now reads

D_{n}:=\bigl(m^{2/p}(M-m_{p/2}(\mu))^{2/p}-m^{2/p-2},m^{2/p}(M-m_{p/2}(\mu))^{2/p}\bigr).

But
[
28
, Lemma 4.5]
needs to be modified to the form given below.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
be supported on an interval
[a,b]
with
0\leq a<b<\infty
and let
M\in(m_{p/2}(\mu),\infty)
; assume that
\mu
has Lebesgue density
h
which is continuous on
[a,b]
and satisfies
\inf_{x\in[a,b]}h(x)>0
. Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d}\in C_{\text{b}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
and
\delta,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Then

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\bigl[\nu_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon,d}(\mu)\wedge\bigl\lvert m_{p/2}(\nu_{n}^{\prime})-m_{p/2}(\mu)\bigr\rvert<\delta\wedge Y_{m}^{(n)}\in D_{n}\bigr]\\
\geq\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)+\frac{1-c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)-M-B_{c,p},

where
\nu_{n}^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\delta_{Y_{i}^{(n)}}
.

Proof.

Differently from
[
28
]
, the objects
a_{i}^{(m-1)}
,
b_{i}^{(m-1)}
and
\Delta_{m-1}
now depend rather on
m
than on
n
. Since
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we additionally have
\xi_{i}^{(m-1)}=b_{i}^{(m-1)}
. This yields

\displaystyle\Wsk\bigl[

\displaystyle\nu_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon,d}(\mu)\wedge\bigl\lvert m_{p/2}(\nu_{n}^{\prime})-m_{p/2}(\mu)\bigr\rvert<\delta\wedge Y_{m}^{(n)}\in D_{n}\bigr]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}\int_{\Delta_{m-1}\times D_{n}}\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\lVert y\rVert_{p/2}^{p/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert y_{j}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}\int_{\Delta_{m-1}\times D_{n}}\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}y_{i}^{p/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m-1}\lvert y_{j}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}

\displaystyle\mspace{100mu}\cdot\mathsf{e}^{-\beta ny_{m}^{p/2}}y_{m}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\lvert y_{m}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}\int_{\Delta_{m-1}\times D_{n}}\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}(b_{i}^{(m-1)})^{p/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}(\eta_{i}^{(m-1)})^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}

\displaystyle\mspace{100mu}\cdot\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m-1}\lvert a_{j}^{(m-1)}-b_{i}^{(m-1)}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathsf{e}^{-\beta ny_{m}^{p/2}}y_{m}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\lvert y_{m}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}\,\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}(b_{i}^{(m-1)})^{p/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}(\eta_{i}^{(m-1)})^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m-1}\lvert a_{j}^{(m-1)}-b_{i}^{(m-1)}\rvert^{\beta}

\displaystyle\mspace{100mu}\cdot\mathsf{e}^{-\beta nm(M-m_{p/2}(\mu))}\eta_{m}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\vol_{m}(\Delta_{m-1}\times D_{n}),

where

\eta_{i}^{(m-1)}:=\begin{cases}a_{i}^{(m-1)}&\text{if }\frac{\beta(n-m+1)}{2}\geq 1,\\
b_{i}^{(m-1)}&\text{if }\frac{\beta(n-m+1)}{2}<1\end{cases}

and

\eta_{m}:=\begin{cases}\inf(D_{n})=m^{2/p}\bigl(M-m_{p/2}(\mu)\bigr)^{2/p}-m^{2/p-2}&\text{if }\frac{\beta(n-m+1)}{2}\geq 1,\\
\sup(D_{n})=m^{2/p}\bigl(M-m_{p/2}(\mu)\bigr)^{2/p}&\text{if }\frac{\beta(n-m+1)}{2}<1.\end{cases}

(In the case
c<1
only the first case occurs for all
n
large enough.) Now we use
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log(Z_{m,n,p,\beta})=B_{c,p}
by Proposition
6.13
, also

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}(b_{i}^{(m-1)})^{p/2}=m_{p/2}(\mu),

and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}{\beta mn}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\log(\eta_{i}^{(m-1)})=\frac{1-c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

as well as

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{mn}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq m-1}\log\lvert a_{j}^{(m-1)}-b_{i}^{(m-1)}\rvert\geq\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y);

there is also

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}{\beta mn}\log(\eta_{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{1-c}{2}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{m}\log\bigl(m^{2/p}\bigl(M-m_{p/2}(\mu)\bigr)^{2/p}-r(n)m^{2/p-2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-c}{2}\lim_{n\to\infty}\Bigl(\frac{2\log(m)}{pm}+\frac{1}{m}\log\Bigl(\bigl(M-m_{p/2}(\mu)\bigr)^{2/p}-\frac{r(n)}{m^{2}}\Bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=0,

where
r(n)\in\{0,1\}
according to the definition of
\eta_{m}
. Finally we know
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log(\vol_{m}(\Delta_{m}\times D_{n}))\geq 0
and hence obtain

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\bigl[

\displaystyle\nu_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon,d}(\mu)\wedge\bigl\lvert m_{p/2}(\nu_{n}^{\prime})-m_{p/2}(\mu)\bigr\rvert<\delta\wedge Y_{m}^{(n)}\in D_{n}\bigr]

\displaystyle\geq-B_{c,p}-m_{p/2}(\mu)+\frac{1-c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-(M-m_{p/2}(\mu)),

which equals the claimed expression.
∎

The proof of
[
28
, Lemma 4.6]
could in principle be adopted without much comment, but for the present article the authors have decided to give a detailed proof, for two reasons: firstly, it is not at all clear that an approximating sequence of measures
(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
as postulated exists indeed, because in contrast to
[
28
]
and to
[
19
]
as referenced therein, our candidate for the rate function,
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}
, contains the additional term
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
; moreover we work with probability measures on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, not
\mathbb{R}
, so it is not immediately clear how the approximating measures are to be constructed, and again neither
[
28
]
nor
[
19
]
provide any details. Secondly, there is an error of sign in
[
28
]
: the display before the line ‘This standard fact can be verified (…)’ must read ‘
\limsup_{k\to\infty}\bigl(-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ldots\bigr)\leq-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ldots
,’ and the reference to
[
19
, p. 214]
(read: ‘p. 216’) does not seem directly applicable.

In the following few lemmas we attempt a careful proof of all partial results omitted or only hinted at, and mentioned above. The first one deals with the concavity of the free entropy under convolution, mentioned in
[
19
, p. 216]
.

Lemma 6.17
.

Define the functional
\Sigma\colon\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{C})\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}
by
\Sigma(\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)
, wherever the integral is defined. Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{C})
be compactly supported, then,

\Sigma(\nu*\mu)\geq\Sigma(\mu).

Via trivial extension, the statement remains true for
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
, or for
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
and
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\nu*\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.

Proof.

We proceed in two steps: first assume
\nu
to be discrete with finite support, then approximate arbitrary
\nu
by discrete measures.

Step 1,
\nu
discrete.
Assume we can write

\nu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}

with some
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
, and
a_{1},\dotsc,a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=1
. By the definition of convolution we have, for any
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{C}}
,

\displaystyle(\nu*\mu)(A)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}1_{A}(x+y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu(A-x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mu(A-x_{i}),

that means,

\nu*\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mu_{x_{i}},

where we have defined ad hoc
\mu_{x_{i}}\colon A\mapsto\mu(A-x_{i})
. Since
\Sigma
is concave by
[
19
, Proposition 5.3.2]
, this readily yields

\Sigma(\nu*\mu)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\Sigma(\mu_{x_{i}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\Sigma(\mu)=\Sigma(\mu),

where we additionally have used that
\Sigma
is invariant under translation, which follows from the fact that its definition depends only on the distance
\lvert x-y\rvert
.

Step 2,
\nu
arbitrary.
Since
\mathbb{C}
is Polish, so is
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{C})
; specifically, a dense subset is given by convex combinations of Dirac measures, that is, discrete measures with finite support. So choose an approximating sequence
(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of such discrete measures converging to
\nu
. First we prove
(\nu_{n}*\mu)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\to\nu*\mu
which we do with Lévy’s continuity theorem; so let
\varphi_{\rho}
denote the characteristic function of a measure
\rho
,
3
3

3

For measures
\rho\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{C})
this is defined via identification
\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}
, i.e.,
\varphi_{\rho}(t):=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mathsf{e}^{\mathsf{i}\Re(\overline{t}z)}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(z)
.
then we know
(\varphi_{\nu_{n}}(t))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\to\varphi_{\nu}(t)
for every
t\in\mathbb{C}
, therefore also

\varphi_{\nu_{n}*\mu}(t)=\varphi_{\nu_{n}}(t)\varphi_{\mu}(t)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\varphi_{\nu}(t)\varphi_{\mu}(t)=\varphi_{\nu*\mu}(t)

for every
t\in\mathbb{C}
. Again by
[
19
, Proposition 5.3.2]
,
\Sigma
is upper semicontinuous, so together with Step 1 we get

\Sigma(\nu*\mu)\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Sigma(\nu_{n}*\mu)\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Sigma(\mu)=\Sigma(\mu).\qed

The next lemma establishes the existence of a “good approximating sequence” for
\mu
such that Lemma
6.16
can be applied. In order to abbreviate notation somewhat we introduce the kernel function

F\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\to(-\infty,\infty],\quad(x,y)\mapsto-c\log\lvert x-y\rvert-\frac{1-c}{2}\log(xy);

then we can rewrite

-c\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-(1-c)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y),

whenever the integrals are defined.

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
with
\mu(\{0\})=0
,
m_{p/2}(\mu)<\infty
, and
