\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}<\infty
. Then there exists a sequence
(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
such that,

(i)

(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\to\mu
(weakly),

(ii)

(m_{p/2}(\mu_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\to m_{p/2}(\mu)
,

(iii)

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}^{\otimes 2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}
, and

(iv)

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mu_{k}
is supported on a compact interval
[a_{k},b_{k}]
with
0<a_{k}<b_{k}<\infty
and has a Lebesgue density
h_{k}
which is continuous on
[a_{k},b_{k}]
and satisfies
\min h_{k}([a_{k},b_{k}])>0
.

Proof.

The construction of
(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
proceeds in three steps, where in each step
\mu
is assumed to behave closer to the desired properties; the steps are going to be pasted together via triangle inequality. We will follow the course sketched in
[
19
, p. 216]
.

Step 1.
Make no further assumptions on
\mu
, and construct
\mu_{k}
to have compact support within
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. To that end define, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\geq k_{0}
,

\mu_{k}\colon\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\to\mathbb{R},\quad A\mapsto\frac{\mu(A\cap[\frac{1}{k},k])}{\mu([\frac{1}{k},k])},

where
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
is chosen such that
\mu([\frac{1}{k},k])>0
for all
k\geq k_{0}
; obviously this is possible since
\mu([\frac{1}{k},k])\to\mu((0,\infty))=1
as
k\to\infty
where we have needed
\mu(\{0\})=0
. Now for any
k\geq k_{0}
,
\mu_{k}
has support contained in
[\frac{1}{k},k]
, and it remains to check conditions (i)–(iii).

Ad (i).
We already have pointed out
\mu([\frac{1}{k},k])\to 1
; similarly, for any
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
, since
(A\cap[\frac{1}{k},k])_{k\geq k_{0}}
is increasing we get
\mu(A\cap[\frac{1}{k},k])\to\mu(A\cap(0,\infty))=\mu(A)
and therefore,

(\mu_{k}(A))_{k\geq k_{0}}\to\mu(A),

so we even have strong convergence of
(\mu_{k})_{k\geq k_{0}}
to
\mu
.

Ad (ii).
For any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
x^{p/2}\Ind_{[1/k,k]}(x)
is nonnegative and monotonically increasing with limit
x^{p/2}
, hence monotone convergence yields,

m_{p/2}(\mu_{k})=\frac{1}{\mu([\frac{1}{k},k])}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}x^{p/2}\Ind_{[1/k,k]}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\frac{1}{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}x^{p/2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=m_{p/2}(\mu).

Ad (iii).
Similarly to (ii) we have
F\Ind_{[1/k,k]^{2}}\to F

\mu^{\otimes 2}
-
almost everywhere; also, the negative part of
F
can be bounded from below by
-C(x^{p/2}+y^{p/2}+x^{p/2}\,y^{p/2})
with some
C>0
, which is
\mu^{\otimes 2}
-
integrable since we assume
m_{p/2}(\mu)<\infty
; and because of
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}<\infty
also
F^{+}
, the positive part of
F
, is
\mu^{\otimes 2}
-
integrable and we have
F\Ind_{[1/k,k]^{2}}\leq F^{+}

\mu^{\otimes 2}
-
almost everywhere. The conclusion now follows via Fatou’s lemma, that is,

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}^{\otimes 2}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\mu([\frac{1}{k},k])^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\Ind_{[1/k,k]^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}.

Step 2.
Next we assume that
\mu
has support contained in an interval
[a,b]
with
0<a<b<\infty
, and we want
\mu_{k}
to be compactly supported within
(0,\infty)
and to have a continuous Lebesgue density.

Fix a nonnegative function
f\in C_{\text{c}}(\mathbb{R})
supported in
[-1,1]
and such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=1
(any smoothing kernel will do), and for
k\geq k_{0}
define
f_{k}\colon x\mapsto kf(kx)
and
\mu_{k}:=f_{k}*\mu
,
4
4

4

This means
\nu_{k}*\mu
where
\mathrm{d}\nu_{k}(x):=f_{k}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
.
where this time
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
is chosen such that
\frac{1}{k_{0}}\leq\frac{a}{2}
. By the basic properties of convolution it follows that
\mu_{k}
is supported in
[a-\frac{1}{k},b+\frac{1}{k}]\subset[\frac{a}{2},b+\frac{a}{2}]
and has the continuous Lebesgue density

h_{k}\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R},\quad x\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}f_{k}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y).

Again we have to assert (i)–(iii).

Ad (i).
Like in the proof of Lemma
6.17
we will invest Lévy’s continuity theorem. Note
5
5

5

Like before this means
\varphi_{\nu_{k}}
where
\mathrm{d}\nu_{k}(x):=f_{k}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
.

\varphi_{f_{k}}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{k}(x)\mathsf{e}^{\mathsf{i}tx}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\mathsf{e}^{\mathsf{i}tx/k}\,\mathrm{d}x\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=1

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
by dominated convergence; this implies

\varphi_{\mu_{k}}(t)=\varphi_{f_{k}*\mu}(t)=\varphi_{f_{k}}(t)\varphi_{\mu}(t)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\varphi_{\mu}(t)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, thus
(\mu_{k})_{k\geq k_{0}}\to\mu
.

Ad (ii).
This is a direct consequence of (i) since all measures involved are supported in the common compact set
[\frac{a}{2},b+\frac{a}{2}]
, and restricted to that set the function
x\mapsto x^{p/2}
is bounded and continuous.

Ad (iii).
With the notation from Lemma
6.17
and immediately using it we have

\Sigma(\mu_{k})=\Sigma(f_{k}*\mu)\geq\Sigma(\mu),

and therewith,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}^{\otimes 2}

\displaystyle=-c\Sigma(\mu_{k})-(1-c)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(x)

\displaystyle\leq-c\Sigma(\mu)-(1-c)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(x).

So it suffices to show

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\biggl(-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(x)\biggr)\leq-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

But like in (ii) this readily follows from (i) as also the logarithm restricted to
[\frac{a}{2},b+\frac{a}{2}]
is continuous and bounded, and therefore we even have existence of the limit.

Step 3.
Lastly assume that
\mu
is compactly supported in
[a,b]\subset(0,\infty)
and has a continuous Lebesgue density
h
. Call
\upsilon:=\Gleichv([a,b])
, and for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
define
\mu_{k}:=(1-\frac{1}{k})\mu+\frac{1}{k}\,\upsilon
. Clearly each
\mu_{k}
is supported on
[a,b]
and has Lebesgue density
h_{k}:=(1-\frac{1}{k})h+\frac{1}{k(b-a)}\Ind_{[a,b]}
which is continuous on
[a,b]
and bounded from below by
\frac{1}{k(b-a)}
on
[a,b]
. Now we verify (i)–(iii).

Ad (i).
We see
(h_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\to h
pointwise everywhere on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and convergence of densities implies weak convergence of measures.

Ad (ii).
Since
m_{p/2}(\upsilon)<\infty
, we get immediately

m_{p/2}(\mu_{k})=\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{k}\Bigr)m_{p/2}(\mu)+\frac{1}{k}\,m_{p/2}(\upsilon)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}m_{p/2}(\mu).

Ad (iii).
It is easy to see that, because
\mu
has a compactly supported Lebesgue density which is bounded from above, the integral
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}
exists and is finite; the same holds true for
\upsilon
and each
\mu_{k}
. Furthermore we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}^{\otimes 2}=\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{k}\Bigr)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}+\frac{2}{k}\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{k}\Bigr)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}(\mu\otimes\upsilon)+\frac{1}{k^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\upsilon^{\otimes 2},

and since all integrals on the right
-
hand side are finite it converges to
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}
as
k\to\infty
, as desired.

Now to put all the steps together, let
\mu
be arbitrary again as the premises of the lemma require. Fix a metric
d_{\text{w}}
inducing the weak topology on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then by Step 1 there exists
\mu_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
supported on
[a_{k},b_{k}]\subset(0,\infty)
such that
d_{\text{w}}(\mu,\mu_{k}^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{3k}
and
\lvert m_{p/2}(\mu_{k}^{\prime})-m_{p/2}(\mu)\rvert\leq\frac{1}{3k}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}(\mu_{k}^{\prime})^{\otimes 2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}+\frac{1}{3k}
.

By Step 2 we can choose
\mu_{k}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
supported in
[\frac{a_{k}}{2},b_{k}+\frac{a_{k}}{2}]
with continuous Lebesgue density such that
d_{\text{w}}(\mu_{k}^{\prime},\mu_{k}^{\prime\prime})\leq\frac{1}{3k}
and
\lvert m_{p/2}(\mu_{k}^{\prime\prime})-m_{p/2}(\mu_{k}^{\prime})\rvert\leq\frac{1}{3k}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}(\mu_{k}^{\prime\prime})^{\otimes 2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}(\mu_{k}^{\prime})^{\otimes 2}+\frac{1}{3k}
.

Lastly by Step 3 we can find
\mu_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
supported on
[\frac{a_{k}}{2},b_{k}+\frac{a_{k}}{2}]
with Lebesgue density
h_{k}
which is continuous on its support and satisfies
\min h_{k}([\frac{a_{k}}{2},b_{k}+\frac{a_{k}}{2}])>0
, such that
d_{\text{w}}(\mu_{k}^{\prime\prime},\mu_{k})\leq\frac{1}{3k}
and
\lvert m_{p/2}(\mu_{k})-m_{p/2}(\mu_{k}^{\prime\prime})\rvert\leq\frac{1}{3k}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}^{\otimes 2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}(\mu_{k}^{\prime\prime})^{\otimes 2}+\frac{1}{3k}
.

Finally the triangle inequality yields
d_{\text{w}}(\mu,\mu_{k})\leq\frac{1}{k}
and
\lvert m_{p/2}(\mu_{k})-m_{p/2}(\mu)\rvert\leq\frac{1}{k}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}^{\otimes 2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}+\frac{1}{k}
, and thus
(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
fulfills all of (i)–(iv).
∎

Now we have gathered all preparatory results to prove the following statement.

Lemma 6.19
.

Define the functional
\mathcal{I}\colon\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}
by

\mathcal{I}(\mu,M):=\inf_{G}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\bigl[(\nu_{n},m_{p/2}(\nu_{n}))\in G\bigr],

where
G
ranges over basic open subsets of
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
(\mu,M)\in G
. Then we have

\mathcal{I}(\mu,M)\geq-\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu,M)

for any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
and
M\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, with
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}
defined in Proposition
6.15
.

Proof.

First we note a few important properties of
\mathcal{I}
.
\mathcal{I}
is of the form
\mathcal{I}(\mu,M)=\inf_{G}I(G)
with an isotone map
I
(i.e.,
U\subset V
implies
I(U)\leq I(V)
). The system of basic open sets
G\in(\mu,M)
is a neighborhood basis of
(\mu,M)
, and then the form of
\mathcal{I}
yields that we may choose any specific neighborhood basis for any point
(\mu,M)
without changing the value
\mathcal{I}(\mu,M)
. Also, as rightly observed in
[
19
, p. 216]
,
\mathcal{I}
is upper semicontinuous.

Now we are going to prove the statement of this lemma. If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
and
M\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
are such that
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu,M)=\infty
, then trivially there is nothing left to prove. By the definition of
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}
this definitely is the case if
m_{p/2}(\mu)>M
, or if
m_{p/2}(\mu)\leq M
and
\mu(\{0\})>0
.

So consider such
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
and
M\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
that
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu,M)<\infty
, then
m_{p/2}(\mu)\leq M
and
\mu(\{0\})=0
, which also implies
m_{p/2}(\mu)>0
and therefore
M>0
. First assume that
\mu
is supported on a compact interval
[a,b]\subset(0,\infty)
with Lebesgue density
h
which is continuous on
[a,b]
and satisfies
\min h([a,b])>0
, and that
m_{p/2}(\mu)<M
. In this situation we can apply the analogue of
[
28
, Lemma 4.5]
and Lemma
6.16
to get

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}

\displaystyle\log\Wsk\bigl[\nu_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon,d}(\mu)\wedge\lvert m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})-M\rvert<\delta\bigr]

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\Bigl[\nu_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon/3,d}(\mu)\wedge\lvert m_{p/2}(\nu_{n}^{\prime})-m_{p/2}(\mu)\rvert<\frac{\delta}{3}\wedge Y_{m}^{(n)}\in D_{n}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\geq-\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu,M)

for any
\delta,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
, and
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d}\in C_{\text{b}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
. Since

\bigl\{\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon,d}(\mu)\times(M-\delta,M+\delta)\,:\,\delta,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},d\in\mathbb{N},f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d}\in C_{\text{b}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\bigr\}

is a neighborhood basis at
(\mu,M)
, taking the infimum over neighborhoods yields

\mathcal{I}(\mu,M)\geq-\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu,M),

as claimed.

Now let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
and
M\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be general, but still such that
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu,M)<\infty
. Recalling the definition of the kernel
F
introduced before Lemma
6.18
, we can rewrite

\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu,M)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}+M+B_{c,p},

and therefore we know
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}<\infty
. Let
(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence as asserted by Lemma
6.18
, and for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
define

M_{k}:=m_{p/2}(\mu_{k})+M-m_{p/2}(\mu)+\frac{1}{k},

then
M_{k}>m_{p/2}(\mu_{k})
and
(M_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\to M
. This means that for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
we are in the special situation treated above, hence

\mathcal{I}(\mu_{k},M_{k})\geq-\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu_{k},M_{k});

then letting
k\to\infty
and using the upper semicontinuity of
\mathcal{I}
,

\mathcal{I}(\mu,M)\geq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{I}(\mu_{k},M_{k})\geq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\bigl(-\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu_{k},M_{k})\bigr)=-\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu_{k},M_{k}).

Here now the crucial property (iii) of Lemma
6.18
enters the picture, since this leads to

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu_{k},M_{k})

\displaystyle=\liminf_{k\to\infty}\biggl(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}^{\otimes 2}+M_{k}+B_{c,p}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}^{\otimes 2}+M+B_{c,p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\limsup_{k\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}^{\otimes 2}+M+B_{c,p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}+M+B_{c,p}=\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu,M),

and together with the display before this concludes the proof.
∎

[
28
, Lemma 4.7]
actually need not be true as stated; the flaw happens on p. 945, at ‘Now, if we take
\varepsilon\mathbin{\downarrow}0
as well as
\delta\mathbin{\downarrow}0
, then’: even though the functional
\nu\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)
be weakly continuous, this is
not
sufficient for

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon,d}(\mu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y),

if the neighbourhoods
\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon,d}(\mu)
depend on fixed functions
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d}\in C_{\text{b}}(\mathbb{R})
. But in truth this setup is not needed since we actually wish to establish (reverting to our notation)

\inf_{G}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\bigl[(\nu_{n},m_{p/2}(\nu_{n}))\in G\bigr]\leq-\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu,M),

where
G
runs through a neighborhood basis of
(\mu,M)
; thus it suffices to consider a specific subset of basic neighborhoods over which to minimize, as then the global infimum can only be smaller. The precise statement is given next.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
and
M\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with
m_{p/2}(\mu)\leq M
. Then there exists a family
(\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
of basic neighborhoods of
\mu
such that

\lim_{\delta,\varepsilon\to 0+}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\bigl[\nu_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\wedge m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})\in(M-\delta,M+\delta)\bigr]\\
\leq\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)+\frac{1-c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)-M-B_{c,p}.

Before proving Lemma
6.20
we give a purely auxiliary result which by the way nicely illustrates how one can evaluate integrals with respect to cone measures.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\alpha\in(-1,\infty)
, then

\int_{\mathbb{S}_{q,1}^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}(\theta)\prod_{i=1}^{m}\theta_{i}^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{q,1}^{m}(\theta)=\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\alpha+1}{q}\bigr)^{m}}{m\omega_{q,1}^{m}\,q^{m-1}\,\Gamma\bigl(\frac{m(\alpha+1)}{q}\bigr)}.

Proof.

The basic property of the integrand which we are going to exploit is its positive homogeneity. By a standard method the integral is extended to all of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}
via introducing an exponential term. To be specific, we consider

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\mathsf{e}^{-\lVert x\rVert_{q}^{q}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x.

(16)

On the one hand use polar integration on (
16
) to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\mathsf{e}^{-\lVert x\rVert_{q}^{q}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=m\omega_{q,1}^{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{q,1}^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}(r\theta)\mathsf{e}^{-\lVert r\theta\rVert_{q}^{q}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}(r\theta_{i})^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{q,1}^{m}(\theta)\,r^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=m\omega_{q,1}^{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}r^{m\alpha+m-1}\,\mathsf{e}^{-r^{q}}\,\mathrm{d}r\int_{\mathbb{S}_{q,1}^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}(\theta)\prod_{i=1}^{m}\theta_{i}^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{q,1}^{m}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{m\omega_{q,1}^{m}\,\Gamma\bigl(\frac{m(\alpha+1)}{q}\bigr)}{q}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{q,1}^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}(\theta)\prod_{i=1}^{m}\theta_{i}^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{q,1}^{m}(\theta).

On the other hand the integrand of (
16
) has tensor product structure, therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}}\mathsf{e}^{-\lVert x\rVert_{q}^{q}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}x^{\alpha}\,\mathsf{e}^{-x^{q}}\,\mathrm{d}x\biggr)^{m}

\displaystyle=\biggl(\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{\alpha+1}{q}\bigr)}{q}\biggr)^{m}.

Equating the two partial results and rearranging lead to the claim.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.20
.

The strategy is the same as in
[
28
]
. For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\gamma\in[0,1)
define the kernels
F(\cdot,\cdot;\gamma),F_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot;\gamma)\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\to(-\infty,\infty]
by

F(x,y;\gamma):=\begin{cases}-\frac{c}{2}\log\lvert x-y\rvert-(1-\gamma)\frac{1-c}{4}\log(xy)+\frac{\gamma}{2}(x^{p/2}+y^{p/2})&\text{if }x>0\wedge y>0\wedge x\neq y,\\
\infty&\text{else},\end{cases}

and

F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)=\min\{F(x,y;\gamma),\alpha\}.

We note some properties of
F
and
F_{\alpha}
: for
\gamma>0
,
F(\cdot,\cdot;\gamma)
and
F_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot;\gamma)
are bounded from below, hence their (coinciding) negative parts are
\mu^{\otimes 2}
-
integrable; and for
\gamma=0
they can be bounded from below by
-C(x^{p/2}+y^{p/2}+(xy)^{p/2})
with some
C>0
, and since we have been supposing
m_{p/2}(\mu)\leq M<\infty
, also in this case the negative parts are
\mu^{\otimes 2}
-
integrable. From this follows that for any
\alpha\geq 0
and
\gamma\in[0,1)
,
F_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot;\gamma)
is
\mu^{\otimes 2}
-
integrable. Also we can rewrite

F(x,y;\gamma)=F(x,y;0)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\Bigl(x^{p/2}+y^{p/2}-\frac{1-c}{2}\log(xy)\Bigr),

wherever the value is finite, which clearly implies
\lim_{\gamma\to 0+}F(x,y;\gamma)=F(x,y;0)
pointwise, and the map
\gamma\to F(x,y;\gamma)
is increasing on

P:=\Bigl\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\,:\,x^{p/2}+y^{p/2}-\frac{1-c}{2}\log(xy)\geq 0\Bigr\}

and decreasing on its complement,
N:=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus P
; this holds true for
F_{\alpha}
too. Notice that
P
and
N
do not depend on
\gamma
. Lastly, for any
\gamma\in[0,1)
and
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}
the map
\alpha\mapsto F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)
is increasing and
\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)=F(x,y;\gamma)
.

Fix a metric
d_{\text{w}}
on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
which metrizes the weak topology, then for any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
choose a basic weak neighborhood
\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}
of
\mu
with
\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(\mu)
(the open ball w.r.t.
d_{\text{w}}
around
\mu
with radius
\varepsilon
).

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\to(-\infty,\infty]
be some functional, then the map
\varepsilon\mapsto\inf\bigl(\mathcal{F}(B_{\varepsilon}(\mu))\bigr)
is decreasing, hence the improper limit
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\inf\bigl(\mathcal{F}(B_{\varepsilon}(\mu))\bigr)
exists; next, for any
\varepsilon>0
we may choose an
\varepsilon^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\in(0,\varepsilon]
such that
B_{\varepsilon^{\prime}(\varepsilon)}(\mu)\subset\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(\mu)
, then as
\varepsilon\to 0+
, so also
\varepsilon^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\to 0+
, and hence

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\inf(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}))=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\inf\bigl(\mathcal{F}(B_{\varepsilon}(\mu))\bigr).

If in addition
\mathcal{F}
is continuous at
\mu
, then there follows

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\inf(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}))=\mathcal{F}(\mu).

(17)

Indeed, the inequality ‘
\leq
’ readily follows from
\inf(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}))\leq\mathcal{F}(\mu)
for any
\varepsilon>0
(since
\mu\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}
); and concerning ‘
\geq
’ it suffices to prove
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\inf(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}))\geq\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\zeta
for any
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. But continuity implies that for any
\zeta>0
there exists
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
such that
\mathcal{F}(\nu)\geq\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\zeta
for any
\nu\in B_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(\mu)
; in particular this remains valid for all
\nu\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
, and by passing to the infimum and then investing
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\inf(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}))\geq\inf(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}))
we arrive at (
17
).

As observed in
[
28
]
, for any
\alpha>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
the map

\nu\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)

is weakly continuous, and therefore identity (
17
) can be applied.

Now that we have finished the preparations, let
\delta,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, w.l.o.g.
\delta<M
, and define

H:=\biggl\{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}\,:\,\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{y_{i}}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\wedge\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{p/2}\in(M-\delta,M+\delta)\biggr\};

then

\displaystyle\Wsk\bigl[

\displaystyle\nu_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\wedge m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})\in(M-\delta,M+\delta)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}\int_{H}\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\lVert y\rVert_{p/2}^{p/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert y_{j}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}\int_{H}\exp\biggl(-\beta n\Bigl(1-\frac{\gamma(m-1)}{cn}\Bigr)\lVert y\rVert_{p/2}^{p/2}\biggr)\exp\biggl(-\frac{2\beta}{c}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq m}F(y_{i},y_{j};\gamma)\biggr)

\displaystyle\mspace{100mu}\cdot\exp\biggl(\Bigl(\frac{\beta(n-m+1)}{2}-1-\frac{\beta(1-\gamma)(1-c)(m-1)}{2c}\Bigr)\sum_{i=1}^{m}\log(y_{i})\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}y,

where we have used the definition of
F
to rewrite
\prod_{i<j}\lvert y_{j}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}
; we want to estimate the integral from above. First note
\lim_{n\to\infty}(1-\frac{\gamma(m-1)}{cn})=1-\gamma>0
, hence the first exponential term may be estimated on
H
from above by
\exp\bigl(\beta n(1-\frac{\gamma(m-1)}{cn})m(M-\delta)\bigr)
for all sufficiently large
n
(so also in the sequel). Next there is

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq m}F(y_{i},y_{j};\gamma)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq m}F_{\alpha}(y_{i},y_{j};\gamma)

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x_{1},x_{2};\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{y}^{\otimes 2}(x_{1},x_{2})-\frac{m\alpha}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{m^{2}}{2}\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x_{1},x_{2};\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}(x_{1},x_{2})-\frac{m\alpha}{2},

where of course
\mu_{y}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{y_{i}}
, and this treats the second exponential term. The third exponential term needs special care; even though we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta n}\Bigl(\frac{\beta(n-m+1)}{2}-1-\frac{\beta(1-\gamma)(1-c)(m-1)}{2c}\Bigr)=\frac{\gamma(1-c)}{2}\geq 0,

in the case
c=1
the coefficient of
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\log(y_{i})
can be negative and hence naively maximizing the exponent over
\{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}\,:\,m(M-\delta)<\lVert y\rVert_{p/2}^{p/2}<(M+\delta)\}
would yield the trivial bound
\infty
. (As in other places the authors of
[
28
]
remain silent on this point although they would have needed it in the proof of their Theorem 1.5.) Instead we have to find a finer estimate for the following integral,

\int_{H}\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1-\beta(1-\gamma)(1-c)(m-1)/(2c)}\,\mathrm{d}y.

Call
\alpha_{n}:=\frac{\beta(n-m+1)}{2}-1-\frac{\beta(1-\gamma)(1-c)(m-1)}{2c}
, then we already have observed above that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\alpha_{n}}{\beta n}=\frac{\gamma(1-c)}{2}
, hence in the case
c<1
we know
\alpha_{n}\geq 0>-1
for all
n
large enough, and in the case
c=1
we get
\alpha_{n}=\frac{\beta(n-m+1)}{2}-1\geq\frac{\beta}{2}-1>-1
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, since
m\leq n
. Therefore we have
\alpha_{n}>-1
for eventually all
n
in any case, and we aim for an application of Lemma
6.21
. Using
H\subset\bigl\{y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}\,:\,m(M-\delta)<\lVert y\rVert_{p/2}^{p/2}<m(M+\delta)\bigr\}
, polar integration leads to

\displaystyle\int_{H}\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\alpha_{n}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq m\omega_{p/2,1}^{m}\int_{(m(M-\delta))^{2/p}}^{(m(M+\delta))^{2/p}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{p/2,1}^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}}(r\theta)\prod_{i=1}^{m}(r\theta_{i})^{\alpha_{n}}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{p/2,1}^{m}(\theta)\,r^{m-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=m\omega_{p/2,1}^{m}\int_{(m(M-\delta))^{2/p}}^{(m(M+\delta))^{2/p}}r^{m(\alpha_{n}+1)-1}\,\mathrm{d}r\int_{\mathbb{S}_{p/2,1}^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}}(\theta)\prod_{i=1}^{m}\theta_{i}^{\alpha_{n}}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa_{p/2,1}^{m}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(m(M+\delta))^{2m(\alpha_{n}+1)/p}}{m(\alpha_{n}+1)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{2(\alpha_{n}+1)}{p}\bigr)^{m}}{\bigl(\frac{p}{2}\bigr)^{m-1}\,\Gamma\bigl(\frac{2m(\alpha_{n}+1)}{p}\bigr)},

where for the last line we have invested Lemma
6.21
(obviously it is immaterial whether we integrate over
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}
or
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}
). Summarizing all this we see

\displaystyle\Wsk\bigl[\nu_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\wedge m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})

\displaystyle\in(M-\delta,M+\delta)\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}\exp\biggl(-\beta mn(M-\delta)\Bigl(1-\frac{\gamma(m-1)}{cn}\Bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\exp\biggl(-\frac{\beta m^{2}}{c}\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)+\frac{\alpha\beta m}{c}\biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\frac{(m(M+\delta))^{2m(\alpha_{n}+1)/p}}{m(\alpha_{n}+1)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{2(\alpha_{n}+1)}{p}\bigr)^{m}}{\bigl(\frac{p}{2}\bigr)^{m-1}\,\Gamma\bigl(\frac{2m(\alpha_{n}+1)}{p}\bigr)}.

Proposition
6.13
tells us
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log(Z_{m,n,p,\beta})=B_{c,p}
. The first exponential term needs no comment and for the second we get

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Bigl(\mathsf{e}^{-\frac{\beta m^{2}}{c}\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)+\frac{\alpha\beta m}{c}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=-\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}(x,y).

Stirling’s formula
\Gamma(z)=\sqrt{2\pi}\,z^{z-1/2}\,\mathsf{e}^{-z+R(z)}
, where
R(z)=\frac{1}{12z}\bigl(1+\BigO(z^{-2})\bigr)
, implies

\frac{m^{2m(\alpha_{n}+1)/p}\,\Gamma\bigl(\frac{2(\alpha_{n}+1)}{p}\bigr)^{m}}{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{2m(\alpha_{n}+1)}{p}\bigr)}=m^{1/2}\Bigl(\frac{\pi p}{\alpha_{n}+1}\Bigr)^{(m-1)/2}\,\mathsf{e}^{mR(2(\alpha_{n}+1)/p)-R(2m(\alpha_{n}+1)/p)},

hence

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\frac{m^{2m(\alpha_{n}+1)/p}\,\Gamma\bigl(\frac{2(\alpha_{n}+1)}{p}\bigr)^{m}}{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{2m(\alpha_{n}+1)}{p}\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\log(m)}{2\beta mn}+\frac{(m-1)\log(\pi p)}{2\beta mn}-\frac{(m+1)\log(\alpha_{n}+1)}{2\beta mn}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{R\bigl(\frac{2(\alpha_{n}+1)}{p}\bigr)}{\beta n}-\frac{R\bigl(\frac{2m(\alpha_{n}+1)}{p}\bigr)}{\beta mn}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0,

where we also have used
\alpha_{n}=\BigO(\beta n)
. Lastly there remains

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\frac{(M+\delta)^{2m(\alpha_{n}+1)/p}}{m(\alpha_{n}+1)\bigl(\frac{p}{2}\bigr)^{m-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(\alpha_{n}+1)\log(M+\delta)}{p\beta n}-\frac{\log(m(\alpha_{n}+1))}{\beta mn}-\frac{(m-1)\log\bigl(\frac{p}{2}\bigr)}{\beta mn}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\frac{\gamma(1-c)\log(M+\delta)}{p}.

Therewith we get

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\bigl[\nu_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\wedge m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})\in(M-\delta,M+\delta)\bigr]\\
\leq-B_{c,p}-(M-\delta)(1-\gamma)-\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)+\frac{\gamma(1-c)}{p}\log(M+\delta).

Now we send
\delta,\varepsilon\to 0+
, so by our discussion at the preparations for the proof concerning the continuity of infima of continuous functionals we have

\lim_{\delta,\varepsilon\to 0+}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\bigl[\nu_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\wedge m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})\in(M-\delta,M+\delta)\bigr]\\
\leq-B_{c,p}-M(1-\gamma)-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)+\frac{\gamma(1-c)}{p}\log(M).

Next we take
\gamma\to 0+
, then clearly
F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)\to F_{\alpha}(x,y;0)
. Recall the sets
P
and
N
from the beginning of this proof; on
P
,
F_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot;\gamma)
is bounded from below by
F_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot;0)
, and on
N
, it is bounded from above by
\alpha
; either bound is
\mu^{\otimes 2}
-
quasiintegrable, hence monotone convergence can be applied on
P
and on
N
separately, and because the negative part of the limiting function
F_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot;0)
is
\mu^{\otimes 2}
-
integrable over
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}
we also have convergence of the integral on all of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, that is,

\lim_{\gamma\to 0+}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;\gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;0)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y).

Lastly we let
\alpha\to\infty
, then
F_{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot;0)
is dominated from below by
F(\cdot,\cdot;0)
whose negative part is integrable as we have discussed above. Thus monotone convergence can be used once more, so

\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}(x,y;0)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F(x,y;0)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y).

Plugging in the definition of
F
we finally have

\lim_{\delta,\varepsilon\to 0+}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\bigl[\nu_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\wedge m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})\in(M-\delta,M+\delta)\bigr]\\
\leq-B_{c,p}-M+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\Bigl(\frac{c}{2}\log\lvert x-y\rvert+\frac{1-c}{4}\log(xy)\Bigr)\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y),

and slight simplification leads to the claimed result.
∎

[
28
, Lemma 4.8]
, which concerns the exponential tightness, carries over analogously, where the reference point is
[
19
, Theorem 5.5.1, p. 232]
now.

We also adapt
[
28
, Remark 4.9]
, that is, the LDP on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
; the proof of

\lim_{A\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\Bigl[m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})<\frac{1}{A}\Bigr]=-\infty

needs to be modified as follows: We have, for any
A\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,

\displaystyle\Wsk\Bigl[

\displaystyle m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})<\frac{1}{A}\Bigr]=\Wsk\Bigl[\lVert Y^{(n)}\rVert_{p/2}<\Bigl(\frac{m}{A}\Bigr)^{2/p}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}}\Ind_{(\frac{m}{A})^{2/p}\mathbb{B}_{p/2,1}^{m}}(y)\,\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\lVert y\rVert_{p/2}^{p/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert y_{j}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}}\Ind_{(\frac{m}{A})^{2/p}\mathbb{B}_{p/2,1}^{m}}(y)\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert y_{j}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}\Bigl(\frac{m}{A}\Bigr)^{\beta mn/p}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{p/2,1}^{m}}(z)\prod_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert z_{j}-z_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}z,

where in the last step we have substituted
z:=\bigl(\frac{A}{m}\bigr)^{2/p}y
. The last integral then is transformed with the same method as in the proof of Theorem
F
, part 1, that is,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}}\Ind_{\mathbb{B}_{p/2,1}^{m}}(z)

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert z_{j}-z_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\frac{(\beta n)^{\beta mn/p}}{\Gamma(\frac{\beta mn}{p}+1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}}\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\lVert y\rVert_{p/2}^{p/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert y_{j}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{(\beta n)^{\beta mn/p}\,Z_{m,n,p,\beta}}{\Gamma(\frac{\beta mn}{p}+1)};

this leads to

\Wsk\Bigl[m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})<\frac{1}{A}\Bigr]\leq\frac{(\beta mn)^{\beta mn/p}}{A^{\beta mn/p}\,\Gamma(\frac{\beta mn}{p}+1)},

and thence

\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\Bigl[m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})<\frac{1}{A}\Bigr]\leq\frac{\log(\beta mn)-\log(A)}{p}-\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Gamma\Bigl(\frac{\beta mn}{p}+1\Bigr).

Stirling’s formula yields
\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl(\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Gamma(\frac{\beta mn}{p}+1)-\frac{1}{p}\log(\frac{\beta mn}{p})\bigr)=-\frac{1}{p}
, hence

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk\Bigl[m_{p/2}(\nu_{n})<\frac{1}{A}\Bigr]\leq\frac{1-\log(A)}{p},

so the claim follows as
A\to\infty
.

Step 3

The result of this step is the LDP for the empirical measure of the squares of the singular values, reached via the contraction principle from the LDP for the empirical pair in Step 2.

Proposition 6.22
.

Let
(X^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of random variables where
X^{(n)}\sim\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and define
\mu_{n}^{2}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{2/p}s_{i}(X^{(n)})^{2}}
. Then
(\mu_{n}^{2})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies a large deviations principle with speed
\beta mn
and good rate function
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}^{2}\colon\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\to[0,\infty]
given by

\mathcal{I}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu):=\begin{cases}-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-\frac{1-c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\frac{\log(\mathsf{e}p)}{p}+B_{c,p}&\text{if }m_{p/2}(\mu)\leq 1,\\
\infty&\text{else,}\end{cases}

which has a unique global minimizer
\mu_{c,p}^{2}
defined by
\mu_{c,p}^{2}(A)=\nu_{c,p}\bigl(m_{p/2}(\nu_{c,p})^{2/p}A\bigr)
, where
\nu_{c,p}
is the same as in Proposition
6.13
; in particular
\mu_{c,p}^{2}
has compact support and a Lebesgue density.

Proof.

The role model here is
[
28
, Proposition 5.1]
which we adopt with the obvious adaptions; in particular
p
has to be replaced by
\frac{p}{2}
. The contraction principle yields the LPD with speed
\beta mn
and good rate function defined by

\mathcal{I}(\mu):=\inf_{(\nu,M)\in F_{p/2}^{-1}\{\mu\}}\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\nu,M).

The value
\mathcal{I}(\mu)=\infty
for
m_{p/2}(\mu)>1
is argued as in
[
28
]
. In the case
m_{p/2}(\mu)\leq 1
, note that actually for any
M\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
there exists a
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
such that
F_{p/2}(\nu,M)=\mu
; simply take
\nu:=F_{p/2}(\mu,M^{-1})
(this
—
important
—
point is not addressed in
[
28
]
). Therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\inf_{(\nu,M)\in F_{p/2}^{-1}\{\mu\}}\biggl(-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-\frac{1-c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)+M+B_{c,p}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\inf_{(\nu,M)\in F_{p/2}^{-1}\{\mu\}}\biggl(-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\bigl(M^{2/p}\lvert x-y\rvert\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)

\displaystyle\mspace{120mu}-\frac{1-c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(M^{2/p}x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+M+B_{c,p}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\inf_{(\nu,M)\in F_{p/2}^{-1}\{\mu\}}\biggl(-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-\frac{1-c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\mspace{120mu}-\frac{c}{p}\log(M)-\frac{1-c}{p}\log(M)+M+B_{c,p}\biggr)

\displaystyle=-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-\frac{1-c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\frac{\log(\mathsf{e}p)}{p}+B_{c,p},

because
\frac{\log(\mathsf{e}p)}{p}
is the global minimum of
-\frac{1}{p}\log(x)+x
on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. But this concludes
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{c,p}^{2}
.

Concerning the minimizer of
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}^{2}
, from Proposition
6.15
we know that
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}
has the global minimizer
(\nu_{c,p},m_{p/2}(\nu_{c,p}))
and hence
\mu_{c,p}^{2}=F_{p/2}(\nu_{c,p},m_{p/2}(\nu_{c,p}))
is a global minimizer of
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}^{2}
. It remains to show unicity. Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
with
\mu\neq\mu_{c,p}^{2}
, w.l.o.g.
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu)<\infty
; then
\{\mu\}
is weakly closed and thus also the fibre
F_{p/2}^{-1}\{\mu\}
is closed in
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Because of
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu)<\infty
and because
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}
is a good rate function there exists
(\nu,M)\in F_{p/2}^{-1}\{\mu\}
such that
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}^{2}(\mu)=\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\nu,M)
. But
\mu\neq\mu_{c,p}^{2}
forces
(\nu,M)\neq(\nu_{c,p},m_{p/2}(\nu_{c,p}))
and therefore
\mathcal{J}_{c,p}^{2}(\nu,M)>0
.
∎

Step 4

The key observation here is stated in the lemma below.

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
-
valued random variables which satisfies an LDP at speed
(s_{n})_{n\geq 1}
with GRF
I
, and let
q\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a measurable bijection with continuous inverse. Then
(\mu_{n}\circ q)_{n\geq 1}
satisfies an LDP at speed
(s_{n})_{n\geq 1}
with GRF
I^{q}\colon\mu\mapsto I(\mu\circ q^{-1})
. In particular
\mu\circ q
is a minimizer of
I^{q}
iff
\mu
is a mimizer of
I
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that the map

F\colon\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})&\to\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\\
\mu&\mapsto\mu\circ q\end{aligned}\right.

is weakly continuous, then apply the contraction principle to
(F(\mu_{n}))_{n\geq 1}
which will lead to the desired results. Note that
F
is well
-
defined since
q
is a measurable isomorphism, and that for the same reason
F
is bijective.

Let
\mu
in
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
and let
V\subset\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
be a basic weak neighborhood of
F(\mu)=\mu\circ q
, that is,

V=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\biggl\{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\,:\,\biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\nu-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}(\mu\circ q)\biggr\rvert<\varepsilon\biggr\}

with some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{k}\in C_{\text{b}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
. Now
f_{i}\circ q^{-1}\in C_{\text{b}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
for any
i\in[1,k]
, therefore

U:=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\biggl\{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\,:\,\biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}(f_{i}\circ q^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}\nu-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}(f_{i}\circ q^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}\mu\biggr\rvert<\varepsilon\biggr\}

is a weak neighborhood of
\mu
, and for any
\nu\in U
we get, for any
i\in[1,k]
,

\biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}(\nu\circ q)-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}f_{i}\,\mathrm{d}(\mu\circ q)\biggr\rvert=\biggl\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}(f_{i}\circ q^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}\nu-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}(f_{i}\circ q^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}\mu\biggr\rvert<\varepsilon,

and thus (recall
F(\nu)=\nu\circ q
)
F(\nu)\in V
.
∎

The most important consequence of Lemma
6.23
for us is that not only the empirical measures of the squares of singular values satisfy an LDP, but also of the singular values themselves. This concludes the proof of Theorem
G
in the case
X^{(n)}\sim\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
.

Proposition 6.24
.

Let
(X^{(n)})_{n\geq m}
be a sequence of random variables such that
X^{(n)}\sim\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and define
\mu_{n}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{1/p}s_{i}(X^{(n)})}
, then the sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satsifies a large deviations principle with speed
\beta mn
and good rate function

\mathcal{I}_{c,p}(\mu):=\begin{cases}-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x^{2}-y^{2}\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-(1-c)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\frac{\log(\mathsf{e}p)}{p}+B_{c,p}&\text{if }m_{p}(\mu)\leq 1,\\
\infty&\text{else,}\end{cases}

which possesses a unique minimizer
\mu_{c,p}
, given by its Lebesgue density

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{c,p}(x)}{\mathrm{d}x}=2x\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{c,p}^{2}(x^{2})}{\mathrm{d}x},

where
\mu_{c,p}^{2}
is the minimizer from Proposition
6.22
.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
6.23
to the LDP for
\bigl(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{2/p}s_{i}(X^{(n)})^{2}}\bigr)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of Proposition
6.22
and the map
q(x):=x^{2}
, defined on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
; use that generally
\delta_{x}\circ q=\delta_{q^{-1}(x)}=\delta_{x^{1/2}}
and therefore especially
\bigl(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{2/p}s_{i}(X^{(n)})^{2}}\bigr)\circ q=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{1/p}s_{i}(X^{(n)})}
; also mind
m_{p/2}(\mu)=m_{p}(\mu\circ q)
. The density of
\mu_{c,p}
is a simple instance of density transformation.
∎

Remark 6.25
.

As already indicated in Remarks
6.6
and
6.14
the minimizer
\mu_{c,2}
is explicitly known. This is because then the rate function
\mathcal{J}_{c,2}
from Proposition
6.13
can be written as

\mathcal{J}_{c,2}(\mu)=\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\log\Bigl(\frac{1}{\lvert x-y\rvert w(x)w(y)}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)+B_{c,2},

with the weight function

w\colon\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathbb{C}&\to\mathbb{C}\\
x&\mapsto x^{(1-c)/(2c)}\,\mathsf{e}^{-x/c}\Ind_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}(x)\end{aligned}\right.

This is a
Laguerre weight
, and by
[
45
, Example IV.5.4]
the minimizer
\nu_{c,2}
of
\mathcal{J}_{c,2}
has Lebesgue density

\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{c,2}(x)}{\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{1}{c\pi x}\Bigl(c-\Bigl(x-\frac{1+c}{2}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}\Ind_{\bigl[\frac{(1-\sqrt{c})^{2}}{2},\frac{(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}}{2}\bigr]}(x).

We can easily compute
m_{1}(\nu_{c,2})=\frac{1}{2}
, and therewith Proposition
6.22
yields the minimizer
\mu_{c,2}^{2}
with density

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{c,2}^{2}(x)}{\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{c,2}}{\mathrm{d}x}\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)=\frac{1}{2\pi cx}\bigl(4c-(x-1-c)^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\Ind_{[(1-\sqrt{c})^{2},(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}]}(x);

from this we can identify
\mu_{c,2}^{2}
as a
Marchenko
–
Pastur distribution.
And finally from Proposition
6.24
we obtain the minimizer
\mu_{c,2}
with density

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{c,2}(x)}{\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{1}{\pi cx}\bigl(4c-(x^{2}-1-c)^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\Ind_{[1-\sqrt{c},1+\sqrt{c}]}(x);

in particular we have for
c=1

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{1,2}(x)}{\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{1}{\pi}\,(4-x^{2})^{1/2}\Ind_{[0,2]}(x),

which corresponds to a quarter
-
circle distribution, as expected.

Also the constant
B_{c,2}
can be computed explicitly because of

\displaystyle Z_{m,n,2,\beta}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}}\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\lVert y\rVert_{1}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert y_{j}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=(\beta n)^{-\beta mn/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}}\mathsf{e}^{-\lVert y\rVert_{1}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert y_{j}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=(\beta n)^{-\beta mn/2}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\beta(n-m+1)}{2}+\frac{\beta(i-1)}{2})\Gamma(1+\frac{\beta i}{2})}{\Gamma(1+\frac{\beta}{2})},

where we have used the Selberg integral of Laguerre type (see
[
36
, p. 354]
). Using Lemma
2.2
to deal with the products of gamma functions we arrive at

B_{c,2}=-\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\log(2)}{2}+\frac{c}{4}\log(c)-\frac{(1-c)^{2}}{4c}\log(1-c).

For
c=1
this is consistent with
[
28
, Theorem 1.5]
.

Step 5

The last step is the establishment of the LDP w.r.t. the uniform distribution on the ball, i.e.
X^{(n)}\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n})
. Recall
s(X^{(n)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}U^{1/(\beta mn)}s(\Theta^{(n)})
, where
\Theta^{(n)}\sim\kappa_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n}
and hence its LDP is provided by Proposition
6.24
, and
U\sim\Gleichv([0,1])
is independent from
\Theta^{(n)}
and satisfies the following well
-
known LDP (see
[
2
]
, e.g., for a quite general version).

Lemma 6.26
.

The sequence
(U^{1/(\beta mn)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies a large deviations principle with speed
\beta mn
and good rate function
\mathcal{I}_{U}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty]
given by

\mathcal{I}_{U}(x):=\begin{cases}-\log(x)&\text{if }x\in(0,1],\\
\infty&\text{else.}\end{cases}

Proposition 6.27
.

Let
(X^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of random variables such that
X^{(n)}\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{p},\beta}^{m\times n})
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\mu_{n}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{m^{1/p}s_{i}(X^{(n)})}
satisfies a large deviations principle with speed
\beta mn
and the same good rate function
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}
as in Proposition
6.24
.

Proof.

Again the argument runs parallel to
[
28
, Proposition 6.2]
, so we adopt their notations with the obvious adaptations, and thus the contraction principle yields the LDP with speed
\beta mn
and the good rate function

\mathcal{I}(\mu):=\inf_{(\nu,u)\in F^{-1}\{\mu\}}\bigl(\mathcal{I}_{c,p}(\nu)-\log(u)\bigr),

where
\mathcal{I}_{c,p}
is the rate function from Proposition
6.24
. The cases
m_{p}(\mu)>1
and
m_{p}(\mu)\leq 1\wedge\mu_{p}(\nu)>1
via the same reasoning lead to
\mathcal{I}(\mu)=\infty
, and in the remaining case
m_{p}(\mu)\leq 1\wedge m_{p}(\nu)\leq 1
these conditions also force
u\in[m_{p}(\mu)^{1/p},1]
and thence

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\inf_{(\nu,u)\in F^{-1}\{\mu\}}\biggl(-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x^{2}-y^{2}\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-(1-c)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)

\displaystyle\mspace{120mu}+\frac{\log(\mathsf{e}p)}{p}+B_{c,p}-\log(u)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\inf_{(\nu,u)\in F^{-1}\{\mu\}}\biggl(-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\Bigl(\frac{\lvert x^{2}-y^{2}\rvert}{u^{2}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-(1-c)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log\Bigl(\frac{x}{u}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\mspace{120mu}+\frac{\log(\mathsf{e}p)}{p}+B_{c,p}-\log(u)\biggr)

\displaystyle=-\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}\log\lvert x^{2}-y^{2}\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-(1-c)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\frac{\log(\mathsf{e}p)}{p}+B_{c,p},

proving
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{c,p}
.
∎

6.4
Proof of Theorem
H

Recall from Theorem
F
, part 3, the representation
X^{(n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}V\diag(((Y_{i}^{(n)})^{1/2})_{i\leq m})U^{*}
whenever
X^{(n)}\sim\Gleichv(\mathbb{B}_{S_{\infty},\beta}^{m\times n})
, where
Y^{(n)}
has Lebesgue density (
15
). Hence
(s_{\pi(i)}(X^{(n)})^{2})_{i\leq m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}Y^{(n)}
, where
\pi\sim\Gleichv(\mathfrak{S}_{m})
is independent of
X^{(n)}
, and via Lemma
6.12
we know
\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{s_{i}(X^{(n)})^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{Y_{i}^{(n)}}=:\nu_{n}
. So we state an LDP for
(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
first.

Proposition 6.28
.

If
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{m}{n}=c\in(0,1]
, then
(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies a large deviations principle at speed
\beta mn
with good rate function

\mathcal{J}_{c,\infty}(\mu)=\begin{cases}-\frac{c}{2}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\log\lvert x-y\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)-\frac{1-c}{2}\int_{[0,1]}\log(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+B_{c,\infty}&\text{if }\supp(\mu)\subset[0,1],\\
\infty&\text{else,}\end{cases}

where
B_{c,\infty}
is the same as in Theorem
H
; and
\mathcal{J}_{c,\infty}
has a unique minimizer
\nu_{c,\infty}
, which is given explicitly by its Lebesgue density,

\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{c,\infty}(x)}{\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{1+c}{2c\pi x}\,\frac{\bigl(x-(\frac{1-c}{1+c})^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}}{(1-x)^{1/2}}\Ind_{[(\frac{1-c}{1+c})^{2},1]}(x).

Before giving a proof for Proposition
6.28
we observe that it implies Theorem
H
almost immediately through an application of Lemma
6.23
.

Now we prove Proposition
6.28
. First notice that, in contrast to Proposition
6.13
,
[
19
, Theorem 5.5.1]
is not applicable directly because they only allow continuous external potentials defined on all
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, but in our case we have

\Ind_{[0,1]^{m}}(y)=\mathsf{e}^{-\beta n\sum_{i=1}^{m}Q(y_{i})}

with

Q(y):=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }y\in[0,1],\\
\infty&\text{else.}\end{cases}

Yet it turns out that their proof can be adapted. Since it requires only minor modifications we present only those and refer the reader to
[
19
]
for the details.

First we show that the rate function must be infinite at any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
with
\supp(\mu)\setminus[0,1]\neq\emptyset
.

Lemma 6.29
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
with
\supp(\mu)\setminus[0,1]\neq\emptyset
. Then

\inf_{G}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log\Wsk[\nu_{n}\in G]=-\infty,

where the infimum is taken over basic neighborhoods
G
of
\mu
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that there exists a weak neighborhood
G
of
\mu
such that
\supp(\nu)\setminus[0,1]\neq\emptyset
for all
\nu\in G
; since
\supp(\nu_{n})\subset[0,1]
this implies
\nu_{n}\notin G
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and hence the statement follows.

Because
(1,\infty)
is open, the evaluation map

\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\ni\nu\mapsto\nu((1,\infty))

is weakly lower semicontinuous; by the premises on
\mu
we know
\mu((1,\infty))>0
, and hence there exists a (basic) weak neighborhood
G
of
\mu
such that, for any
\nu\in G
,

\nu((1,\infty))>\frac{\mu((1,\infty))}{2}>0.

This implies
\supp(\nu)\setminus[0,1]\neq\emptyset
for all
\nu\in G
.
∎

From now on we only consider
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
such that
\supp(\mu)\subset[0,1]
.

In order to adapt the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 5.5.1]
, define the kernel functions on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}
,

\displaystyle F(x,y):=\begin{cases}-\frac{c}{2}\log\lvert x-y\rvert-\frac{1-c}{4}\log(xy)&\text{if }(x,y)\in[0,1]^{2},\\
\infty&\text{else,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{n}(x,y):=\begin{cases}-\frac{m}{2n}\log\lvert x-y\rvert-\frac{\beta(n-m+1)-2}{4\beta n}\log(xy)&\text{if }(x,y)\in[0,1]^{2},\\
\infty&\text{else,}\end{cases}

and for any
\alpha>0
their cutoffs
F_{\alpha}:=\min\{F,\alpha\}
and analogously
\tilde{F}_{n,\alpha}
. Then
F
and
\tilde{F}_{n}
are bounded from below and lower semicontinuous, and
F_{\alpha}
and
\tilde{F}_{n,\alpha}
are bounded and lower semicontinuous. Therefore for any
F^{\prime}\in\{F,\tilde{F}_{n},F_{\alpha},\tilde{F}_{n,\alpha}\}
the map

\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\ni\nu\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}

is a well
-
defined weakly lower semicontinuous functional.

[
19
, Lemma 5.5.2]
remains true unchanged. The existence of the unique minimizers
\mu_{0}
,
\tilde{\mu}_{n}
needs to be argued differently: write

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}=\frac{c}{2}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\log\Bigl(\frac{1}{\lvert x-y\rvert w(x)w(y)}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}(x,y)

with the weight function

w\colon\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathbb{C}&\to\mathbb{C}\\
x&\mapsto x^{(1-c)/(2c)}\Ind_{[0,1]}(x)\end{aligned}\right.,

then
w
is an admissible weight in the sense of
[
45
, Definition I.1.1]
, and by
[
45
, Theorem I.1.3]
the energy functional admits a unique minimizer
\nu_{c,\infty}
; even more,
[
45
, Example IV.5.3]
with
\theta:=\frac{1-c}{1+c}
gives the Lebesgue density of
\nu_{c,\infty}
, and it equals that given in Proposition
6.28
. The analogous argument is valid for
\tilde{F}_{n}
, but the only relevant information about
\tilde{\mu}_{n}
which we need is that
\supp(\tilde{\mu}_{n})\subset[0,1]
, and this already proves that
(\tilde{\mu}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight, which is the first part of
[
19
, Lemma 5.5.3]
, and the argument for the second part does not change.

[
19
, Lemma 5.5.4]
is adapted easily; note that integration extends simply over
[0,1]^{m}
, and
Q=0
. In truth we know more as

Z_{m,n,\infty,\beta}=\int_{[0,1]^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\lvert y_{j}-y_{i}\rvert^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}y

is just a Selberg integral whose explicit value we know and whose asymptotics can be tackled with Lemma
2.2
; this yields the limit
B_{c,\infty}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta mn}\log(Z_{m,n,\infty,\beta})
stated in Theorem
H
.

The only thing to be noted in the proof of
[
19
, Lemma 5.5.5]
is that
\nu\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}
is only weakly lower semicontinuous, but this suffices to ensure

\inf_{G}\biggl(-\inf_{\nu\in G}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}\biggr)\leq-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2},

which is all we need. Indeed, for any
M\in\mathbb{R}
with
M<\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}
there exists a basic neighborhood
G
of
\mu
such that, for all
\nu\in G
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}>M
. This implies
\inf_{\nu\in G}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}\geq M
, hence
\sup_{G}\inf_{\nu\in G}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{\otimes 2}\geq M
, and letting
M\to\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}}F_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes 2}
and taking the negatives proves the claim.

The only subtlety in the proof of
[
19
, Lemma 5.5.6]
is that the exponent
\frac{\beta(n-m+1)}{2}-1
may be positive or negative in the case
c=1
(in the case
c<1
it is positive up to finitely many
n
), so
y_{i}^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}
must be bounded from below by
\min\bigl\{(a_{i}^{(n)})^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1},(b_{i}^{(n)})^{\beta(n-m+1)/2-1}\bigr\}
; yet this does not influence the validity of the following arguments.

Lastly the exponential tightness of
(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a simple consequence of
\supp(\nu_{n})\subset[0,1]
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and this concludes the proof of Proposition
6.28
.
